Chapter 10 Working with Children, Older Adults and Special Settings

In this chapter
  1. 10.1 Children as Clients: explain how working with children differs from working with adults, from referral and consent to assessment, and what a developmental view adds.
  2. 10.2 Play Therapy: compare the psychoanalytic, non-directive, filial, sandplay and other forms of play therapy and the people behind them.
  3. 10.3 Cognitive Behavioural, Parent and School Interventions: describe CBT for children, how parent training and school-based programmes are used, and what school mental health involves.
  4. 10.4 Treating Common Childhood and Adolescent Problems: outline the treatment of elimination problems, anxiety and school refusal, ADHD, conduct problems, adolescent depression and self-harm.
  5. 10.5 Autism, Intellectual Disability and Learning Disability: compare the main interventions for autism, explain training and support in intellectual disability, and describe how specific learning disabilities are remediated.
  6. 10.6 Child Abuse, Child Protection and Adolescents: describe the forms of child abuse and neglect, the clinician's duties under the POCSO Act 2012, and how to engage adolescents.
  7. 10.7 Working with Older Adults: describe geropsychology, dementia care and caregiver support, reminiscence, validation and cognitive stimulation, late-life depression and the assessment of capacity.
  8. 10.8 Counselling: distinguish counselling from psychotherapy and guidance, describe the counselling process and skills, its main areas, and the theories of career counselling.
  9. 10.9 Forensic Clinical Psychology: explain the clinician's roles in the legal system, from fitness to stand trial and the insanity defence to risk assessment, custody evaluation, juvenile justice and correctional work.
  10. 10.10 Creative, Adjunct and Digital Therapies: describe art, music, dance and movement, drama, bibliotherapy, animal-assisted and yoga-based therapies, and the promise and ethics of digital mental health.

Weightage. 24 of the 422 questions mapped in this book (5.7%) test this chapter, 19 of them from real past papers. 1 of its 10 sections is High yield.

The boy was called Dibs, and he was five years old. For almost two years he had attended a small private school on the Upper East Side of New York, and in all that time his teachers had never managed a conversation with him. Each morning he stood whimpering at the classroom door until someone led him in. Then he crept slowly around the edges of the room, keeping away from the other children; he hid, rocked himself, sucked his thumb, or lay rigid on the floor. When it was time to go home he crouched in a corner and hit out at anyone who tried to put on his hat and coat, and on some days his mother sent the family chauffeur, who simply carried him out to the car. His teachers could not decide what was wrong with him. Some days they thought he was mentally retarded, as the phrase then went. On other days they suspected that he was unusually bright, and one of them was half sure that he could read. They wondered whether he was autistic, or only very unhappy. The school psychologist and the paediatrician had each tried to test him, and he had refused to cooperate with either. The staff were close to asking his parents to take him away. Before they did, they invited a clinical psychologist, Virginia Axline, to come and observe him.

Dibs came from a wealthy and highly educated family, and his parents had already reached their own verdict: he was probably mentally defective, and nothing much could be done. His mother, whom the teachers found cold and difficult, agreed to bring him to Axline's playroom once a week. Only later, in her own conversations with Axline, did the rest of the story come out. The pregnancy had never been planned. When the child seemed backward, the parents had been ashamed of him and had cut him off from the world. A neurologist had found nothing wrong with him. A psychiatrist had told them that the problem was theirs, that Dibs was an emotionally deprived child, which they could not bear to hear. And his mother, desperate to prove that he could learn, had tested and drilled him constantly without ever quite getting close to him.

In the playroom Axline did something that nobody in Dibs's life had done: she let him lead. She did not quiz him, ask him to perform or tell him what to play. She watched, she put into words the feelings that his play seemed to carry, and she waited. Week by week Dibs began to talk, and the playroom filled with his inner life. He locked the doll family inside the dollhouse, then staged a fire from which the little boy doll rescued them. He sucked on a baby's feeding bottle and asked for help with his hat and shoes, as if trying out what it would be like to be little and looked after; then he read hard words aloud, painted with real skill and mended broken toys. His teachers reported that he was changing at school too. A week after the sessions ended, Dibs was given the Stanford-Binet intelligence test. His IQ was 168, far into the gifted range, and his reading was years ahead of his age. Axline told the story in Dibs in Search of Self (1964). Years later, she wrote, a friend who taught at a school for gifted boys showed her a letter that Dibs, by then fifteen, had written for the school newspaper in defence of a friend.

Notice three things about Dibs, because they are true of almost every child a clinical psychologist meets. He did not refer himself: the adults around him decided that something was wrong, disagreed about what it was, and brought him. He could not explain his trouble in words; he showed it, and worked it through, in play. And his difficulty did not live inside him alone. It lived in a family and in a school, and the adults who referred him were part of the problem as well as part of the solution. This chapter is about clinical work with people who do not fit the picture of the standard adult client who walks in alone, describes a problem and asks for help: children and adolescents, older adults with failing memories and the exhausted relatives who care for them, people who seek counselling for the ordinary crises of school, work and marriage, people caught up in the legal system, and people helped through art, music, movement or a screen. Remember Dibs; you will return to him at the end of the chapter.

10.1 Children as ClientsMedium yield · 3 Qs

Clinical psychology was born with a child client. The first psychological clinic, which Lightner Witmer opened at the University of Pennsylvania in 1896, saw children who were struggling at school (Section 1.4). Yet for most of the twentieth century the talking therapies were designed for adults and then cut down to child size, as if a child were simply a small adult with a small vocabulary. The modern view is that working with a child is a different job, not a smaller one. It begins with a different kind of referral, uses different methods of assessment, needs different arrangements for consent and confidentiality, and rests on a different kind of knowledge: knowledge of how children normally develop. Child clinical psychology (also called clinical child and adolescent psychology) is the branch of clinical psychology that assesses, treats and tries to prevent psychological problems in children and adolescents, always in the context of their development, their families and their schools. This section takes its distinctive features one at a time and ends with how child mental health care is organised in India.

Why a child is not a small adult

Start with the most obvious difference. Adults usually refer themselves, or at least agree to come. Children almost never do. A child arrives because a parent, a teacher, a paediatrician or, occasionally, a court has decided that something is wrong. This has three consequences. First, the reason for referral tells you about the referrer as well as about the child. The same restless eight-year-old may be "naughty" to a strict teacher with forty-five pupils, "just a boy" to an indulgent grandmother and "hyperactive" to a parent who has been reading about attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) on the internet. Second, children are referred mostly for problems that bother adults. Disruptive, aggressive, defiant and overactive behaviour, which clinicians call externalising problems because the trouble is directed outwards at other people, gets a child noticed quickly. Anxiety, sadness, worry and withdrawal, the internalising problems, are directed inwards; they disturb nobody else and are easily missed, so the quiet, frightened child in the back row is under-referred while the child who throws chalk is referred at once. (This is one reason why boys outnumber girls in child clinics before puberty.) Third, the child may not believe there is any problem at all, or may believe that the problem is that the adults are angry with him. Motivation for change, which adult therapies largely take for granted, has to be built.

The second difference is that what counts as a problem depends on age. A three-year-old who wets the bed, is terrified of the dark, throws tantrums when frustrated and talks to an imaginary friend is behaving quite normally; a ten-year-old who does all four is not. Children's fears follow a predictable sequence (strangers and separation in infancy; animals, the dark and imaginary creatures in the preschool years; injury, school and the judgement of other children later), and most of them fade without any treatment at all. So the first question a child clinician asks is not "Is this behaviour abnormal?" but "Is it abnormal for a child of this age and developmental level, in this family and this culture?" Answering it needs a working knowledge of normal development, which is taught in the Developmental book. It also needs humility, because development is a moving target: a problem may disappear, change its form or grow worse as the child moves into a new phase of life.

Third, children have limited means of telling you what is wrong. A young child may have few words for feelings beyond "good", "bad" and "angry", may not be able to say when something started, and cannot step back and reflect on her own thoughts in the way that adult talking therapies require; in Jean Piaget's terms, a child below about seven is still in the preoperational stage, thinking concretely and from her own point of view. Children are also suggestible. They tend to give the answer they think the adult wants, especially in reply to a leading question, and they may agree to things they do not understand simply to please. This is why child clinicians work through play, drawing, puppets and stories, why they speak in short, concrete sentences, and why cognitive behavioural therapy for children has to be made visual and practical (Section 10.3).

Fourth, a child lives inside systems that he cannot change. An adult who is miserable at work can look for another job; a child who is bullied at school or frightened at home cannot move out. His behaviour is shaped from hour to hour by the people around him, so the most powerful interventions often work through those people: parents are trained (Section 4.10 and Section 10.3), teachers adjust their classrooms, and sometimes the whole family is seen together (Chapter 7). Many child clinicians spend more time with the adults than with the child. The parents' own difficulties, a mother's depression, a father's drinking, a marriage at war, are often part of the picture and must be noticed and addressed, even though the parents did not come to be treated. This raises an ethical question that adult work rarely meets: who exactly is the client? The usual answer is that the child is the client and the parents are partners in the work, but the clinician has to keep asking whose interests a particular decision serves.

Finally, there is a hopeful side to all this. Because children are still developing, help given early can change the direction of a life rather than repair damage already done. Treating a frightened, withdrawn five-year-old may prevent a depressed and isolated adolescence. That is why child work is so closely bound up with prevention (Section 9.6), and why the developmental view in the next subsection matters so much. Table 10.1 gathers the differences together.

Table 10.1 How the typical child client differs from the typical adult client.
FeatureTypical adult clientTypical child client
Who decides to seek helpThe clientParents, teachers, doctors or a court
Who supplies the informationMainly the clientParents, teachers, the child and the clinician's own observation
Main medium of therapyTalkPlay, drawing, activity and talk suited to the child's age
Whose behaviour is changedMainly the client'sOften the parents' and teachers' as well as the child's
Who agrees to treatmentThe client consentsA parent or guardian consents; the child assents
Yardstick for abnormalityAdult normsNorms for the child's age and developmental level

A case shows how these differences play out. Kunal is eight and in Class 3 at a crowded English-medium school in Pune. His class teacher writes in his school diary that he "does not listen, leaves his seat and hits other boys", and asks the parents to "get him checked". At the first appointment his mother says he is perfectly well behaved at home; his father thinks he is simply lazy and needs strictness; his grandmother, who looks after him in the afternoons, says he is a good boy who is bored at school. Kunal himself, asked why he has come, says, "Because Ma'am is angry with me." Who is right? Possibly all of them. Kunal's behaviour may really differ between a noisy classroom and a quiet home; each adult sees a different slice of it; each carries a different standard of how an eight-year-old ought to behave; and Kunal, like most children of his age, experiences the problem as other people's anger rather than as something about himself. Later in this section you will see how a clinician turns this tangle of accounts into an assessment.

Exam trap

Children are referred by others (parents, teachers, doctors, courts), and mostly for externalising problems that disturb adults; internalising problems such as anxiety and depression are under-referred. Reject options such as "children usually refer themselves" or "the child's own report is the most reliable source". And remember that abnormality in children is judged against age norms: bedwetting, tantrums and fear of the dark are normal at three and problems at ten.

A developmental view: developmental psychopathology

If age changes what counts as abnormal, the study of childhood problems needs a theory of development built into it. That theory is developmental psychopathology, a field that took shape in the 1970s and 1980s around one simple idea: normal and abnormal development are best studied together, because each throws light on the other. Its manifesto is usually taken to be a 1984 paper in Child Development, "The domain of developmental psychopathology", written by the American attachment researcher L. Alan Sroufe and the British child psychiatrist Michael Rutter (1933 to 2021). They argued that what sets the field apart from abnormal psychology, from psychiatry and even from clinical child psychology is its developmental focus: it asks how a pattern of adaptation at one age leads, through the child's dealings with a changing environment, to adaptation or maladaptation at the next. Developmental transformation, they wrote, is the rule, so the links between early adaptation and later disorder will seldom be simple or direct. With Dante Cicchetti and others, Sroufe and Rutter made the field one of the most productive in child mental health.

Developmental psychopathologists think in terms of pathways rather than fixed diseases. Two kinds of continuity matter clinically. In homotypic continuity the same problem persists in the same form over time, as when an anxious child becomes an anxious adult. In heterotypic continuity an underlying vulnerability shows itself in different forms at different ages: the irritable, defiant child may become a depressed adolescent; the shy, inhibited toddler may become a socially anxious teenager. Heterotypic continuity is one reason why today's childhood diagnosis predicts tomorrow's problems only imperfectly, and why a child clinician watches the whole trajectory rather than a single snapshot.

Two further principles, borrowed from general systems theory (where Ludwig von Bertalanffy had described the first of them; Section 7.1), were set out for the field by Dante Cicchetti and Fred Rogosch in 1996. Equifinality means that many different starting points can lead to the same outcome: one adolescent's depression may grow out of an inherited vulnerability, another's out of years of bullying, a third's out of a parent's constant harsh criticism. Multifinality means that one starting point can lead to many different outcomes: of a hundred children whose parents go through a bitter divorce, some become depressed, some become aggressive, and most, in time, adjust well. Figure 10.1 sets the two side by side. Together they explain why no single cause ever accounts for a childhood disorder, and why knowing that a child has been through a particular adversity never tells you, on its own, what will become of him.

What decides which path a child takes? Developmental psychopathology answers in the language of risk and protection. A risk factor (Section 2.7 defined the term) is anything that raises the probability of a poor outcome; a protective factor is anything that lowers it, especially when risk is present. The most important lesson about risk came from a single island.

Key study: Rutter's Isle of Wight studies (from 1964)

In 1964 and 1965 Michael Rutter, working with Jack Tizard and Kingsley Whitmore, set out to count, rather than guess, how many children had psychiatric problems. They screened the ten- and eleven-year-olds at school on the Isle of Wight, off the south coast of England, about two thousand children, using questionnaires completed by parents and by teachers (the Rutter parent and teacher scales were developed for the purpose), and then interviewed and tested the children who screened positive along with a random comparison group. About 7 percent had a clinically significant disorder, most often an emotional or a conduct disorder. Rates were several times higher among children with brain disorders such as epilepsy or cerebral palsy, and severe reading difficulties went hand in hand with antisocial behaviour. Strikingly, the children picked out by parents and those picked out by teachers overlapped far less than anyone expected, an early sign of the informant problem you will meet later in this section. When the same children were seen again at fourteen, inner misery turned out to be common but open alienation from parents was not, which undermined the old belief that adolescent turmoil is normal and necessary (Section 10.6 returns to adolescence). A comparison with an inner London borough found rates roughly twice as high in the city.

From the London and island families, Rutter (1979) drew his best-known conclusion. He took six family adversities: severe marital discord, low social status, overcrowding or a large family, a father's criminality, a mother's psychiatric disorder, and the child's admission into the care of the local authority. A child with only one of them was at no greater risk of disorder than a child with none; two together roughly quadrupled the risk; four or more raised it about tenfold. It is the accumulation of adversities, not any single one, that does the damage. The criticism is mainly one of generalisation: the island was a largely white, semi-rural community of the 1960s, and the diagnostic categories have since changed. The principle of cumulative risk has nonetheless been replicated many times.

If risk accumulates, so can protection. On the Hawaiian island of Kauai, Emmy Werner (1929 to 2017) and Ruth Smith followed all 698 children born on the island in 1955 for about forty years. Roughly a third of them were judged to be at high risk, having been exposed before the age of two to several adversities such as birth complications, chronic poverty, family discord, or a parent with little education or a mental illness. About two-thirds of these high-risk children went on to develop serious learning or behaviour problems in childhood or mental health problems, delinquency or early pregnancy in adolescence. But one-third grew into competent, confident and caring young adults, and many of the others recovered by mid-life. What protected them? An easy-going temperament; at least one close, stable bond with a caring adult, who was often not a parent but a grandparent, an aunt or an older sibling; and support from outside the family, from a teacher, a youth group or a religious community. Werner and Smith called their 1982 book Vulnerable but Invincible, but the word that stuck was resilience: positive adaptation in spite of significant adversity. Later researchers, among them Ann Masten, stressed that resilience is not a rare, heroic trait that some children are born with. In Masten's phrase it is "ordinary magic", arising from ordinary human resources (a caring relationship, a working brain, a chance to be good at something), and it is a process that can be strengthened, which is exactly what intervention tries to do.

The final element of the developmental view is that the child and the environment shape each other. In 1975 Arnold Sameroff and Michael Chandler proposed a transactional model of development: outcomes are produced not by a fixed child or a fixed environment but by continuous, two-way exchanges between them over time. A premature, irritable baby makes an anxious young mother more anxious; her anxiety makes feeding tense, which makes the baby more irritable, which convinces the mother that she is failing, and so on. Neither the baby nor the mother "caused" the problem; the transaction did. Interest in early relationships as sources of risk goes back to René Spitz's descriptions of hospitalism (1945) and of anaclitic depression (1946), the weeping, withdrawal and physical decline of institutionalised infants who had been separated from their mothers in the second half of the first year (Section 3.9), and to John Bowlby's attachment theory (Section 3.5). Secure attachment is now one of the best-studied protective factors, and the disorganised attachment linked to maltreatment one of the best-studied risks; the details belong to the Developmental book.

Exam trap

Equifinality: different causes, same outcome. Multifinality: same cause, different outcomes. They are easy to swap, so anchor them to the word endings: equi-final, the "final" outcome is equal. Heterotypic continuity is the same vulnerability in a different form at a later age; homotypic continuity is the same problem in the same form. The founding statement of developmental psychopathology is Sroufe and Rutter (1984); the Isle of Wight studies and the cumulative-risk finding (one adversity adds little, four multiply risk about tenfold) are Rutter's; the Kauai resilience study is Werner and Smith's; "anaclitic depression" in separated infants is René Spitz's, not Piaget's, Skinner's or Eysenck's.

Temperament and goodness of fit

Any parent of two children will tell you that babies arrive different. One sleeps through the night by three months, smiles at strangers and takes every change in her stride; her brother wakes at unpredictable hours, screams at a new food and takes weeks to accept a new babysitter. These early, biologically rooted differences in emotional and behavioural style are called temperament. They are visible in the first months of life and fairly stable, though by no means fixed. For a clinician the important discovery was not that temperament exists but that it matters only in relation to the child's surroundings.

Key study: the New York Longitudinal Study (from 1956)

In 1956 two New York psychiatrists, the married couple Alexander Thomas and Stella Chess (1914 to 2007), working with Herbert Birch, began to follow more than 130 children from early infancy into adulthood. Instead of asking parents what they felt about their babies, they asked in detail what each baby actually did: how it reacted to its first bath, a new food, a stranger. They rated nine dimensions: activity level, rhythmicity (the regularity of sleeping, feeding and toileting), approach or withdrawal in response to new things, adaptability, threshold of responsiveness, intensity of reaction, quality of mood, distractibility, and attention span and persistence. Three clusters emerged. About 40 percent were easy children: regular, cheerful, quick to approach and adapt. About 10 percent were difficult children: irregular, withdrawing from new things, slow to adapt, intense and often negative in mood. About 15 percent were slow-to-warm-up children: low in activity, mildly negative and withdrawn at first meeting with something new, but able to adapt with repeated, unhurried exposure. The remaining third or so did not fit any cluster. Difficult children were more likely than others to develop behaviour problems later, but many did not. What made the difference, Thomas and Chess concluded, was how well the child's temperament matched what the environment demanded of it.

Thomas and Chess called this match goodness of fit, and they set the idea out fully in Temperament and Development (1977). Goodness of fit exists when the demands, expectations and opportunities of the child's environment suit the child's temperament and capacities; poorness of fit exists when they clash, and it is poorness of fit, not temperament as such, that produces stress, conflict and eventually behaviour problems. A highly active child does well in a family that takes him to the park every evening and badly in a small flat with a parent who needs silence to work. A slow-to-warm-up child flourishes with a teacher who lets her watch before joining in and struggles with a teacher who insists that every child perform on the first day. The idea fits neatly with Sameroff's transactional model: a poor fit sets off a cycle in which the child's reaction provokes the adult, and the adult's reaction makes the child worse.

Push a case through it. Tara is four and has just started lower kindergarten in Chennai. For three weeks she has cried every morning at the gate, clung to her mother and refused to speak in class. Her mother, a lively, sociable woman, is mortified; she has started pushing Tara forward at family gatherings ("Say hello to Aunty! Sing the rhyme!"), and the more she pushes, the more Tara hides. The class teacher has written that Tara "may have a problem". A careful history shows that Tara has always been like this with new things: she took a month to accept her grandmother's house and now loves it. She is a classic slow-to-warm-up child, and what is going wrong is the fit. The clinician's work is with the adults. The mother learns to stop prompting and praising performance, to give Tara advance warning of new situations and to let her watch from her lap first; the teacher agrees to let Tara arrive ten minutes early to settle in, to give her a small, predictable job and not to call on her to speak in front of the class until she volunteers. By the end of the term Tara is chatting to two friends. Nobody changed her temperament. The adults changed the demands.

Clinical link

Goodness of fit is one of the most useful ideas a child clinician can give to parents, because it removes blame from both sides. The child is not "bad", "stubborn" or "abnormal", and the parents are not failures; the two simply do not yet fit, and fit can be improved. Explaining a child's temperament ("She needs time with new things; that is how she is built") often lowers a parent's anger more than any technique. It also guards against over-diagnosis: a slow-to-warm-up child is not a case of social anxiety disorder, and a highly active, intense child is not automatically a case of ADHD, though either may become one if the fit stays poor for long enough.

Exam trap

Goodness of fit (Thomas and Chess) is the match between a child's temperament and the demands and expectations of the environment, especially of parents and teachers. It is not the IQ difference between identical twins, the number of siblings in a family or the genetic concordance between parent and child (those are behaviour-genetic and family-structure ideas from the Developmental and Research books). The New York Longitudinal Study began in 1956; its three types are easy (about 40 percent), difficult (about 10 percent) and slow-to-warm-up (about 15 percent), with the rest unclassifiable. "Goodness of fit" in statistics, the match between observed data and a model, is a different idea entirely.

Assessing children: many informants, many settings

Because a child cannot give a full account of himself, and because his behaviour depends so much on where he is and who is with him, assessment in child clinical psychology is built on three multiplications: several informants (the child, both parents, grandparents or other carers, teachers), several methods (interview, observation, rating scales, tests, school and medical records) and several settings (home, school, clinic). This approach is called multi-informant assessment. Section 2.2 noted that the informants often disagree. How much they disagree, and what the disagreement means, was settled by one of the most cited papers in child psychology.

Key study: Achenbach, McConaughy and Howell (1987)

Thomas M. Achenbach, Stephanie McConaughy and Catherine Howell pooled the results of 119 studies that had compared different people's ratings of the same children's behavioural and emotional problems. Agreement between similar informants who see the child in the same kind of setting (two parents, two teachers, two mental health workers) was moderate, an average correlation of about 0.60. Agreement between different kinds of informant who see the child in different settings, such as a parent and a teacher, was low, about 0.28. Agreement between the child's own report and any adult's report was lower still, about 0.22 (Figure 10.2). Agreement was somewhat better for children aged six to eleven than for adolescents, and better for externalising than for internalising problems. The authors concluded that most of the disagreement is not simply error. Children really do behave differently in different situations, which they called situational specificity, so each informant contributes valid information that the others cannot supply, and no single informant can serve as the gold standard. The finding shaped Achenbach's own system of parallel forms for parents, teachers and young people (the Child Behavior Checklist, Teacher's Report Form and Youth Self-Report, described in the Assessment book). Its limitation is that a correlation tells you how much informants agree, not which of them is right in a particular case.

Why do the accounts differ so much? Five reasons are well established.

  • Behaviour really does vary with the setting. A classroom demands things a home does not (sitting still, listening, waiting for a turn among forty children), and a child may be anxious only at school or defiant only with one parent.
  • Each informant sees a different slice of the child's life. Parents see mornings, evenings, homework and sleep; teachers see learning, friendships and structured tasks; only the child has direct access to his own fears, sad thoughts and worries. Children are therefore often the best informants about internalising problems, and adults about externalising ones.
  • Informants use different yardsticks. A teacher compares a child with dozens of children of the same age; the parent of an only child has no comparison at all, and the parent of a placid older sister may find an ordinary, lively boy exhausting.
  • The informant's own state colours the report. An exhausted or depressed parent may see more problems (the "depression-distortion" idea, which has some, though disputed, research support); a parent who fears stigma may see fewer; a teacher who already dislikes a child may rate everything he does more harshly.
  • The child's developmental level limits self-report. Young children find it hard to judge how often or how long something happens, and adolescents may play problems down to avoid trouble or play them up to be taken seriously.

The modern view, argued by Andres De Los Reyes and Alan Kazdin in 2005, is that informant discrepancies are themselves information. If a boy's teacher reports severe inattention and his parents report none, that tells the clinician something about where and when the problem arises and what the two settings demand. Diagnostic systems build the same logic into their criteria: for a diagnosis of ADHD, for example, several symptoms must be present in two or more settings (the Disorders book gives the criteria).

Return to Kunal. The clinician asks his teacher to complete a teacher rating form and his mother a parent form, interviews Kunal with the help of drawings and a picture "feelings thermometer", and spends a morning in his classroom. The teacher's ratings are high for inattention and restlessness; the mother's are in the normal range. The classroom visit explains part of the gap. Kunal leaves his seat almost only during the long periods of copying from the blackboard, and he squints at the board from the fifth row. An eye test shows that he is short-sighted. At home his homework is done at the kitchen table, one to one, with his grandmother beside him and the book a few inches from his nose. Spectacles and a seat in the front row remove much of the problem. What remains, a genuine difficulty in keeping his attention on long, unbroken tasks, is managed with short blocks of work and a daily report card shared between school and home (Section 10.3). No informant was wrong. Each had seen a true but partial picture, and the clinician's job was to put the pieces together, and to rule out the simple physical explanation first.

Talking with children needs its own skills. Section 2.2 gave the basics (play, drawing, short concrete questions, no leading questions); three further points matter. The first is order. With young children the clinician usually meets the parents first, sometimes while the child plays in the room, and then sees the child; adolescents are better seen on their own early in the first meeting, to show them that their own view counts (Section 10.6). The second is tailoring to age. Preschool children are best engaged through play with puppets, dolls and crayons; children of primary-school age can answer concrete questions, rate their feelings on a picture scale, finish sentences or say what they would ask for if they had three wishes; adolescents can be interviewed much as adults are, with more care over privacy. The third is structure. Research and specialist clinics use structured and semi-structured diagnostic interviews with parallel child and parent versions, such as the Kiddie Schedule for Affective Disorders and Schizophrenia (K-SADS), which are described in the Assessment book.

Observation fills the gaps that words leave. It begins in the waiting room: how does the child separate from the parent, how does the parent handle a restless child, does the child explore the room or cling? It continues in the clinic, where a parent and child may be asked to play together, or to tackle a frustrating puzzle, while the clinician watches; and wherever possible it goes to the classroom or the playground, where the behaviour that caused the referral actually happens. The methods of naturalistic and analogue observation, and the functional analysis that links a behaviour to its antecedents and consequences, are taught in Section 2.8.

With young children, and with children whose language is limited, play itself becomes an assessment tool. In play-based assessment the clinician watches a child play, alone, with a parent or with the clinician, and judges from it the child's cognitive, language, social and emotional development. Developmental level shows in the kind of play: simple practice play with objects, pretend play in which a block becomes a telephone, or games with rules. Social level shows in whether the child plays alone, plays beside another child without interacting (the parallel play that Mildred Parten described in 1932) or plays together with others. Emotional themes show in what is played out: repeated crashes, rescues, punishments, abandoned babies or hiding. And the child's attention, persistence, tolerance of frustration and response to limits show in how the play unfolds. Play-based assessment is not a test. Its observations are hypotheses, to be checked against the history, the other informants and standardised measures.

Those standardised measures complete the picture: broad rating scales completed by parents, teachers and older children (the Achenbach forms, or Robert Goodman's Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire), narrower scales for particular problems such as ADHD, developmental screening tools for infants and preschool children (the Denver Developmental Screening Test, the Indian adaptation of the Vineland Social Maturity Scale, the Trivandrum Developmental Screening Chart), intelligence and educational tests, and the autism instruments, including the Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism discussed in Section 9.9. All of these belong to the Assessment book. What matters here is the principle behind them: no single instrument, informant or setting is ever enough.

Exam trap

"When diagnosing developmental disorders, multi-informant assessment is recommended because..." The answer is that symptoms may vary across contexts (JEMAS 2025). "Parents tend to over-report problems" cannot be the reason, because parents over-report some problems and under-report others (especially internalising ones). "Teachers provide no relevant information" is false; teachers are often the best judges of attention, learning and peer behaviour. "Clinical observation alone is not sufficient" is true, but it is a consequence of the real reason: the clinic is just one more context, and a child may behave quite differently in a strange room with a stranger. Keep the numbers: about 0.60 between similar informants, 0.28 between different informants and 0.22 between the child and adults (Achenbach, McConaughy and Howell, 1987).

Consent, assent and confidentiality with children

Section 1.8 sets out the general principles of informed consent and confidentiality. With children they take a special shape, because a child cannot legally consent to treatment. In India a person becomes a legal adult at 18, the age of majority under the Indian Majority Act of 1875, so for anyone younger the legally valid consent is given by a parent or legal guardian. That does not make the child's own view irrelevant. The ethical standard is to seek the child's assent as well: agreement given by a person who cannot legally consent, after an explanation pitched at his or her level of understanding. The ethics code of the American Psychological Association puts it plainly. For persons who are legally incapable of giving informed consent, psychologists nevertheless provide an appropriate explanation, seek the individual's assent, consider his or her preferences and best interests, and obtain permission from a legally authorised person (Standard 3.10(b)). Behind this lies the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child (1989), which India ratified in 1992. Its Article 12 gives every child who is capable of forming views the right to express them freely in all matters affecting the child, with those views given due weight according to the child's age and maturity.

In practice this means three things. The clinician explains to the child, in words he can follow, who she is, what will happen ("We will play and talk; I will also meet your parents and your teacher") and what it is all for. The child's dissent is taken seriously: an unwilling child is not dragged through an assessment unless something urgent is at stake, and his reasons for refusing are themselves worth exploring. And the clinician checks who actually holds legal authority, because after a separation or divorce, in foster care, or when grandparents are raising a child while the parents work in another city, the adult who brings the child may not be the adult entitled to consent.

Confidentiality is harder still. Parents have a legitimate right to know how their child is doing. Children, especially older children, need to know that what they say in the playroom will not be repeated word for word at the dinner table, or they will say nothing worth hearing. The usual solution is to agree the ground rules openly at the first meeting, with parents and child together. Parents will be told about general progress and about what they can do to help, but not the details of what the child says or plays unless the child agrees; and everyone is told in advance that the clinician must break confidence if the child is at risk of serious harm, from himself or from someone else. That last limit is not optional. Under the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act 2012, anyone who knows or suspects that a child has been sexually abused must report it to the police or the Special Juvenile Police Unit, and failing to report is itself an offence; Section 10.6 sets out what this means for a clinician, together with the special problems of confidentiality with adolescents, who may be sexually active, using substances or questioning their sexuality.

Clinical link

Here is one way to explain confidentiality to a nine-year-old, with her parents in the room: "This is a special room. What you say and play here stays between us, and I won't tell your mum and dad what you said unless you want me to. I will tell them in general how things are going, and we can decide together what to share. There is one exception. If I am worried that someone is hurting you, or that you might get badly hurt, I will have to tell the people whose job is to keep you safe, and I will tell you first." Said at the start, this prevents a far worse moment later, when a child discovers after a disclosure that the promise of secrecy was never real.

Exam trap

Consent is the legally valid agreement of the person entitled to give it: for a minor in India, a parent or guardian (the age of majority is 18). Assent is the child's own agreement after an age-appropriate explanation; it is ethically required but not legally sufficient. A child's confidentiality is not absolute: risk of serious harm and suspected sexual abuse override it, and reporting suspected sexual abuse is mandatory under the POCSO Act 2012 (Section 10.6). Never promise a child unconditional secrecy.

Child mental health care in India

The idea of a clinic for troubled children came to India from the child guidance movement, which began in the United States and Britain in the 1920s (Section 1.4) with its team of psychiatrist, psychologist and psychiatric social worker (Chapter 7 tells how that team helped give birth to family therapy), and which owed much to Alfred Adler's child guidance clinics in Vienna (Section 3.8). India's first child guidance clinic opened in 1937 at the Sir Dorabji Tata Graduate School of Social Work in Bombay, which had been founded the year before and was renamed the Tata Institute of Social Sciences (TISS) in 1944. The clinic still runs, now as Muskaan, a child and adolescent guidance centre. In the following decades child guidance clinics opened in general hospitals, medical colleges and mental health institutes, and the National Institute of Mental Health and Neurosciences (NIMHANS) in Bengaluru built one of the country's largest child and adolescent psychiatry services and a major training centre for child work.

The need is enormous. At the 2011 Census about three in every ten Indians were under fifteen. The National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016, carried out by NIMHANS, included adolescents and found that about 7.3 percent of those aged 13 to 17 had a current mental disorder, at nearly equal rates in boys and girls (the survey's adult figures and the treatment gap are given in Section 1.10). Very few of these young people ever see a mental health professional. Specialists are scarce and concentrated in cities; families usually turn first to paediatricians, teachers, relatives or religious healers; and when a referral does come, it is very often about falling marks rather than about feelings.

The public health system's main tool for finding children early is the Rashtriya Bal Swasthya Karyakram (RBSK, the National Child Health Programme), launched in 2013 under the National Health Mission. It aims to screen children from birth to 18 years for the "four Ds": defects at birth, diseases, deficiencies, and developmental delays including disability. Mobile health teams visit anganwadis and government schools, and children who screen positive are referred to District Early Intervention Centres, whose teams include a psychologist and a special educator. The conditions screened include developmental delay, autism, learning disorders and ADHD, which makes the programme an important doorway into child clinical services. The rest of the Indian framework appears elsewhere in this book: school mental health and adolescent health programmes in Section 10.3, the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 and the National Trust Act 1999 in Section 1.10, and disability certification in Section 9.9.

Indian context

A large share of child referrals in Indian clinics are about school: poor marks, "not concentrating", refusing to study, fear of examinations. Behind such a referral may lie a specific learning disability, ADHD, anxiety, depression, a vision or hearing problem, a gap between the child's ability and the expectations of the school or family, or trouble at home, so "poor marks" is a starting point for assessment, never a diagnosis. Decisions about a child are often made by the whole family; grandparents may need to be part of the plan, and a father who cannot leave work may need a phone call. Families may expect a single visit that ends with a tablet or a stern talk to the child, and explaining why several sessions, work with the parents and contact with the school are needed is part of the clinician's job. Stigma makes many parents reluctant to tell the school, and many schools have no counsellor. Language matters too: a child who thinks and dreams in Tamil or Marathi should not have to perform in English to be understood.

Exam trap

India's first child guidance clinic: 1937, at the Sir Dorabji Tata Graduate School of Social Work in Bombay (now TISS, Mumbai). RBSK (2013) screens children from birth to 18 for the four Ds: defects at birth, diseases, deficiencies and developmental delays including disability. Do not confuse it with the RKSK (Rashtriya Kishor Swasthya Karyakram), the adolescent health programme (Section 10.3). The National Mental Health Survey 2015 to 2016 found a current prevalence of about 7.3 percent among 13 to 17 year olds.

Dibs could never have been assessed by questionnaire or interview alone. He did not answer questions; he did not even look at the people who asked them. What finally let him show what was wrong, and begin to put it right, was a room full of toys and an adult who knew how to watch. That method is the subject of the next section.

10.2 Play TherapyLower yield · 1 Q

Leave a four-year-old alone for ten minutes with a box of small toys and watch. Within a few minutes the toys have become a world. The lorry crashes; the father doll shouts; the baby is put to bed, tenderly, and then, quite suddenly, thrown out of it. Adults talk out their troubles; children play them out. Play therapy is a form of psychotherapy in which a trained therapist uses play, the child's natural medium of communication, within a therapeutic relationship, to help a child (usually aged about three to twelve) express feelings, make sense of experiences and resolve psychological difficulties. It is not one therapy but a family of them, as different from one another as psychoanalysis is from behaviour therapy, and the question that divides them is always the same: who leads, the child or the therapist, and what does the therapist do with what the child plays? This section first asks why play should be therapeutic at all, then follows the family from its psychoanalytic beginnings, through the non-directive method that reached Dibs, to the filial, sandplay, structured and integrative therapies of today, and ends with what the evidence shows.

Why play? Piaget, Vygotsky and the healing powers of play

The Swiss psychologist Jean Piaget (1896 to 1980) gave the first systematic account of children's play in Play, Dreams and Imitation in Childhood (published in French in 1945). For Piaget, play is assimilation in its purest form: in play the child bends reality to fit what he already knows and wants, instead of adapting himself to reality. He described a developmental sequence. Practice play comes first, in infancy, when a baby shakes a rattle again and again for the sheer pleasure of mastery. Symbolic play (pretend play) follows from about two to seven years: one thing stands for another, so that a stick becomes a horse and a doll becomes a baby brother. Games with rules dominate from about seven onwards. Symbolic play is the raw material of play therapy. A child who cannot say "I hate the new baby and I am afraid Amma loves him more" can make a doll baby fall off the table, and the feeling is now out in the room, where it can be seen, accepted and worked on. Piaget's stages themselves belong to the Developmental book.

The Russian psychologist Lev Vygotsky (1896 to 1934) saw something else in play. In a 1933 lecture on play and its role in the mental development of the child, he argued that play creates a zone of proximal development: "in play a child always behaves beyond his average age, above his daily behaviour; in play it is as though he were a head taller than himself." The zone of proximal development (ZPD) is the gap between what a child can do alone and what he can do with the guidance and encouragement of a more skilled partner: the range of tasks that are too difficult to master alone but can be accomplished with help. A four-year-old who cannot wait for anything will wait patiently for his turn when he is playing shopkeeper, because the role demands it. Later writers, David Wood, Jerome Bruner and Gail Ross (1976), called the adult's adjustable, gradually withdrawn support scaffolding. In play therapy the therapist is often that more skilled partner, who by reflecting, structuring or simply staying present lets a child do emotionally what he cannot yet do alone.

Clinicians who agree on little else agree that play does at least five things in therapy.

  • Communication. For a young child, in Garry Landreth's phrase, toys are words and play is language. The play therapist learns to "listen" to play as an adult therapist listens to speech.
  • Emotional release. Feelings that cannot be spoken can be acted out on a punching toy or in a battle of toy soldiers. Psychoanalysts called the reliving abreaction and the relief catharsis (Section 3.1).
  • Mastery. Children repeat in play what overwhelmed them in life, turning something that happened to them into something they make happen. Sigmund Freud described his eighteen-month-old grandson throwing a cotton reel on a string out of his cot with a long "o-o-o-o" (fort, "gone") and pulling it back with a joyful "da" ("there"), staging his mother's departures and returns so that he controlled them (Beyond the Pleasure Principle, 1920). The child who has just had an injection gives injections to her dolls.
  • Safe distance. Because it is "only play", and because it happens to a doll rather than to me, a child can approach frightening material at a distance that he controls, and retreat when it becomes too much.
  • Relationship and rehearsal. Play builds the relationship with the therapist, and it gives the child a safe place to try out new ways of behaving: asking, waiting, losing a game, being angry without hurting anyone.
Exam trap

The zone of proximal development (Vygotsky) is the range of tasks too complex to master alone but achievable with guidance from a more skilful partner. It is not the range of tasks too simple to need help (that is what the child can already do alone), nor tasks too complex even with help (those lie beyond the zone), nor "learning through experience rather than instruction". Scaffolding is Wood, Bruner and Ross's word (1976), not Vygotsky's own. Symbolic play (about two to seven years) is Piaget's term; the "fort-da" game is Freud's account of play as mastery.

Psychoanalytic play therapy: Hug-Hellmuth, Anna Freud and Melanie Klein

Psychoanalysis met children early, but at second hand. Freud's famous case of "Little Hans" (1909), a five-year-old with a fear of horses, was conducted almost entirely through the boy's father, who reported Hans's words and games to Freud; Freud saw the boy himself only once. The first analyst to work directly with children, and to make their play part of the method, was the Viennese analyst Hermine Hug-Hellmuth (1871 to 1924). In a paper of 1920 on the technique of child analysis she argued that young children cannot free-associate as adults do, that the analyst should meet the child through the child's own activities, and that play, drawing and games open a way into the child's unconscious. She is generally regarded as the first child psychoanalyst. Her life ended horribly. In 1924 she was murdered in her flat by her nephew Rolf, whom she had brought up after his mother's death, and who later claimed that her psychoanalytic experiments on him had ruined his life. The scandal, together with doubts about the authenticity of a girl's diary that she had edited, made her a figure the movement preferred to forget, and the history of child analysis is usually told from the two women who came next.

Section 3.4 introduced Anna Freud (1895 to 1982), Sigmund Freud's youngest daughter, and her approach to child analysis. Here the question is what she actually did with a child in the room. In lectures given in Vienna in 1926 and published as Introduction to the Technique of Child Analysis (1927), she started from the fact that a child, unlike an adult patient, does not come to treatment by choice, does not believe he is ill and has no wish to be cured. So analysis must begin with a preparatory phase (sometimes called the introductory phase), in which the analyst sets out to make herself interesting, useful and trusted, and to win the child's attachment, before any interpretation is attempted. Play mattered to her chiefly as a way of building that relationship and of observing the child, but she doubted that every act in play carries a hidden symbolic meaning. If a child knocks over a toy figure, she objected, it may express aggression towards his father, but it may equally reproduce something he saw in the street the day before. She relied as much on the child's talk, dreams, daydreams and drawings, and on regular information from the parents, as on play. And because the child's parents are still present in his life, she argued, the child does not form a full transference neurosis, and the analyst must be an educator as well as an interpreter, strengthening the child's ego and helping the parents to support it.

Melanie Klein (1882 to 1960) disagreed with almost every one of these conclusions. She had begun analysing children in 1919, the first of them her own son, and in Berlin in the early 1920s she developed her play technique. Each child had a drawer or box of his own containing small, simple toys (little wooden men and women, carts, cars, trains, animals, bricks and houses), with paper, pencils, scissors, string and water, in a plain room that could be messed up without harm. Klein treated the child's play as the exact equivalent of an adult's free associations, so that everything the child did with the toys could be understood and interpreted as an expression of unconscious phantasy and anxiety. She interpreted from the very first session, directly and deeply, including sexual and aggressive meanings and the child's hostility towards the analyst (the negative transference), because she believed that well-timed interpretation relieves anxiety and makes the child more, not less, able to go on. When a little boy she called Peter, aged three and three-quarters, kept knocking toy carriages and horses together in his first two sessions, she told him that they were his Daddy and Mummy bumping their "thingummies" (his word for genitals) together, and that he thought this was how his little brother had come to be born. Children, in her view, form transferences as fully as adults; there was no need for a preparatory phase, no place for education, and little need for the parents in the analysis itself. She analysed children younger than anyone had before, among them Rita, aged two years and nine months, and set out her method in The Psycho-Analysis of Children (1932).

The quarrel was public almost at once. Anna Freud's book provoked a symposium on child analysis at the British Psychoanalytical Society in London in 1927, at which Klein and her supporters attacked it point by point, and the rivalry ran on until the Controversial Discussions of 1942 to 1944 divided British psychoanalysis into Kleinian, Anna Freudian and Independent groups (Section 3.4). Table 10.2 sets the two positions side by side.

Table 10.2 Anna Freud and Melanie Klein on child analysis.
QuestionAnna FreudMelanie Klein
Key textIntroduction to the Technique of Child Analysis (1927)The Psycho-Analysis of Children (1932)
How therapy beginsA preparatory phase to win the child's trustInterpretation from the first session
What play meansA way to build the relationship and observe; not every act is symbolicThe equivalent of free association; all of it can be interpreted
InterpretationCautious, later, often of the ego's defencesDirect and deep, including sexual and aggressive phantasy
TransferencePartial; no full transference neurosis while the parents are presentFull, as in adults, including the negative transference
ParentsActively involved as informants and alliesKept largely outside the analysis
Analyst's roleAnalyst and educatorAnalyst only; no educational influence
Institutional legacyHampstead Child Therapy Clinic, now the Anna Freud CentreKleinian child psychotherapy and object relations theory

Most psychodynamic child therapists today take something from both. They interpret more cautiously and more gradually than Klein, and they work with parents as Anna Freud did; but, like Klein, they treat play as meaningful communication, attend to what the child brings into the relationship with the therapist, and use interpretation to help the child understand feelings that he could not otherwise put into words. Donald Winnicott's squiggle game, in which therapist and child take turns to turn each other's scribbles into pictures (Section 3.4), belongs to the same tradition.

Exam trap

The first analyst to use play in the analysis of children was Hermine Hug-Hellmuth (paper of 1920). Play as the equivalent of free association, deep interpretation from the first session and full transference in children are Melanie Klein's. The preparatory (introductory) phase, work with parents, an educational role and "no full transference neurosis" are Anna Freud's. Little Hans (1909) was treated by Freud through his father, not through play therapy. The squiggle game is Winnicott's; sandplay is Dora Kalff's and is Jungian, not Kleinian.

Release, structured and relationship play therapies

The first variants of play therapy to break away from full analysis appeared in America in the 1930s. In 1938 the New York child psychiatrist David M. Levy (1892 to 1977), remembered also for his studies of maternal overprotection, described release therapy, a structured, short-term method for young children, mostly under about ten, who had suffered a specific and recent frightening experience: a dog bite, an operation, a road accident. After a little free play the therapist set out toys that recreated the event (a toy dog, a doll in a hospital bed, two toy cars) and let the child play it out, again and again. Levy believed the repetition itself was curative. The child released the pent-up fear and anger, and, by controlling the scene this time, mastered it. The therapist interpreted little; the play did the work. Levy chose his cases carefully: the method suited a recent, clearly defined trauma in a child from a reasonably secure family, not long-standing problems or troubled family relationships. In 1955 Gove Hambidge extended the idea into structured play therapy, in which the therapist, once rapport is established, deliberately sets up a scene from the child's life that is causing difficulty (the arrival of a new baby, a quarrel between the parents), lets the child play it through, and then returns to free play so that the child can recover before the session ends. Today's trauma-focused play methods, and the trauma narrative of trauma-focused CBT (Section 10.3), are their descendants.

A second line came from Otto Rank, the member of Freud's circle who broke away to stress the patient's own will and the creative power of the present relationship (Section 3.6). His American followers, the social worker Jessie Taft (The Dynamics of Therapy in a Controlled Relationship, 1933) and the psychiatrist Frederick H. Allen, director of the Philadelphia Child Guidance Clinic (Psychotherapy with Children, 1942), developed relationship therapy. Its claims were new. What heals is not insight into the past but the child's experience of a real relationship with the therapist in the present. The child has within himself the capacity to grow, to separate and to take responsibility for himself, and the therapist's job is to provide a relationship in which he can discover that capacity, including the experience of ending, which Rank's followers treated as therapeutic in itself. Through Taft these ideas reached Carl Rogers in Rochester (Section 6.2), and through Rogers they reached Virginia Axline.

The humanistic psychologist Clark Moustakas (1923 to 2012), later known also as the founder of heuristic research, carried relationship therapy into play therapy proper. In Children in Play Therapy (1953), Psychotherapy with Children: The Living Relationship (1959) and, much later, Relationship Play Therapy (1997), he described a therapy in which the therapist's genuine presence, faith in the child and acceptance of his feelings matter more than any technique. In a 1955 paper on emotional adjustment and the play therapy process he described how children's feelings typically change over the course of successful play therapy. They begin as diffuse negative feelings, expressed everywhere and at everything; become ambivalent, often anxious or hostile; then become negative feelings focused more directly on particular people, such as parents, siblings or the therapist; then mixed positive and negative feelings towards those same people; and finally clearer, more realistic feelings in which positive attitudes predominate and negative ones are kept in proportion. The sequence is a useful map for a therapist who is tempted to stop when a child's anger at his parents first comes out: that is usually the middle of the journey, not the end.

Exam trap

Release therapy is David Levy's (1938): toys chosen by the therapist to let a child replay a specific, recent trauma. Structured play therapy is Gove Hambidge's (1955) extension of it. Relationship therapy comes from Otto Rank through Jessie Taft (1933) and Frederick Allen (1942) and centres on the present relationship, not the past. Clark Moustakas wrote Children in Play Therapy (1953) and described the stages through which a child's feelings move in play therapy, from diffuse negativity to realistic, mostly positive feelings. Do not credit any of these to Axline.

Virginia Axline and non-directive play therapy

Virginia Mae Axline (1911 to 1988) was born in Fort Wayne, Indiana, and taught in elementary schools for several years before going to graduate school at Ohio State University, where she began working with Carl Rogers just as he was developing non-directive therapy (Section 6.2). She took her doctorate in education at Teachers College, Columbia University, in 1950, and taught at the University of Chicago, Columbia and New York University before returning to Ohio State and, later, private practice. In Play Therapy (1947) she applied Rogers's approach to children and created non-directive play therapy: a method in which the child, not the therapist, leads, and the therapist provides a relationship of warmth, acceptance and permissiveness in which the child can play out his feelings and find his own solutions. Seventeen years later Dibs in Search of Self carried her name far outside psychology.

Axline's theory is Rogers's theory of personality applied to a child (Section 6.2). Every child has within him a powerful drive towards growth, maturity and self-direction. Problems arise when that drive is blocked: when the child's need to be accepted as he is goes unmet, and the gap grows between the self he is and the self that the adults around him will accept. In a permissive playroom, with an adult who accepts whatever he feels, the child plays out his feelings, bringing them to the surface, facing them, and learning to control them or let them go. "Play," Axline wrote, "is the child's natural medium of self-expression." From this follow her eight basic principles (Table 10.3), still the most examined list in child psychotherapy.

Table 10.3 Virginia Axline's eight basic principles of non-directive play therapy (Play Therapy, 1947).
No.The therapist...In the playroom this means
1develops a warm, friendly relationship with the child, in which good rapport is established as soon as possibleA friendly welcome; no quizzing, no rush to reach "the problem"
2accepts the child exactly as he isNo wish that the child were quieter, cleaner or more talkative; neither praise nor blame
3establishes a feeling of permissiveness in the relationship, so that the child feels free to express his feelings completelyThe child may shout, make a mess or say that he hates his sister
4is alert to recognise the feelings the child is expressing and reflects them back to him, so that he gains insight into his behaviour"You're really angry with that doll." Reflection, not interpretation
5maintains a deep respect for the child's ability to solve his own problems if given the opportunity; the responsibility to make choices and to change is the child's"You can decide." The therapist does not fix things for the child
6does not attempt to direct the child's actions or conversation in any manner; the child leads the way and the therapist followsNo suggestions about what to play, no leading questions
7does not attempt to hurry the therapy along; it is a gradual processWeeks of play that seem to go nowhere are allowed
8establishes only those limitations that are necessary to anchor the therapy to the world of reality and to make the child aware of his responsibility in the relationshipFew, firm limits: no hurting himself or the therapist, no wrecking the room, and the session ends on time

Notice above all the difference between principle 4 and what Klein did. Suppose a six-year-old hits the father doll with a toy hammer. A Kleinian analyst might interpret: "You are angry with your daddy because he goes into Mummy's room at night and leaves you out." A non-directive therapist reflects: "You're hitting him really hard. You're angry with him." The reflection names only what the child is showing, here and now; it adds no theory and no past, and it leaves the child free to go further or not. Axline believed that a child who feels understood at this level will go further on his own when he is ready, and Dibs was her best illustration. When he locked the doll family inside the house and staged a fire, the non-directive response was not to explain to him what the fire meant but to name what he was doing and what he seemed to feel, and to let him decide where the story went. In Dibs's story it went to a rescue, with the little boy doll saving the family.

The eighth principle is often misread. Non-directive does not mean that anything goes. Axline kept a small number of firm limits: the child may not hurt himself or the therapist, may not deliberately destroy the room or its equipment, and must leave when the time is up. The limits are few, stated calmly when they are needed rather than read out like rules at the start, and enforced without anger. They also do therapeutic work of their own. They make the room safe for strong feelings, they tie the fantasy world of play to the real world outside, and they let the child practise feeling something intensely without acting on it destructively.

Non-directive play therapy has obvious strengths. It is respectful, it suits young children who cannot or will not talk, it reaches children whom other approaches cannot reach, and it rarely does harm. Its weaknesses are just as plain. It is slow, by design (principle 7). It does not target specific symptoms, so a child with a phobia, bedwetting or severe defiance may play happily for months while the problem stays put; for those problems specific behavioural and cognitive treatments work faster (Sections 10.3 and 10.4). It depends heavily on the therapist's skill and on the child's capacity for symbolic play, which is limited in many children with autism or severe intellectual disability. Its early evidence was almost all case studies, of which Dibs is the most famous. And it asks parents to pay for, and wait through, a treatment in which their child "only plays", a problem that led directly to the idea of training the parents themselves.

Exam trap

Axline's eight principles are built on acceptance, permissiveness, reflection of feelings, respect for the child's own capacity, following the child's lead, not hurrying, and minimal limits. An option that says the therapist should interpret the child's unconscious motives, direct or suggest the play, praise good behaviour, speed up the process or set many rules at the outset is not one of them. Non-directive play therapy (Play Therapy, 1947) applies Carl Rogers's client-centred approach, not Klein's or Anna Freud's; Dibs in Search of Self (1964) is Axline's case study. "Play is the child's natural medium of self-expression" is Axline's line.

Child-centred play therapy today: Landreth, the playroom and limits

Axline's method was carried forward, given a training structure and renamed above all by the American counsellor educator Garry Landreth, who founded the Center for Play Therapy at the University of North Texas and whose Play Therapy: The Art of the Relationship (1991) is the standard text of child-centred play therapy (CCPT), the modern form of non-directive play therapy. Landreth summed up its rationale in a single sentence: toys are children's words and play is their language. CCPT keeps Axline's principles and spells out what the therapist actually says. Its main responses are these.

  • Tracking. The therapist describes what the child is doing ("You're putting that one right on top"), without naming a toy before the child has named it, since the "car" may be a spaceship. Tracking tells the child that he has the therapist's full attention.
  • Reflecting content. The therapist restates what the child says in her own words, so that the child knows he has been heard.
  • Reflecting feelings. The therapist names the feeling the child is expressing ("You're disappointed that the tower fell down"), giving the child words for it.
  • Returning responsibility. Decisions are handed back to the child ("In here, you can decide what colour it is") instead of being made or solved for him, which builds self-direction.
  • Encouragement rather than praise. "You worked really hard on that" recognises effort; "That's beautiful" is an adult's verdict that the child will then work to earn, which is exactly the conditional acceptance CCPT tries to avoid.
  • Limit setting. Limits are set only when needed, by the method described next.

Landreth's method of setting a limit is known as the ACT model: acknowledge the child's feeling, communicate the limit, and target an acceptable alternative. If the child keeps breaking the limit, the therapist moves to choice-giving: the child may choose to keep the limit, or choose to lose the toy, or the rest of that day's session, and either choice is respected and carried through calmly. The limit is stated in a neutral, impersonal form ("The walls are not for painting"), which separates the behaviour from the child and avoids a contest of wills.

Watch the model at work. Anaya is six and lives in Bengaluru. Since her baby brother was born and the family moved into a new flat four months ago she has become clingy, has started wetting the bed again now and then, and hits her mother whenever the baby is being fed. In her fourth session she spends twenty minutes feeding, bathing and then roughly spanking a baby doll while the therapist tracks and reflects: "The baby's getting his milk... Now he's getting a smack. You're really cross with that baby." Then Anaya picks up a dart gun and aims it at the therapist. "Anaya, you're really angry, and you'd like to shoot me" (acknowledge). "But I'm not for shooting" (communicate). "You can shoot the bop bag" (target). She hesitates, swings round and fires three darts into the inflatable punching toy, laughing. Nobody has explained the new baby to her. She has been allowed to have her feelings about him, in full, in a place where they cannot hurt anyone, and to find out that an adult can bear them. Over the following weeks the doll play becomes gentler, and at home the hitting stops first.

The playroom itself is part of the therapy. Landreth recommended a small room, about 12 by 15 feet (roughly 3.7 by 4.6 metres), large enough to move about in but small enough that the child cannot get far from the therapist; washable floors and walls, a sink, and low open shelves on which every toy is visible and within reach; and the same toys in the same places every week, so that the room is predictable. Toys are to be selected, not collected. Each should invite a particular kind of expression, and mechanical or electronic toys that do the playing for the child are left out. Landreth grouped them into three categories (Table 10.4).

Table 10.4 Landreth's three categories of play therapy toys.
CategoryExamplesWhat they invite
Real-life and nurturing toysDoll family, dollhouse, feeding bottle, kitchen set, toy money, cars and buses, toy telephonePlaying out family life, everyday events, care and being cared for
Acting-out and aggressive-release toysBop bag, toy soldiers, dart gun and rubber knife, handcuffs, wild animalsExpressing anger, fear and power safely; playing out conflict
Creative-expression and emotional-release toysSand, water, paints, clay, crayons, blocks, puppetsMaking, destroying and remaking; mastery and creativity
Exam trap

Child-centred play therapy is Garry Landreth's modern development of Axline's non-directive method; "toys are children's words and play is their language" is his. ACT stands for acknowledge the feeling, communicate the limit, target an alternative. CCPT uses encouragement rather than evaluative praise and returns responsibility to the child rather than solving problems for him. "Non-directive" never means "no limits".

Filial therapy: parents as therapists

If play therapy helps because a child feels accepted and understood by an adult, why should that adult be a stranger whom the child sees for forty-five minutes a week? In 1964 the American psychologist Bernard Guerney, working with his wife Louise Guerney, published "Filial therapy: description and rationale" in the Journal of Consulting Psychology, proposing that parents themselves be trained to hold child-centred play sessions with their own children. In filial therapy (from the Latin filius and filia, son and daughter) small groups of parents are taught the basic skills of child-centred play therapy (tracking, reflecting feelings, following the child's lead and setting limits). They practise first in supervised sessions in the clinic, often watched by the therapist and the rest of the group, and then hold regular half-hour play sessions at home, bringing their experiences back to the group for discussion. The rationale was partly practical, because there will never be enough play therapists, and partly theoretical: parents are the most important people in a child's life, so a change in the way a parent responds reaches the child every day rather than once a week, and it lasts after therapy ends.

Garry Landreth and Sue Bratton later condensed filial therapy into a ten-session group programme, child-parent relationship therapy (CPRT, 2006). Parents meet weekly in a group, learn one skill at a time, hold a 30-minute "special playtime" with one child at home each week, and record it so that the group can give feedback. When Anaya's mother joined such a group after Anaya's fourth session, she was startled to find how much of her usual talk with her daughter consisted of questions, instructions and corrections. In her first recorded special playtime she managed two reflections of feeling in thirty minutes; by the sixth she was reflecting and tracking steadily, and Anaya had begun to bring her anger about the baby to her mother instead of to the doll.

Filial therapy and parent-child interaction therapy (PCIT, Section 4.10) look alike from the outside, since both coach parents in child-led play, but they come from opposite traditions. PCIT's first phase, child-directed interaction with its PRIDE skills, borrows heavily from play therapy; but its second phase teaches clear commands and time-out to reduce disruptive behaviour, the therapist coaches live through an earpiece, and its target is conduct. Filial therapy stays humanistic throughout. Its goals are the parent-child relationship, the child's self-esteem and the parent's empathy and acceptance, and it teaches limit setting rather than a programme of discipline.

Exam trap

Filial therapy (Bernard and Louise Guerney, 1964) trains parents as the therapeutic agents, conducting child-centred play sessions with their own children. It is not family therapy (Chapter 7), not "filial piety" (a cultural value of duty to parents), not Theraplay and not PCIT. Its ten-session descendant, child-parent relationship therapy, is Landreth and Bratton's (2006). In Bratton's 2005 meta-analysis, play therapy delivered by trained parents produced the largest effects of all.

Sandplay and the World Technique

The sand tray, now one of the most familiar objects in any playroom, was introduced by a London paediatrician, Margaret Lowenfeld (1890 to 1973). In 1928 she opened a clinic in Notting Hill for "nervous and difficult" children, which by 1931 had become the Institute of Child Psychology.
