Lowenfeld wanted a way for children to show their thinking without having to put it into words, and she remembered the pleasure she had taken as a child in H. G. Wells's little book Floor Games (1911), in which Wells described building imaginary towns on the nursery floor with toys. In 1929 she gave her young patients a tray of sand, water and a cabinet of miniature objects (people, animals, houses, trees, fences, vehicles) and invited them to build whatever they liked. The method became known as the World Technique, after the "worlds" the children built. Lowenfeld deliberately avoided imposing any single theory on the worlds; she wanted the child's own meaning, expressed in what she believed was a non-verbal mode of thinking. Carl Jung saw the technique when it was presented at a British Psychological Society conference in 1937. Lowenfeld also devised the Lowenfeld Mosaic Test, which belongs to the Assessment book.

Dora Kalff (1904 to 1990), the Swiss Jungian whom Section 3.8 mentioned, was encouraged by Jung to study the World Technique, and in the 1950s she went to London to learn it from Lowenfeld. Back in Switzerland she joined it to Jungian psychology to create sandplay therapy, which she described in Sandspiel (1966; in English, Sandplay). With Lowenfeld's agreement the two methods kept separate names: sand tray or World Technique for Lowenfeld's, sandplay for Kalff's. Kalff used a tray of standard size, small enough to be taken in at a single glance and painted blue inside, so that digging down to the bottom suggests water; beside it stood dry and wet sand and shelves of miniatures. The therapist's main task is to provide what Kalff called a free and protected space: free, because the client may build anything at all; protected, because the therapist's accepting, mostly silent presence and the boundaries of the tray and the session make it safe to do so. Over a series of trays, each photographed at the end of the session, Kalff saw the psyche heal itself, moving from images of chaos and struggle towards images of order and wholeness, sometimes a mandala-like centre, which she read in Jung's terms as the emergence of the Self. Interpretation is usually held back while the work goes on; therapist and client may look through the photographs together much later. Sandplay is used with adults as well as with children.

Exam trap

The World Technique (1929) is Margaret Lowenfeld's, a London paediatrician inspired by H. G. Wells's Floor Games; it is not tied to any one theory. Sandplay therapy is Dora Kalff's, developed from the 1950s and set out in Sandspiel (1966); it is Jungian, and its key phrase is the "free and protected space". Do not credit sandplay to Klein, Axline or Jung himself, and do not confuse the Lowenfeld Mosaic Test (an assessment) with the World Technique (a therapy).

Theraplay and the structured and integrative play therapies

Not every play therapy leaves the child in charge. Theraplay was developed by the American psychologist Ann Jernberg (1928 to 1993), who in 1967 became clinical director of the mental health programme of Head Start in Chicago, the federal preschool programme for poor children. With thousands of children to serve and no money for professionals, she trained graduate students and Head Start mothers to deliver a short, active, playful treatment modelled on the interactions of a healthy parent and infant: peek-a-boo, rocking, feeding, singing, caring for small hurts, measuring the child, games of balance and catching. It is adult-led and physical rather than verbal or symbolic. Theraplay is organised around four dimensions of a healthy relationship: structure (the adult organises the session and keeps the child safe), engagement (the adult is attuned, playful and fully present), nurture (the adult soothes, comforts and cares) and challenge (the adult helps the child stretch towards new mastery). Parents watch the early sessions, often with a second therapist who explains what is happening, and then join in, so that the new way of relating is carried home. Theraplay is used particularly with children who have attachment difficulties, among them adopted and fostered children, and with withdrawn, anxious or autistic children.

Several other play therapies take their names from adult therapies taught elsewhere in this book.

  • Cognitive-behavioural play therapy, developed by Susan Knell (1993) for children of about two to six, uses puppets, dolls and stories to model coping statements, problem solving and gradual exposure. It is cognitive behavioural therapy for children too young to talk about their thoughts (Section 10.3 covers CBT with older children).
  • Gestalt play therapy, set out by Violet Oaklander in Windows to Our Children (1978), uses drawing, clay, sand and fantasy to sharpen a child's awareness of sensations and feelings and to restore contact, the aims of Gestalt therapy (Section 6.3).
  • Adlerian play therapy, developed by Terry Kottman, uses play to understand the child's lifestyle and mistaken goals and relies heavily on encouragement (Section 3.8).
  • Prescriptive play therapy, championed by Charles Schaefer, who with Kevin O'Connor founded the Association for Play Therapy in the United States in 1982, chooses the techniques to fit the particular child and problem. Its rationale is that play has many distinct therapeutic powers (communication, release, mastery, relationship and others), and that they are not equally useful for every difficulty. Most practising play therapists today are integrative in this sense.

Table 10.5 brings the whole family together, and Figure 10.3 places its members in time.

Table 10.5 The main forms of play therapy compared.
ApproachKey figure and dateWho leadsCore idea
Kleinian play analysisMelanie Klein, 1920s (book 1932)Child plays; analyst interpretsPlay equals free association; deep interpretation
Anna Freudian child analysisAnna Freud, 1927Analyst, after a preparatory phaseWin trust, work with parents, educate as well as interpret
Release therapyDavid Levy, 1938Therapist sets the sceneReplay a specific recent trauma to release feeling
Relationship therapyJessie Taft, 1933; Frederick Allen, 1942ChildThe present relationship and the child's will to grow

Exam trap

Theraplay (Ann Jernberg, Chicago Head Start, 1967) is adult-led, physical and attachment-based, organised around structure, engagement, nurture and challenge, with parents brought into the sessions. It is the opposite of non-directive play therapy, and it is not filial therapy (parents trained to hold child-led sessions) or PCIT (behavioural parent training). Cognitive-behavioural play therapy is Susan Knell's; Gestalt play therapy is Violet Oaklander's; the Association for Play Therapy was founded in 1982 by Charles Schaefer and Kevin O'Connor.

Does play therapy work?

For its first half-century play therapy rested on case studies, and Dibs, however moving, is a case study: one child, one therapist, no comparison group, and an outcome reported by the therapist herself. Controlled studies accumulated slowly, and by the end of the twentieth century there were enough to pool.

Key study: Bratton, Ray, Rhine and Jones (2005)

Sue Bratton and her colleagues pooled 93 controlled outcome studies of play therapy published between 1953 and 2000, comparing children who received play therapy with children who received none or a minimal alternative. The average effect size was 0.80 of a standard deviation, a large effect by Jacob Cohen's conventions (Section 8.2): the average treated child ended up better off than about four in five untreated children. Humanistic, non-directive approaches showed a larger average effect (0.92) than non-humanistic, directive ones (0.71), although far fewer studies of the directive kind were available (12 against 73). The largest effect of all (1.15) came when parents, trained and supervised in filial methods, conducted the play sessions themselves. Critics pointed out that many of the studies were small, many were unpublished dissertations, outcome measures varied widely and were often completed by people who knew which children had been treated, and very different children and problems were lumped together. Later and stricter re-analyses have produced smaller, though still positive, average effects.

Where, then, does play therapy fit? The evidence and clinical judgement agree that it is a sound choice for young children with emotional and adjustment difficulties after life events (a new sibling, a move, a divorce, a bereavement, a stay in hospital), for children whose difficulties are bound up with relationships, for withdrawn children who cannot be reached through talk, and as a way of building the relationship within which other methods can work. For several specific disorders, more targeted treatments have stronger evidence and should usually come first: parent management training for conduct problems (Section 4.10), cognitive behavioural therapy for anxiety (Section 10.3), trauma-focused CBT after abuse or disaster (Section 10.3), behavioural treatment and medication for ADHD (Section 10.4), and structured behavioural and developmental programmes for autism (Section 10.5). In practice the boundary is blurred, because almost every child therapy uses play. The Coping Cat programme for anxious children uses role-play and games, and trauma-focused CBT may use dolls and drawings to build the child's trauma narrative. The real choice is less between play and no play than between play as the treatment itself and play as the vehicle for a more structured treatment.

Indian context

Play therapy needs little equipment, and most of it can be local: a steel kitchen set, clay, dolls that include grandparents as well as parents, a toy autorickshaw and bus, small animals, sand and water. Floor play comes naturally to most Indian children. Three adjustments help. Parents often expect advice and quick results, and may see a child who "only plays" as money wasted; a clear explanation at the start of what play therapy does, and regular meetings with the parents, prevent early drop-out. Space in hospital clinics is often scarce, so a corner with a mat, a small set of carefully chosen toys and a fixed routine may have to serve as the playroom, and it can. And because trained child therapists are few and concentrated in cities, filial approaches, in which parents, grandparents or other carers learn to hold the sessions, make particular sense. Controlled trials from India are still few, so it is wise to track each child's progress with simple, repeated measures from parents and teachers.

Exam trap

Bratton, Ray, Rhine and Jones (2005): 93 controlled studies, average effect size about 0.80 (large); humanistic approaches about 0.92 against 0.71 for directive ones; the largest effect, about 1.15, when parents delivered the play sessions. Play therapy is well supported for general emotional and adjustment problems, but it is not the first-line treatment for conduct disorder, ADHD, specific anxiety disorders or autism, where more targeted methods have stronger evidence.

Play is the medium; it is not the whole of child treatment. When a child's difficulty is a specific fear, a pattern of defiance that the whole family has learned, or a set of skills that school demands, the clinician turns to more structured methods built on the learning principles of Chapter 4 and the cognitive principles of Chapter 5, delivered to the child, the parents and the school together. Those methods are the subject of Section 10.3.

Scan Your Brain

10.3 Cognitive Behavioural, Parent and School InterventionsMedium yield · 4 Qs

Play therapy, as Section 10.2 showed, trusts the child to find his own way through a problem in a free and accepting relationship. The treatments in this section start from a different bet. They assume that many of the problems children bring to a clinic (fears that keep them from school, outbursts that wreck a classroom, defiance that exhausts a family) are maintained by learned habits of thinking and acting, and by the way the adults around the child respond, so that the quickest route to change is to teach new skills directly: to the child, to the parents and to the teachers. These are the most thoroughly tested psychological treatments for children, and they make up most of what a child clinical psychologist actually does in a working week. Chapter 4 has already taught the behavioural building blocks (reinforcement, extinction, time-out, token economies, parent management training and classroom management), and Chapter 5 the cognitive ones. This section shows how those blocks are assembled into named programmes for children, and how the school can become a place of treatment and prevention as well as of learning.

Adapting cognitive behavioural therapy for children

Cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT) for adults, as Chapter 5 described it, asks a great deal of the client. He must notice his own thoughts, put them into words, write them in a thought record, treat them as hypotheses and test them against evidence. Ask a seven-year-old what she was thinking when she refused to go into her classroom and the honest answer is usually "I don't know" or "I was scared". This is not stubbornness. The ability to think about one's own thinking (metacognition) is still developing in middle childhood, abstract reasoning about probabilities ("how likely is it, really, that Mummy will have an accident?") is hard before adolescence, and a young child's vocabulary for feelings may run to little more than "happy", "sad" and "angry". The stages of cognitive development themselves belong to the Developmental book; the clinical point is that CBT has to be rebuilt, not merely simplified, to fit the child in front of you.

Three adjustments follow, and they define child CBT. The first is that the younger the child, the more the treatment leans on its behavioural half: graded exposure, modelling, role-play, rehearsal and rewards do most of the work, while the cognitive part is kept concrete. Instead of disputing a belief, a therapist may draw a cartoon of a boy with an empty thought bubble above his head and ask the child what the boy might be thinking as a dog runs towards him, or turn worry into a character (a "worry monster") that can be talked back to. Feelings are rated on a thermometer drawn on paper rather than on a scale from 0 to 100. Self-talk is taught as short, memorable coping statements, a line of work that began with Donald Meichenbaum's self-instructional training for impulsive children (Section 5.5). With adolescents, by contrast, the work can look much more like adult cognitive therapy.

The second adjustment concerns the therapist's style. Child CBT therapists describe themselves as coaches: active, playful, encouraging and willing to model the very behaviour they want to see. Modelling here is usually coping modelling (Section 4.7). The therapist does not show a fearless performance; she shows herself feeling a little nervous, using a skill and getting through ("My heart is beating fast. That's my body's alarm. I'll take a slow breath and try anyway"), because a model who struggles and copes is more believable to an anxious child than one who never struggles at all. Effort is rewarded with stickers, points or a game at the end of the session, and homework becomes a challenge to be conquered rather than a worksheet to be completed.

The third adjustment is the family. A child does not live alone with his thoughts; he lives with parents who may, with the best intentions, keep his problem going. A mother who answers every question for her shy son, lets him sleep in her bed whenever he is frightened or writes a note excusing him from the school picnic is reducing his distress today and protecting his anxiety for tomorrow. Clinicians call this family accommodation: the changes family members make to their own behaviour to spare an anxious child distress or to avoid conflict. Parents may therefore join child CBT in one of three roles. As consultants they supply information about the child's problem and progress. As collaborators they coach the child through homework and reward his efforts. And as co-clients they receive help for their own part in the problem: their own anxiety, their accommodation of the child's fears, or their inconsistent discipline. How much the parent component adds depends on the family. A trial by Paula M. Barrett, Mark Dadds and Ronald Rapee in Brisbane (1996) randomly assigned 79 anxious children aged 7 to 14 to CBT, CBT plus a family anxiety management component, or a waiting list. Twelve months after treatment, about 70 percent of children who had received CBT alone and about 96 percent of those whose parents had also been trained were free of an anxiety diagnosis. Later trials, summarised by Barlow and Durand, suggest that family-based treatment outperforms individual treatment mainly when a parent is anxious too.

Table 10.6 sets the adjustments side by side. Notice that the principles do not change; what changes is how they are delivered.

Table 10.6 How cognitive behavioural therapy is adapted for children.
FeatureCBT with adultsCBT with children
Who seeks helpUsually the clientUsually a parent or teacher; the child must be engaged
Cognitive workThought records, disputing beliefs, Socratic questioningCartoons and thought bubbles, coping self-talk, simple "detective" questions
Balance of methodsCognitive and behaviouralMore behavioural the younger the child: exposure, modelling, role-play, rewards
Measuring feelingsRatings from 0 to 100Feelings thermometer, faces, coloured scales
Therapist styleCollaborative guideActive coach; coping modelling; games
HomeworkWritten assignmentsShort, concrete challenges with a reward
FamilyUsually not involvedParents as consultants, collaborators or co-clients
Exam trap

CBT is not unsuitable for young children; it is adapted. A question asking what is distinctive about CBT with children wants concrete, visual methods, a larger behavioural component, coping modelling and the involvement of parents. Do not pick options that say child CBT avoids exposure (exposure is its active ingredient for anxiety) or that it is simply play therapy with homework. Family accommodation (parents changing their own behaviour to spare the anxious child distress) maintains anxiety and is a target of treatment, not a technique.

The Coping Cat: CBT for anxious children

Anxiety disorders are the commonest psychological problems of childhood, and the treatment that set the pattern for nearly every later programme came from Philip C. Kendall and his colleagues at the Child and Adolescent Anxiety Disorders Clinic of Temple University in Philadelphia. Kendall had made his name in the late 1970s and the 1980s with cognitive behavioural treatment for impulsive children (his book on it with Lauren Braswell appeared in 1985), built on the view that a child's difficulties are shaped by how he interprets events and by the coping skills he has. In the late 1980s he turned the same approach to anxious children and built it into a manual that ordinary clinicians could follow. The child's workbook, published in 1990, gave the programme its name. The Coping Cat is a manualised CBT programme for children aged about 7 to 13 with generalised anxiety, separation anxiety or social anxiety, usually delivered in 16 weekly sessions of about 50 minutes. (The disorders themselves are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

The programme has two halves. The first eight sessions are educational: the child learns to recognise anxious feelings and the bodily signs that go with them, to relax, to notice anxious self-talk and replace it with coping self-talk, to solve problems and to reward himself for trying. The second eight sessions are for practice. The child and therapist build a hierarchy of feared situations and the child faces them one by one, first in imagination and then in real life, using his skills, with the therapist alongside at first and then on his own. Parents attend separate sessions (in the standard manual, the fourth and the ninth) to learn what the programme is doing and how to support it, and each week the child takes home a "Show That I Can" task: a small challenge to carry out and record. The skills are gathered into a four-step coping plan with a memorable acronym. The FEAR plan asks four questions: Feeling frightened? (noticing the bodily signs of anxiety); Expecting bad things to happen? (catching the anxious thought); Actions and attitudes that can help (coping self-talk and things to do); and Results and rewards (judging how it went and rewarding the effort, not just success).

Watch the plan at work. Vihaan is nine and lives with his parents and grandmother in Bengaluru. For a year he has refused to stay anywhere without his mother: not at his cousin's birthday party, not at his great-aunt's flat downstairs, and lately not even in his own room at bedtime. Each refusal is preceded by stomach aches and tearful questions about whether his mother will come back. His FEAR plan for an evening at his great-aunt's flat is shown in Figure 10.4. Notice that the "E" step does not argue Vihaan out of his fear with statistics, which a nine-year-old would not find convincing; it helps him notice his prediction and find a more realistic one ("Amma always comes back; she has never forgotten me"). Notice too that the reward is for trying. If Vihaan stays for one hour instead of the planned two, he still earns his sticker and some praise for coping, and the next step on his ladder is made a little smaller.

Key study: Kendall's first randomised trial of the Coping Cat (1994)

Question. Can a structured, 16-session CBT programme cure anxiety disorders in children, and do the gains last? Method. Kendall randomly assigned 47 children aged 9 to 13 with anxiety disorders to the 16-session programme or to a waiting list. Outcome was measured from every angle the field then had: the child's self-report, parent and teacher reports, cognitive assessment, diagnostic interviews and direct observation of the child's behaviour. Result. Many treated children no longer met criteria for an anxiety disorder at the end of treatment, and they stayed within normal limits on many measures at a one-year follow-up; children on the waiting list improved far less. Conclusion. Childhood anxiety disorders respond to a brief, manualised CBT programme. Criticism. The sample was small and the comparison was with a waiting list, not with another treatment, so the trial could not show what in the programme mattered. A second trial with 94 children (Kendall and colleagues, 1997) replicated the result and offered a first answer: the gains appeared mainly after the exposure sessions of the second half had begun.

The strongest test came a decade later. In the Child/Adolescent Anxiety Multimodal Study (CAMS), reported by John Walkup and colleagues, including Kendall, in 2008, 488 children and adolescents aged 7 to 17 with separation anxiety, generalised anxiety or social anxiety were randomly assigned to 14 sessions of CBT based on the Coping Cat, to the antidepressant sertraline, to the combination, or to placebo pills, for 12 weeks. The proportion rated much or very much improved was about 81 percent with the combination, 60 percent with CBT alone, 55 percent with sertraline alone and 24 percent with placebo. CBT and the drug did about equally well, the combination did best, and CBT came with fewer side effects such as insomnia, fatigue and restlessness. The programme has also been adapted for teenagers (the C.A.T. Project, for ages about 14 to 17) and translated for other countries; the Australian "Coping Koala" used in Barrett's 1996 trial was one such adaptation. When children are told that a little cat in a workbook copes with being scared, they are being offered a coping model of their own.

Exam trap

The Coping Cat is Philip Kendall's programme for childhood anxiety (generalised, separation and social anxiety), not Alan Kazdin's (problem-solving skills training for conduct problems, described later in this section), not Kenneth Kendler's (a behaviour geneticist) and not a play therapy. FEAR = Feeling frightened? Expecting bad things to happen? Actions and attitudes that can help; Results and rewards. The first half teaches skills, the second half applies them through graded exposure, which is the active ingredient. In CAMS (2008) the combination of CBT and sertraline beat either alone, and CBT and sertraline were roughly equal.

Trauma-focused CBT for children

Section 5.6 introduced trauma-focused cognitive-behavioural therapy (TF-CBT), the treatment that Judith Cohen, Anthony Mannarino and Esther Deblinger developed in the 1990s, first for sexually abused children, and listed its components under the acronym PRACTICE: psychoeducation and parenting skills, relaxation, affective expression and modulation, cognitive coping, trauma narrative and processing, in vivo mastery of trauma reminders, conjoint child-parent sessions, and enhancing future safety. Here the interest is in how the treatment is put together for a child, because its structure is what makes it suitable for children at all.

TF-CBT usually runs for about 12 to 16 sessions, and the components fall into three broad phases of roughly equal length. In the stabilisation and skills phase (psychoeducation, parenting skills, relaxation, feelings work and cognitive coping) the child learns what trauma does to people, how to calm his body, how to name and manage feelings and how to catch unhelpful thoughts. In the trauma narrative and processing phase he tells the story of what happened, a little at a time, in words, drawings, a book or a poem, and the therapist helps him identify and correct the distorted beliefs embedded in it ("It was my fault"; "Nowhere is safe"). In the integration and consolidation phase he faces safe reminders he has been avoiding (in vivo mastery, the one component used only when needed), shares the narrative with his caregiver in joint sessions, and learns skills for future safety. Two features run through all three phases. One is gradual exposure: from the first session the child is asked to say a little more about the trauma than before, so the narrative is the peak of a slope, not a cliff. The other is the caregiver, who must not be the person who harmed the child. Each session is typically split between the child and the caregiver, who learns the same skills, deals with her own distress, and is prepared to hear the narrative calmly when the child shares it. Parents are treated as the main agents of the child's recovery.

Consider a ten-year-old girl from a fishing village who was trapped with her younger brother on the roof of their house for a night when floods swept through, and who now wakes screaming, refuses to cross the bridge to school and has decided that she should have saved the family's goat. Her TF-CBT begins not with the night of the flood but with psychoeducation about how frightened brains keep sounding alarms after danger has passed, breathing practice, and a game in which she and her mother name feelings. Only when she can calm herself does she begin her "flood book", one chapter a week, and the belief "I should have saved her" is examined gently ("What could a ten-year-old holding her little brother's hand on a roof have done?"). The book ends with what she knows now, and is read aloud to her mother in a joint session. The last sessions walk her, step by step, across the bridge.

The evidence is unusually strong. In a multisite trial by Cohen, Deblinger, Mannarino and Robert Steer (2004), 229 children aged 8 to 14 who had been sexually abused, 89 percent of whom met full criteria for post-traumatic stress disorder, were randomly assigned with their caregivers to TF-CBT or to child-centred therapy, a supportive, non-directive treatment of the kind described in Section 10.2. Children who received TF-CBT improved significantly more in post-traumatic stress, depression, behaviour problems, shame and abuse-related attributions, and their parents improved more too, in their own depression, their distress about the abuse and their parenting. A follow-up in 2006 found the advantages still present 6 and 12 months later. TF-CBT is recommended as the first-line psychological treatment for traumatised children and adolescents in the major international guidelines. Its use with children who have been abused, and the clinician's legal duties when abuse is disclosed, are taken up in Section 10.6.

Exam trap

"What is the main goal of TF-CBT?" The keyed answer is to develop the trauma narrative and desensitise traumatic memories (Entrance Mock). The tempting options are each part true. Teaching coping skills is the first phase, the preparation for the narrative, not its goal. Emotion regulation and mindfulness describes dialectical behaviour therapy (Section 5.8) better than TF-CBT. "Closure and relief" is too vague to be the goal of any particular therapy. Remember also that TF-CBT beat child-centred therapy (Cohen and colleagues, 2004), and that the participating caregiver must be non-offending.

Skills training for aggressive children: problem solving and Coping Power

Parent management training (Section 4.10) changes the consequences that adults provide. But aggressive children also carry something into every encounter that their parents cannot change from outside: a way of reading other people. In a study published in 1980 the American developmental psychologist Kenneth A. Dodge led boys working on a puzzle to believe that another boy, out of sight, had spoiled part of their work, and varied what that boy seemed to intend: harm, help or something left unclear. When the intention was ambiguous, aggressive boys reacted as though the act had been deliberate, and retaliated, far more often than other boys. Later studies using stories (a classmate knocks over the tower of blocks a boy has built, and it is not clear whether by accident) found the same pattern. Dodge called this the hostile attribution bias: a tendency to attribute hostile intent to others when their intentions are ambiguous. In his later social information-processing model (with Nicki Crick, 1994), the bias is one of several possible faults in the sequence a child goes through in any social situation: noticing the cues, interpreting them, choosing a goal, generating possible responses, deciding on one and carrying it out. Aggressive children tend to notice fewer cues, read hostility into them, think of fewer responses (and mostly aggressive ones), and expect aggression to work.

If aggression rests partly on faulty problem solving, problem solving can be taught. Alan Kazdin, whose description of parent management training you met in Section 4.10, built a child treatment on this idea. problem-solving skills training (PSST) teaches the child a fixed series of steps for approaching any interpersonal problem, usually as self-statements he learns to say to himself: what am I supposed to do; what are my choices; concentrate and think about each one; choose one; and check how it worked. The steps are practised first on games and simple tasks, then on role-played versions of the situations that get the child into trouble (being teased, being told to stop playing), and finally in real life, with points earned for using them. In a trial by Kazdin, Todd Siegel and Debra Bass (1992), 97 children aged 7 to 13 referred for severe antisocial behaviour received PSST, PMT or both. Each treatment helped, but the combination produced larger and more lasting changes and brought a greater proportion of children into the normal range at a one-year follow-up. Treating the child and the parents together worked better than treating either alone.

The same logic, extended to the school, produced the best-known group programme. John E. Lochman, working first on an Anger Coping Program for aggressive boys in the 1980s and then with Karen Wells, developed the Coping Power Program, a group CBT programme for aggressive children around the move from primary to middle school, with a child component and a parallel parent component. In the child groups, delivered in school, boys work on setting and keeping goals, recognising the bodily signs of anger and using self-talk and distraction to stay calm, taking the other person's perspective and checking their attributions (Dodge's bias, taught as "detective work" about other people's intentions), solving problems and resisting peer pressure. In the parent groups, parents learn the skills of PMT, together with ways of managing their own stress and of keeping track of their children. In a randomised trial reported by Lochman and Wells in 2004, aggressive boys who received the full programme had lower rates of covert delinquency and of parent-rated substance use one year later than controls, with the strongest effects when the parent component was included, while improvements in teacher-rated behaviour at school seemed to come mainly from the child groups.

Exam trap

Match the name to the method. Hostile attribution bias and the social information-processing model: Kenneth Dodge (1980; Crick and Dodge, 1994). Problem-solving skills training for antisocial children: Alan Kazdin (combined with PMT it beat either alone, 1992). Coping Power: John Lochman (with Karen Wells), child groups plus parent groups. Coping Cat: Philip Kendall, anxiety. Do not mix up "Coping Power" (anger and aggression) with "Coping Cat" (anxiety).

Parent training revisited: Barkley, Triple P and what every programme shares

Section 4.10 told the story of parent management training: Gerald Patterson's observation that defiant children and their parents train each other through the coercive cycle, and the treatment that teaches parents to track behaviour, attend to and praise good behaviour, give clear commands, ignore minor misbehaviour and use brief time-out and loss of privileges. It also sketched three of PMT's descendants, Sheila Eyberg's parent-child interaction therapy, Carolyn Webster-Stratton's Incredible Years and Matthew Sanders's Triple P. Two more deserve a closer look here, because they show the two directions in which parent training has grown: towards a precise clinical manual for one kind of child, and towards a public health system for whole populations.

The first is the programme of the American clinical psychologist Russell Barkley (born 1949), best known for his research on attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD). His manual Defiant Children (1987, revised in later editions) builds on Constance Hanf's two-stage model (Section 4.10) and leads parents of non-compliant children through a sequence of about ten steps, each taught in one session and practised at home for a week before the next is added. It begins by explaining why children misbehave (the child's temperament, the parents' own characteristics, family stress and the way consequences are delivered all play a part), and only then teaches skills: daily "special time" in which the parent attends to the child's play without commands or questions; attending to and praising compliance and independent play; a home token system using poker chips for younger children and points for older ones; response cost; time-out for serious non-compliance; extending time-out to other misbehaviour; planning ahead for difficult public places such as shops and weddings; a daily school report card so that home rewards follow school behaviour (Section 4.10); and, finally, how to handle new problems after therapy ends. Return to Manav, an eight-year-old in Delhi whose teachers suspect ADHD and whose mother says that every instruction at home turns into a battle. In the second week of a Barkley-style programme his mother practises fifteen minutes of special time after school, describing what he builds with his blocks and praising him, without a single instruction; by the fourth week a jar of chips on the dining table records every instruction he follows the first time, and the chips buy cricket in the park and choice of the evening's television. Section 10.4 returns to Manav's ADHD.

The second is Triple P, the Positive Parenting Program developed by the Australian clinical psychologist Matthew R. Sanders and his colleagues at the University of Queensland. Sanders's insight was that most parents do not need a dozen clinic sessions, and that a system which offers only intensive treatment will reach only the few families who can get to a clinic. Triple P is therefore organised as five levels of increasing strength: a universal media and information campaign for all parents; two levels of brief consultation, often through primary health care, for parents worried about a specific, mild problem such as bedtime or tantrums; a standard, broad parent training programme, delivered individually, in groups, by telephone or as self-help; and an enhanced level that adds help with the parents' own mood, stress and marital conflict. The guiding idea is the minimally sufficient intervention: give each family the least intensive help that will be enough, and step up only if it is not. Its other central idea is parental self-regulation: parents are taught to set their own goals, monitor their own behaviour and solve their own problems, so that they do not remain dependent on the therapist. Triple P has been tested at the level of whole communities. In a trial in South Carolina reported by Ronald Prinz, Sanders and colleagues in 2009, counties were randomly assigned either to receive the whole Triple P system or to continue with usual services, and the Triple P counties had lower rates of substantiated child maltreatment, out-of-home placements of children, and hospital-treated maltreatment injuries.

Look across all these programmes (Patterson's, Kazdin's, Barkley's, PCIT, the Incredible Years and Triple P) and the family resemblance is striking. Table 10.7 lists what they share and what they deliberately leave out.

Table 10.7 What behavioural parent training programmes share, and what they leave out.
Shared by the programmesLeft out of the programmes
A social learning model: problem behaviour is learned and maintained by its consequencesBlaming the child's character or the parents' personalities
Parents, not the child, as the main agents of changeThe child as the only person in treatment
Positive attention, play and praise taught first ("catch the child being good")Discipline taught before the relationship is repaired
Rewards for good behaviour: stickers, points, chips, privilegesBribes offered to stop a tantrum already in progress
Clear, specific commands, one at a timeVague requests, lectures and repeated nagging
Planned ignoring of minor attention-seeking (extinction)Giving in to stop the noise (negative reinforcement)
Brief, mild, predictable consequences: time-out, loss of privilegesPhysical punishment, shaming and threats
Modelling, role-play, homework and coaching, with progress measuredInsight into unconscious motives as the route to change

One row of the table needs a closer look, because it is the one examiners test. Every programme teaches parents to stop using negative reinforcement. In the coercive cycle (Figure 4.14), the parent withdraws a demand when the child screams, which negatively reinforces the screaming, and the parent's giving in is itself negatively reinforced by the end of the screaming. PMT breaks the cycle by teaching the parent to hold the demand calmly and to sit out the extinction burst. Negative reinforcement is therefore the trap PMT exists to remove, not one of its techniques.

What kind of parent do these programmes aim to create? The developmental psychologist Diana Baumrind described the answer in the 1960s as the authoritative style: warm and responsive, and at the same time firm, consistent and willing to explain the rules (the Remember box in Section 7.7 sets it beside the authoritarian, permissive and uninvolved styles, which belong to the Developmental book). A more recent worry runs in the opposite direction from neglect, and it has a vivid name. The phrase goes back to the child psychologist Haim Ginott (1922 to 1973), whose book Between Parent and Teenager (1969) quotes a teenager complaining that his mother "hovers over me like a helicopter". helicopter parenting is an over-involved, over-protective style in which parents monitor and manage a child's life far beyond what his age requires, solving problems he could solve himself, intervening with teachers and friends, and steering his choices of subjects and careers. It has no single cause. Parental anxiety (about safety, failure or the future), parents' unfulfilled wishes lived out through the child, parents' own personality traits (such as perfectionism and a need for control), and the pressures of a fiercely competitive education system all feed it, which is why the keyed answer to "helicopter parenting is the result of" is "all of the above". Studies have linked it with higher anxiety, lower self-efficacy and poorer self-regulation in children and young adults, although the evidence is not uniform. For the clinician it matters because it is a form of accommodation on a grand scale: an anxious child with a helicopter parent rarely gets the chance to discover that he can cope.

Exam trap

"Which of the following is not a part of parent management training?" Answer: negative reinforcement (IHBAS 2020). Attending to positive behaviour, rewarding positive behaviour and ignoring undesirable behaviour are all core PMT skills. A parent who deliberately ignores tantrums to discourage them is using extinction (Amity 2023); a key that marks "operant escape" is wrong, because escape describes behaviour that ends something unpleasant, which is what the parent who gives in is doing. "Emotional support with clear boundaries and accountability" is Baumrind's authoritative style (Real PYQ 2025), not authoritarian (strict, cold), permissive (warm, few rules) or neglectful. Helicopter parenting is multiply caused: anxiety, unfulfilled wishes and personality traits together (IHBAS 2020). Triple P is Sanders (Queensland, five levels); Defiant Children is Barkley; PCIT is Eyberg; the Incredible Years is Webster-Stratton.

Schools as a setting for treatment and prevention

Children spend about a thousand hours a year in school, and almost every child in a district can be found there, which makes the school both the place where many problems first show themselves and the most efficient place to reach children who would never come to a clinic. Section 4.10 has already described the behavioural tools that work inside a single classroom: rules, praise and planned ignoring, the Good Behaviour Game, classroom token economies, response cost and the daily report card. The modern idea of school mental health goes further. It treats the whole school as a system that can promote well-being in every child, catch problems early in some and arrange intensive help for a few, in a continuum that mirrors the universal, selective and indicated prevention of Section 9.6. Table 10.8 shows the three tiers.

Table 10.8 The three tiers of school mental health.
TierWho it reachesExamples
Universal (tier 1)Every child in the schoolSchool-wide rules and recognition, social and emotional learning lessons, anti-bullying policy, life-skills education
Targeted (tier 2)Children at risk or with early problemsSmall-group CBT or social skills groups, daily report cards, check-in with a mentor, Coping Power groups
Intensive (tier 3)A few children with serious problemsIndividual functional assessment and behaviour plan, individual therapy, referral to a clinic, crisis response

The best-known version of this tiered model for behaviour is school-wide positive behaviour support (often called positive behavioural interventions and supports, PBIS), developed in the 1990s by Robert Horner and George Sugai at the University of Oregon from the positive behaviour support movement of Section 4.6. At the universal tier, the whole staff agrees on three to five positively stated expectations (be safe, be respectful, be responsible), teaches them as deliberately as arithmetic, acknowledges children who meet them, and uses data on discipline referrals to decide where problems are concentrated. A common rule of thumb is that universal supports are enough for about four in five children, that perhaps one in seven needs targeted help, and that about one in twenty needs individual, intensive support based on a functional assessment.

A second universal approach teaches the skills themselves. social and emotional learning (SEL) is the process through which children learn to understand and manage emotions, set and reach goals, feel and show empathy, build relationships and make responsible decisions. The Collaborative for Academic, Social, and Emotional Learning (CASEL), founded in the United States in 1994, groups these into five competencies: self-awareness, self-management, social awareness, relationship skills and responsible decision-making. Is teaching them a distraction from academic work? A meta-analysis by Joseph Durlak, Roger Weissberg and colleagues (2011) of 213 school-based universal SEL programmes, involving 270,034 children from kindergarten to high school, found the opposite: children in SEL programmes improved in social and emotional skills, attitudes and behaviour, and their academic achievement rose by about 11 percentile points compared with controls. The programmes worked when ordinary teachers delivered them, provided they were well implemented.

A third concerns a problem that every Indian parent and teacher recognises. The Swedish-Norwegian psychologist Dan Olweus (1931 to 2020), of the University of Bergen, began studying bullying in Scandinavian schools in the 1970s. In his definition, bullying is aggressive behaviour in which a child is exposed, repeatedly and over time, to negative actions by one or more other children, in a relationship marked by an imbalance of power or strength. The three elements (intent to harm, repetition, and a power imbalance) separate bullying from ordinary conflict between equals. It may be physical, verbal or relational (spreading rumours, deliberate exclusion), and today it is often carried on through phones as cyberbullying. In late 1982 three boys in northern Norway took their own lives, apparently after severe bullying, and in 1983 the Norwegian government launched a national campaign against bullying in schools. Olweus evaluated the programme he designed for it in Bergen, following about 2,500 children in classes 5 to 8 from 42 schools for two and a half years, and reported that bully and victim problems fell by 50 percent or more, along with reductions in vandalism, theft and truancy. The study was not a randomised trial (the national campaign made random allocation impossible), but later evaluations in Norway and the United States have found smaller, real reductions. The Olweus Bullying Prevention Program works at several levels at once: the school (a survey of bullying, a coordinating committee, better supervision of the playground and corridors at break times), the classroom (clear rules against bullying and regular class meetings), the individual (serious talks with children who bully, with victims and with their parents) and, in later versions, the community.

Where does a psychologist fit in all this? Rarely as the person who teaches every lesson. The clinical psychologist or school counsellor working with a school is most useful as a consultant: helping teachers design behaviour plans, training them to run SEL and anti-bullying work, screening for children who need more help, running targeted groups, assessing and referring children with serious difficulties, and responding when a crisis strikes (a suicide, a death, a disaster; Section 9.7). Educational and vocational guidance, the oldest part of school counselling, is described in Section 10.8.

Exam trap

Olweus's definition of bullying has three parts: intentional harm, repetition over time and an imbalance of power; a single fight between equals is not bullying. The Bergen programme (from 1983) followed a national campaign triggered by three suicides in 1982. School-wide positive behaviour support is a three-tier model (universal, targeted, intensive) from Horner and Sugai; the Good Behaviour Game is Barrish, Saunders and Wolf (Section 4.10). CASEL's five SEL competencies are self-awareness, self-management, social awareness, relationship skills and responsible decision-making; "intelligence" and "academic achievement" are not on the list.

School mental health in India

The need is large. In the National Mental Health Survey of 2015 to 2016, conducted by NIMHANS, about 7.3 percent of the adolescents aged 13 to 17 who were surveyed had a diagnosable mental disorder, and very few were receiving care. India's response has come through several programmes rather than one, and you should know the main ones by name.

  • The Rashtriya Kishor Swasthya Karyakram (RKSK), the national adolescent health programme of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, was launched on 7 January 2014 for young people aged 10 to 19. It covers six themes: nutrition, sexual and reproductive health, non-communicable diseases, substance misuse, injuries and violence (including gender-based violence), and mental health. It works through trained peer educators (two boys and two girls for about every thousand people in a village) and through Adolescent Friendly Health Clinics in government health facilities, which offer counselling as well as medical care.
  • The School Health Programme under Ayushman Bharat, a joint initiative of the health and education ministries, began in February 2020 to identify two teachers in every government school as "Health and Wellness Ambassadors", who run weekly activity-based sessions on themes that include emotional well-being, interpersonal relationships and safety from violence, and link students to the adolescent health clinics.
  • Manodarpan, launched by the Ministry of Education (then the Ministry of Human Resource Development) on 21 July 2020, during the COVID-19 pandemic, offers psychosocial support to school and college students, their parents and teachers through a national tele-counselling helpline, a website, a handbook and a directory of counsellors.
  • The Delhi government's Happiness Curriculum, launched on 2 July 2018 for children from nursery to Class 8 in its schools, gives a daily period to mindfulness, reflective stories, activities and expression: a home-grown example of social and emotional learning.

Two further facts shape the work. Section 17 of the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act 2009 bans physical punishment and mental harassment in school (Section 4.10), so teachers need practical alternatives, which is exactly what the methods of this section supply. And many private schools now employ a counsellor, while most government schools do not, so the clinical psychologist who can train teachers multiplies his reach many times over.

Clinical link

In Indian schools the commonest referrals to a counsellor are falling marks, exam anxiety, bullying, refusal to attend, defiance and, in older children, low mood and self-harm. Academic pressure is the background to most of them, and parents often ask the psychologist to "motivate" the child to study more. A useful first step is to separate the problem the adults are worried about from the problem the child is living with. A Class 10 student whose marks are falling may be depressed (Section 10.4), being bullied, struggling with an undiagnosed learning disability (Section 10.5) or simply exhausted by tuition until ten at night, and each calls for something different.

10.4 Treating Common Childhood and Adolescent ProblemsLower yield · 1 Q

Open the register of any child guidance clinic in India and the same problems fill page after page: the eight-year-old who still wets the bed, the eleven-year-old who has not been to school for a month, the restless boy whose teacher has written "cannot sit still" on every report, the defiant twelve-year-old whose parents have run out of punishments, the fifteen-year-old who has stopped eating with the family and has cuts on her forearm. This section follows those children into treatment. Their disorders themselves (the criteria, the prevalence, the causes) are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book; here the question is always the clinician's question: what works, for whom, and how do I deliver it? You will find that the answer draws on every tool met so far in this book, put together in different proportions for different problems.

Bedwetting and soiling

Section 4.4 told the story of the treatment that founded behaviour therapy with children: the Mowrers' bell and pad (1938), its explanation by classical conditioning and by Lovibond's avoidance account, its high success rate and its weakness of relapse, the refinements of overlearning and intermittent alarms, Azrin, Sneed and Foxx's intensive dry-bed training (1974), and the drug desmopressin. Two further procedures complete the behavioural picture of nocturnal enuresis, and one comparison of all of them tells you which to choose.

The first procedure works during the day. Many bedwetting children have a small functional bladder capacity: they feel the urge and pass urine at lower volumes than other children. In 1970 H. D. Kimmel and E. Kimmel described what became known as retention control training: the child drinks extra fluid during the day and, when he feels the urge, is asked to hold on for a few minutes before going to the toilet, with the waiting time lengthened gradually from day to day and each success rewarded. The aim is to stretch the bladder's capacity and to strengthen the child's control over the urge. Some families like it because it does not disturb anyone's sleep. On its own, however, it has proved much weaker than the alarm, and it is now used mainly as one ingredient of a package.

The best-known package came from the American psychologist Arthur C. Houts and his colleagues. full-spectrum home training (1983) combines four elements: the urine alarm, cleanliness training (after a wet night the child strips the bed, takes the sheets to the laundry and remakes the bed), retention control training, and overlearning once the child is dry. It is taught to parents and children in a single group session, with a written manual and a family contract for home. In their first report, Houts, Robert Liebert and Wendy Padawer treated 60 children this way with only about 15 minutes of professional time for each; 81 percent stopped wetting, and only about a quarter of those relapsed over the following year. A meta-analysis by Houts, Jeffrey Berman and Hilary Abramson (1994) then compared the treatments directly. Enuretic children benefited substantially from treatment of almost any kind, but more children improved with psychological than with drug treatment, and the treatments that included a urine alarm were the ones most likely to keep their gains after treatment ended.

In practice the clinician works through a sequence. A paediatrician first rules out medical causes (a urinary infection, constipation, diabetes, or new wetting after a long dry spell, which raises the question of stress or abuse). The family is told that bedwetting is common, involuntary and nobody's fault, and all punishment and teasing are stopped. Simple measures come next: fluids spread through the day rather than loaded in the evening, a visit to the toilet before bed, and a star chart that rewards behaviour the child can control (going to the toilet before bed, helping to change the sheets) rather than dryness alone. For a motivated child of school age the alarm is then the treatment of choice, continued until the child has had about two weeks of consecutive dry nights and finished with overlearning. Desmopressin is kept for a short-term need such as a school trip, or for families who cannot manage the alarm.

Soiling is less common, more shameful and more often misunderstood. encopresis is the repeated passing of faeces in inappropriate places (usually the clothes) by a child old enough to be expected to be toilet trained (by convention at least four years old; the criteria belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book). The key clinical fact is that most cases are retentive: the child has chronic constipation, often after a painful bowel movement taught him to hold on; a hard mass builds up in the lower bowel, stretches it so that the normal urge to go is no longer felt, and liquid stool leaks around the mass. Parents who see soiled underwear assume laziness or defiance, scold the child, and the child, ashamed, hides the underwear and holds on even harder. The non-retentive form, without constipation, is less common and more often linked with stress, family conflict or oppositional behaviour.

Treatment of retentive encopresis is therefore a partnership with the paediatrician. The bowel is first cleared of the impacted mass with laxatives or enemas, and then kept soft with a maintenance laxative and a diet with more fibre and fluid. The psychologist's part is the behavioural programme that teaches the bowel and the child a new routine. In scheduled toileting the child sits on the toilet for about five to ten minutes after each main meal, when the gastrocolic reflex makes a bowel movement most likely, with his feet supported on a footstool so that he can push effectively. He earns rewards on a chart for sitting, for passing a motion in the toilet and for clean pants, and there is no punishment for accidents, which are handled matter-of-factly (the child helps to clean up, without lectures). Just as important is removing blame. Michael White's case of Nick and "Sneaky Poo" (Section 6.8) showed how externalising the problem, turning it into a character that tricks the child and the family, can unite parents and child against the problem instead of against each other. Table 10.9 summarises the treatments for both problems.

Table 10.9 Psychological treatments for elimination problems.
TreatmentDeveloper and dateWhat it involvesStatus
Bell and pad (urine alarm)Mowrer and Mowrer, 1938Alarm wakes the child at the first drops of urineFirst-line for nocturnal enuresis; relapse common
OverlearningYoung and Morgan, 1972Extra fluids at bedtime once the child is dry, alarm still in placeReduces relapse
Retention control trainingKimmel and Kimmel, 1970Daytime holding on for gradually longer periodsWeak alone; used in packages
Dry-bed trainingAzrin, Sneed and Foxx, 1974Alarm, hourly waking, positive practice, cleanliness trainingEffective but demanding
Full-spectrum home trainingHouts and colleagues, 1983Alarm, cleanliness training, retention control, overlearningEffective and cheap to deliver
Scheduled toileting with rewardsStandard behavioural practiceSitting after meals, footstool, chart, with laxatives for constipationCore treatment for encopresis
Exam trap

The urine alarm is for enuresis, not encopresis; most encopresis is retentive (constipation with overflow), so treatment starts with clearing the bowel and continues with scheduled toileting after meals and rewards. Retention control training is Kimmel and Kimmel (1970); dry-bed training is Azrin, Sneed and Foxx (1974); full-spectrum home training is Houts. Dry-bed training contains positive practice, which is a form of overcorrection, but when a question asks what overcorrection is used to manage, the keyed answer is repetitive aggressive or disruptive behaviour, as in autism or intellectual disability (Section 4.6), not bedwetting. Punishing a child for wetting or soiling makes both problems worse.

Anxiety, school refusal and selective mutism

The treatment of childhood anxiety disorders was set out in Section 10.3: CBT with graded exposure, such as the Coping Cat, is the first-line treatment; parents are drawn in, especially when they are anxious themselves or when family accommodation is keeping the anxiety alive; and for moderate to severe anxiety a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor such as sertraline may be added, the combination doing best in the CAMS trial. Parents can even carry the treatment alone. In the SPACE programme (Supportive Parenting for Anxious Childhood Emotions) of Eli Lebowitz and colleagues, the therapist sees only the parents, who learn to reduce their accommodation of the child's fears while expressing confidence that the child can cope; in a randomised trial published in 2020 with 124 anxious children aged 7 to 14, this parent-only treatment proved no less effective than CBT delivered to the child.

Why should a parent's presence keep a child anxious? Learning theory supplies a precise answer. A safety signal is a stimulus that has been consistently present with, or paired with, the absence of something feared or painful, so that its presence comes to reduce fear. The contrasting stimulus, which predicts that the aversive event is coming, is a warning signal or avoidance cue, and the pleasant drop in fear when an aversive event ends is called relief. For a child with separation anxiety, the mother is a powerful safety signal: when she is in the room, nothing bad happens. That is comforting, but it also means that the child never learns that he would have been safe without her, and safety signals are therefore gradually faded in exposure therapy, much as safety behaviours are dropped in adult CBT (Section 5.6).

Anxiety turns into an emergency when it keeps a child out of school. The problem has been recognised for a long time. In 1941 the American psychiatrist Adelaide M. Johnson and her colleagues coined the term school phobia, which they saw, in psychoanalytic terms, as an expression of mutual separation anxiety between mother and child. The British child psychiatrist Ian Berg and his colleagues (1969) gave the description that is still used to separate school refusal from truancy. In school refusal, the child has severe difficulty attending school, often leading to prolonged absence; he shows emotional upset at the prospect of going (fearfulness, temper, misery, or bodily complaints such as stomach aches and headaches without a medical cause); he stays at home with his parents' knowledge during school hours; and he shows no significant antisocial behaviour such as stealing, lying or destructiveness. The truant, by contrast, hides his absence from his parents and spends the day elsewhere, often with peers, and is not anxious about school. Table 10.10 sets the two side by side.

Table 10.10 School refusal and truancy compared.
FeatureSchool refusalTruancy
Parents' knowledgeChild stays at home with parents' knowledgeAbsence hidden from parents
Emotion about schoolFear, misery, tantrums, bodily complaints on school morningsLittle anxiety; boredom or lack of interest
Antisocial behaviourAbsentOften present (lying, stealing, delinquent peers)
Where the child goesHomeElsewhere, often with friends
SchoolworkOften average or good; child may want to do work at homeOften poor
Usual treatmentAnxiety-focused CBT, graded return, parent workBehavioural contracts, family and school measures, conduct-problem treatments

Berg's distinction tells you what the absence looks like. It does not tell you why it continues, and two children who both stay at home with their parents' knowledge may be doing so for quite different reasons. The American psychologist Christopher A. Kearney, working with Wendy K. Silverman, proposed a functional model in 1990. Kearney uses the broad term school refusal behaviour for any child-motivated refusal to attend school or difficulty staying there for the whole day, and asks what the refusal achieves for the child. He identified four functions, measured by the School Refusal Assessment Scale (SRAS, later revised), which asks the child and the parents a set of questions about each one (Figure 10.5). Two functions are maintained by negative reinforcement: staying away gets the child away from something unpleasant at school. He may be avoiding school-related things that provoke distress (the noisy assembly, the bus, the toilets, a general dread that has no single object), or escaping aversive social or evaluative situations (being teased, reading aloud, examinations, sports in front of others). The other two are maintained by positive reinforcement: staying at home gets him something pleasant. He may be seeking attention from a parent, often with separation worries, or pursuing tangible rewards outside school (television, the phone, games, sleeping late, time with friends). Each function points to its own treatment, which is the point of the model: in Kearney and Silverman's first study of seven children, six were back in school full time after treatment matched to their function, and stayed there at a six-month follow-up.

Whatever the function, a few principles hold for nearly every case. The first is to act quickly, because every week at home makes the return harder: the child falls behind, friendships fade, the fear grows, and the comforts of home become more firmly established. The second is a graded return, planned with the school, in which attendance is built up step by step, much as an exposure hierarchy is (Section 4.3). The third is to work with the parents, who must agree to make school-hour home life dull (no television, phone or games until the school day is over), to give calm, firm instructions on school mornings, to resist the pull of the child's distress at the gate, and to praise every step towards school. The fourth is to enlist the school: a named teacher who meets the child on arrival, a quiet place to go if he is overwhelmed, help with work he has missed, and action against any bullying.

Ishita is twelve and in Class 7 at a private school in Pune. She missed a week of school with a stomach infection in July and has not been back since; it is now late August. Every school morning she says that her stomach hurts, cries when her mother insists, and is allowed to stay at home, where she watches serials on television and messages her friends. Her paediatrician has found nothing wrong. On the School Refusal Assessment Scale the highest scores are for escaping evaluative situations (a mathematics test was due the week she fell ill, and a girl in her class had laughed at her answer in front of everyone the term before) and for tangible rewards at home. The plan therefore has two halves. With the psychologist, Ishita learns to manage her anxiety, to challenge the prediction "everyone will laugh at me", and to rehearse what she will say when friends ask where she has been. With the school, a return is planned: the first day, she arrives with her mother after assembly and stays for two periods with her favourite teacher; by the end of the second week she stays until lunch; by the end of the fourth she is back full time, and her mathematics teacher has agreed to let her sit the missed test privately. At home, the television is switched off from eight in the morning until four in the afternoon, and each school day earns her an hour of phone time in the evening.

A related problem shows how specific a child's anxiety can be. In selective mutism a child who speaks normally at home consistently fails to speak in particular social situations, most often at school, for more than a month (and not only in the first month of school). The disorder is now grouped with the anxiety disorders, and most affected children are socially anxious; well-meaning parents and teachers sometimes keep it going by answering for the child. Treatment uses the behavioural principles of exposure, starting from where the child already speaks. In stimulus fading applied to speech, the child first talks freely with a parent in an empty classroom, then a teacher enters the room at a distance, then gradually comes closer and joins the conversation, until the child is speaking to the teacher alone; the stimulus that controls speech (the parent) is gradually faded out while a new one (the teacher) is faded in. Shaping (from nodding, to sounds, to single words, to sentences) and rewards for "brave talking" are added, and pressure, bribery and punishment for not speaking are avoided. Barlow and Durand describe an intensive week-long group programme for children aged 4 to 8, the Brave Buddies camp at Boston University, built on these methods.

Exam trap

The four functions in Kearney's model (with Silverman, 1990): avoiding school stimuli that provoke negative feelings, escaping aversive social or evaluative situations (both negative reinforcement), seeking attention and seeking tangible rewards outside school (both positive reinforcement). Remember the general distinction they rest on (Real PYQ 2024): in escape learning the behaviour ends an aversive situation that is already present, whereas in avoidance learning it prevents the aversive situation from beginning. A stimulus paired with the absence of a noxious stimulus becomes a safety signal (IHBAS 2020), not an avoidance cue, which predicts danger, nor a relief stimulus, and "readiness prompt" is not a learning-theory term at all. The same logic answers a second Real PYQ 2024 item: a compulsion such as repeated hand-washing that brings down the anxiety of a contamination obsession is best seen as an avoidance response or safety behaviour, maintained by negative reinforcement (Section 4.3), not a conditioned stimulus, an unconditioned response or an operant kept going by positive reinforcement. The child who stays home with the parents' knowledge and is anxious is a school refuser; the one who hides his absence is a truant. "School phobia" is the older term (Johnson and colleagues, 1941).

Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder: behaviour, medication and the MTA study

No childhood problem has generated more argument about treatment than attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD). Its core features (inattention, hyperactivity and impulsivity, beginning in childhood and present in more than one setting) are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book. Treatment has always run on two tracks. The biological track began by accident. In 1937 the American psychiatrist Charles Bradley, working at a children's home in Rhode Island, gave the stimulant Benzedrine (an amphetamine) to children with behaviour problems in the hope of easing headaches after a medical procedure, and noticed instead that many of them became calmer, more attentive and better at their schoolwork. Today the most widely used medicines are the stimulant medication methylphenidate and the amphetamines, which increase the availability of dopamine and noradrenaline in the brain and, in hundreds of trials, reduce the core symptoms in most children within days. Non-stimulant drugs such as atomoxetine are alternatives. The stimulants do not "sedate" children, and the old belief that they have a paradoxical calming effect only in children with ADHD is wrong: in low doses, people with and without ADHD respond in the same way, with improved focus. Their common side effects are loss of appetite, difficulty falling asleep and, in some children, irritability; and their effects last only as long as the medicine is taken.

The psychological track uses the methods of Chapter 4 and of Section 10.3. In a review of the evidence in 2008, William Pelham and Gregory Fabiano classed three behavioural treatments as well established for ADHD: behavioural parent training (programmes such as Barkley's, in which parents learn to structure the day, give clear commands and use points, praise and time-out consistently); behavioural classroom management (the rules, praise, response cost and daily report cards of Section 4.10); and intensive peer-focused behavioural treatment in recreational settings, of which the best-known example is Pelham's summer treatment programme, a day camp in which children spend several weeks in sports, games and classroom-like activities under a detailed point system, with coaching in social skills and good sportsmanship. Behavioural treatment has a strength that medicine lacks: it can teach skills (following instructions, finishing work, getting on with other children) and change the way parents and teachers respond. Its weaknesses are that it demands a great deal of adults, and that its effects, like medicine's, tend to fade when it stops. How do the two tracks compare? That question produced the largest treatment trial in the history of child psychiatry.

Key study: the Multimodal Treatment Study of Children with ADHD (MTA, 1999)

Question. Over more than a few weeks, is medication, intensive behavioural treatment or their combination the best treatment for ADHD? Method. The National Institute of Mental Health funded six research teams in the United States and Canada, which randomly assigned 579 children aged 7 to 9.9 with the combined type of ADHD to one of four arms for 14 months. Medication management meant carefully adjusted doses, usually of methylphenidate, with monthly review visits. Intensive behavioural treatment combined about 35 sessions of parent training, consultation with the child's teacher and a daily report card, a classroom aide for part of the school year and an eight-week summer treatment programme, with the therapist's involvement gradually reduced over time. The combined arm received both. The fourth arm, community care, was referred back to ordinary services in the community. Result. All four groups improved. For the core ADHD symptoms, children in the combined and medication arms improved significantly more than those in the behavioural and community care arms, and the combination was not significantly better than medication alone. For several other outcomes, however (oppositional and aggressive behaviour, anxiety and depressive symptoms, teacher-rated social skills, parent-child relations and reading), the combined treatment did better than behavioural treatment or community care while medication alone did not, and combined treatment achieved its results with somewhat lower doses of medicine. Strikingly, the study's medication management beat community care although two-thirds of the community care children were also taking medicine: how a drug is given matters as much as which drug. Conclusion. For core symptoms over 14 months, careful medication management was the most effective single treatment; combining it with behavioural treatment brought modest further benefits in other areas of the child's life. Criticism. The behavioural treatment had been faded before the final assessment, while medication continued right up to it, which favoured the medication arms; and the outcome that matters most, how the children fared years later, could not be judged at 14 months.

Re-analyses sharpened the picture. When James Swanson and his colleagues (2001) combined parent and teacher ratings into a single measure of "success" (symptoms rated as "just a little" or less at the end of treatment), the success rates were 68 percent for combined treatment, 56 percent for medication management, 34 percent for behavioural treatment and 25 percent for community care (Figure 10.6), and the advantage of the combination over medication alone, small as it was, now reached statistical significance. The follow-ups were more sobering. By 36 months, two years after the study's own treatment had ended, the groups no longer differed on any measure, partly because many children had stopped or started medication on their own. At six and eight years (Brooke Molina and colleagues, 2009) the original groups still did not differ, and as a group the children who had had ADHD were doing worse than their classmates on most measures. What predicted how a child was doing in adolescence was not which treatment he had received but how well he had responded in the first few years.

What should a clinician take from all this? Not that behavioural treatment is useless, for three reasons. First, guidelines recommend parent training as the first treatment for preschool children with ADHD, for whom medication is used more cautiously. Second, behavioural treatment improves things medication does not reach, such as parenting and the child's relationships, and the combination allows lower doses. Third, the order of treatment may matter. In a trial by Pelham and colleagues (2016) with 146 children aged 5 to 12, children who began with a low dose of behavioural treatment (eight group sessions of parent training and a daily report card set up with the teacher) and had medication added only if needed did better on the main outcome, classroom rule violations, than children who began with medication and had behavioural treatment added, and their parents were much more likely to keep attending. For a school-age child with marked symptoms, the usual recommendation today is medication together with parent training and classroom management.

Return to Manav, the eight-year-old in Delhi whose parent training began in Section 10.3. A child psychiatrist confirms ADHD, combined presentation, and discusses methylphenidate with the family; his parents want to try the behavioural programme first, and the psychiatrist agrees to review in three months. The psychologist meets Manav's class teacher, and together they set up a daily report card in his school diary with three targets (starts written work within two minutes of the instruction; stays in his seat during the maths period with no more than two reminders; keeps his hands to himself in the line), with the chips from his home programme as the back-up reward. Manav sits at the front, away from the window, and is given his instructions one step at a time. By the review, his mornings at home are calmer and his teacher reports fewer fights, but he still finishes barely half of his written work. Medication is started at a low dose, and the behavioural programme continues alongside it: the MTA's combined arm, adapted to one family and one Delhi classroom.

Exam trap

The MTA study (MTA Cooperative Group, 1999; 579 children aged 7 to 9; 14 months) had four arms: medication management, intensive behavioural treatment, the combination, and community care. For core ADHD symptoms, medication management and the combination beat behavioural treatment and community care, and the combination was not significantly better than medication alone; the combination's advantages were in other outcomes (oppositional behaviour, anxiety, social skills, parent-child relations, reading) and in the use of lower doses. By three years the groups no longer differed. Do not write that "behavioural treatment was as effective as medication for core symptoms" or that "combined treatment was clearly superior for core symptoms". Stimulants were first used by Charles Bradley (1937). ADHD is a neurodevelopmental disorder, with symptoms required before age 12 in DSM-5 (Psychiatric Disorders book).

Oppositional and conduct problems

Defiance, aggression and rule-breaking are the commonest reason children in many countries are referred to mental health services, and the best treatment depends above all on the child's age. For children up to about the end of primary school, behavioural parent training is the treatment of first choice: Patterson's PMT and its descendants (Section 4.10), PCIT and the Incredible Years for the youngest children, and Barkley's programme and Triple P (Section 10.3).
