From about seven or eight, child-focused skills training (Kazdin's problem-solving skills training, Lochman's Coping Power) adds to what parents can achieve, and the combination works best. For adolescents with serious antisocial behaviour, parent training alone is too weak, because the young person's behaviour is now maintained by peers, school and neighbourhood as much as by family. Here the family-ecological programmes taught in Section 7.7 take over: James Alexander and Bruce Parsons's functional family therapy, and Scott Henggeler's multisystemic therapy, a home-based treatment grounded in Bronfenbrenner's social-ecological model that works with the family, school and peers at once. In an early trial Henggeler and his colleagues (1992) randomly assigned 84 serious juvenile offenders to MST or to usual youth services; those receiving MST had fewer arrests and spent on average about ten fewer weeks in custody.

One warning from this research deserves a place in every clinician's memory, because it runs against common sense. It seems natural to treat aggressive or delinquent adolescents together in groups: it is efficient, and the young people can practise skills on each other. Yet Thomas J. Dishion, Joan McCord and François Poulin (1999), reviewing their own studies and others, showed that putting high-risk adolescents together in groups can make them worse. Within such groups the young people laugh at, attend to and admire each other's talk about rule-breaking, a process Dishion called deviancy training, and in two controlled intervention studies the adolescents assigned to peer-group interventions showed more problem behaviour later, and in one of them worse outcomes in adulthood, than control youth. The high-risk adolescents were the most vulnerable to this effect. Group work with antisocial young people is not forbidden by this finding, but it must be structured tightly, run by skilled leaders, and mixed with prosocial peers where possible; working through parents and families avoids the problem altogether.

Medication has a limited place: stimulants help when ADHD is also present, and some medicines are used for severe, persistent aggression, but there is no medicine for conduct problems themselves. The work of the juvenile justice system with young offenders is described in Section 10.9.

Exam trap

Match treatment to age: parent management training for young children (first choice up to about 12), child skills training (PSST, Coping Power) added for school-age children, and multisystemic therapy (Henggeler, home-based, Bronfenbrenner's ecology) or functional family therapy (Alexander and Parsons) for antisocial adolescents. Deviancy training (Dishion, McCord and Poulin, 1999) is the reason that grouping high-risk adolescents together can be harmful; it is an iatrogenic effect, not a technique.

Depression in adolescents

Depression becomes much more common after puberty, especially in girls, and in adolescents it often shows itself as irritability, falling marks, withdrawal from friends and arguments at home rather than as obvious sadness. Its treatment follows a stepped logic. For mild depression, support, monitoring, psychoeducation for the family and attention to sleep, activity and school stress may be enough, with a brief psychological therapy if it persists. For moderate to severe depression the main psychological treatments are CBT and interpersonal psychotherapy, and an antidepressant may be added.

CBT for depressed adolescents uses the methods of Chapter 5 (behavioural activation, activity scheduling, cognitive restructuring, problem solving), with more attention to peers and family. The best-known group version is the Adolescent Coping with Depression course, developed by Gregory N. Clarke, Peter Lewinsohn and colleagues in Oregon from Lewinsohn's adult course. In a trial reported in 1999, 123 adolescents with major depression or dysthymia were randomly assigned to 16 two-hour group sessions over eight weeks, to the same groups with a separate group for their parents, or to a waiting list; about two-thirds of the treated adolescents (67 percent) had recovered by the end of treatment, against 48 percent on the waiting list, and adding the parent group made no significant difference. The interpersonal alternative is IPT-A, Laura Mufson and Myrna Weissman's adaptation of interpersonal psychotherapy for adolescents (Section 3.7), which focuses on the disputes, transitions and losses in the young person's relationships, with parents brought in when needed. In a trial in five school-based clinics in New York City (Mufson and colleagues, 2004), 63 depressed, mostly low-income adolescents received IPT-A or the clinics' usual treatment; those who received IPT-A had fewer depressive symptoms and better overall and social functioning, showing that the therapy works when delivered by ordinary school clinicians.

The role of medication was examined in the Treatment for Adolescents with Depression Study (TADS, 2004), described in Section 5.11: after 12 weeks, fluoxetine combined with CBT helped the most adolescents, fluoxetine alone came next, and CBT alone did no better than placebo at that point, although it caught up with the other active treatments by 36 weeks. Fluoxetine is the antidepressant with the strongest evidence in this age group. In 2004 the United States Food and Drug Administration added a black box warning, its strongest form of warning, to antidepressants used in children and adolescents, because pooled trials showed a small increase in suicidal thoughts and behaviour (not in completed suicides) in the first weeks of treatment. The practical lesson is close monitoring when a young person starts an antidepressant, not avoiding treatment, since untreated depression is itself a major cause of suicide.

Riya is fifteen and in Class 10 in Mysuru, with her board examinations five months away. Over the past three months she has stopped going down to play badminton with her friends, sleeps late, picks at her food and snaps at her mother, and her class teacher has noticed that she no longer hands in her work. Her mother brought her after finding thin cuts on her forearm. Riya tells the psychologist, alone, that she feels "like a failure who will never get into science", that her father's comparison of her marks with her cousin's plays on a loop in her head, and that cutting "makes the pressure go away for a bit". Her treatment combines the elements of this section and the next. Sessions of CBT begin with behavioural activation (a timetable that puts badminton and an evening walk with a friend back into the week, alongside study) and move on to the thought "I'm a failure", tested against her actual record. Two family sessions address the comparisons with her cousin, and help her parents see the falling marks as a symptom of depression rather than laziness. Because her depression is moderate and she has begun to self-harm, she is also seen by a psychiatrist, and the safety work described next runs alongside everything else.

Exam trap

The two main psychological treatments for adolescent depression are CBT (for example Clarke and Lewinsohn's Adolescent Coping with Depression course) and IPT-A (Mufson and Weissman). In TADS the combination of fluoxetine and CBT was best at 12 weeks and CBT alone was no better than placebo at that point. The 2004 black box warning concerned a small rise in suicidal thoughts and behaviour, not completed suicide, and calls for monitoring, not for withholding treatment. Irritability can be the presenting mood of a depressed adolescent.

Self-harm and suicidal adolescents

Self-harm, most often cutting the arms or legs or taking an overdose of tablets, is far commoner in adolescence than at any other age, and it covers two overlapping groups. In non-suicidal self-injury (NSSI) the young person injures herself without intending to die, most often to relieve unbearable feelings (as Riya put it, to "make the pressure go away"), to punish herself, to feel something when she feels numb, or to communicate distress; the relief it brings negatively reinforces it, which is why it tends to recur. In a suicide attempt there is some intention to die. The two cannot be told apart reliably from the method, and many young people who begin with NSSI later attempt suicide, so every episode of self-harm is taken seriously. The assessment of suicide risk is taught in Section 2.7 and crisis work in Section 9.7.

Several principles guide the work with a young person who has harmed herself. Every adolescent who presents after self-harm should receive a full psychosocial assessment by a trained professional, covering the act and its meaning, her current mental state, her relationships, school, any abuse or bullying, substance use and the supports around her. Current guidance, such as that of NICE in the United Kingdom (2022), warns against using risk scales or tools to predict who will repeat self-harm or die by suicide, because they predict poorly; the assessment is used to understand the person and plan care, not to sort her into "low" or "high" risk. A safety plan of the kind developed by Stanley and Brown (Section 9.7) is written with her, in her own words: her warning signs, things she can do to cope on her own, people and places that distract her, people she can ask for help, professionals and helplines to call, and how to make her surroundings safer. With adolescents, means restriction involves the family directly: medicines in the house are locked away or kept in small quantities, and in rural India, where pesticides are a common means of suicide, they are stored locked and away from the home. Parents are usually told about self-harm, because a young person's safety outweighs her wish for secrecy; the limits of confidentiality with minors are discussed in Section 10.6. And the old "no-suicide contract" has no evidence behind it and has been replaced by the safety plan.

For adolescents who harm themselves repeatedly, two structured therapies have the best evidence. Dialectical behaviour therapy for adolescents (DBT-A), adapted by Alec Miller, Jill Rathus and Marsha Linehan (Section 5.8), shortens Linehan's programme, brings parents into a multifamily skills group and adds a module on "walking the middle path" between extremes in family life; in a multisite trial by Elizabeth McCauley and colleagues (2018) it reduced suicide attempts and self-harm more than supportive therapy. mentalisation-based treatment for adolescents (MBT-A), adapted from Anthony Bateman and Peter Fonagy's treatment for adults (Section 3.5), helps the young person and her family understand the mental states (feelings, wishes, intentions) behind their own and each other's behaviour; in a trial by Trudie Rossouw and Fonagy (2012) with 80 adolescents who self-harmed and were depressed, a year of MBT-A reduced self-harm and depression more than usual treatment. Two pointers complete the picture for India. Section 115 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 presumes that a person who attempts suicide is under severe stress and directs the government to provide care, effectively ending the treatment of a suicide attempt as a crime (Chapter 1); and the national Tele-MANAS helpline offers round-the-clock support (Chapters 1 and 9).

Clinical link

When you ask an adolescent about self-harm, ask directly and calmly ("Some young people who feel the way you do hurt themselves. Have you ever done that?"). Asking about suicide does not put the idea into a young person's head; Section 2.7 tells the study by Madelyn Gould and colleagues (2005) of New York high-school students that showed it. Avoid two common reactions that make things worse: horror, which teaches the young person to hide the injuries, and dismissing it as "attention-seeking", which ignores the fact that a need for attention, for someone to notice her distress, is itself a reason to respond. Look at the wounds' care with the medical team, and never make treatment conditional on her promising never to self-harm again.

Eating problems, and choosing a treatment

Eating disorders usually begin in adolescence, and the treatment with the strongest evidence for adolescent anorexia nervosa is family-based: the Maudsley approach of Christopher Dare, Ivan Eisler and colleagues, written into a manual by James Lock and Daniel le Grange as family-based treatment, in which parents take charge of their child's eating without blame until weight is restored, and then hand control back gradually. It is taught in Section 7.7, and the disorders themselves in the Psychiatric Disorders book. Substance use in adolescents is treated with family-based programmes such as multidimensional family therapy (Section 7.7) and with motivational interviewing (Section 6.9).

Step back and look at the whole section. Almost every effective treatment for children and adolescents shares three features: it is structured, with clear goals and measured progress; it is skills-based, teaching the child, the parents or the teachers to do something new; and it involves the adults who control the child's world, more so the younger the child. Table 10.11 brings the problems and their main treatments together.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The changes parents make to their own behaviour to spare an anxious child distress are called family .
  2. In Kendall's Coping Cat programme, the four-step coping plan is called the plan.
  3. The centrepiece of trauma-focused CBT, in which the child gradually tells the story of what happened, is the trauma .
  4. Dodge's finding that aggressive boys read hostile intent into ambiguous acts is called the hostile bias.
  5. Triple P, a five-level parenting system, was developed by Matthew in Queensland.
  6. Parent management training teaches parents to stop using negative , the trap at the heart of the coercive cycle.
  7. Olweus defined bullying by intent to harm, repetition over time and an imbalance of .
  8. Daytime training in which a child holds urine for gradually longer periods is called retention training.
  9. In Kearney's model, school refusal that gets a child television and games at home is maintained by reinforcement.
  10. In the MTA study, the four arms were medication management, intensive behavioural treatment, the combination and care.

Answers: (1) accommodation, (2) FEAR, (3) narrative, (4) attribution, (5) Sanders, (6) reinforcement, (7) power (or strength), (8) control, (9) positive, (10) community.

10.5 Autism, Intellectual Disability and Learning DisabilityHigh yield · 8 Qs

Walk into the child clinic of any large Indian hospital on a weekday morning and three groups of children will account for a large part of the queue: children with autism, children with intellectual disability, and children of ordinary intelligence who cannot learn to read, write or calculate as their classmates do. All three are neurodevelopmental conditions. They begin in the developmental period, they are rooted in the way the brain develops, and none of them is cured. That last fact changes what "treatment" means. The aim is not to remove a disease but to teach the skills the child has not acquired, to reduce the behaviour that stands in the way of learning, to change the environment so that the child can take part in it, and to support the family over many years. The criteria, causes and course of these conditions are described in the Psychiatric Disorders book, and their assessment in the Assessment book (with the Indian certification scales in Section 9.9). This section is about what clinicians do. Keep one fact from the Disorders book in mind as you read: DSM-5 requires two domains for autism, persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction, and restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour and interests. Almost every intervention you are about to meet targets one or both of them.

Exam trap

The DSM-5 questions on autism (the full criteria are in the Disorders book). What a diagnosis requires is persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts (criterion A), together with restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities (criterion B), present from the early developmental period (JEMAS 2025). Delayed speech alone, intellectual disability and hyperactivity-impulsivity are not required, and an IQ below 70 is not a core symptom (IHBAS 2024): DSM-5 records intellectual and language impairment as specifiers ("with or without accompanying intellectual impairment", "with or without accompanying language impairment"). Even so, intellectual disability and language disorder are frequently associated with autism, and ADHD is among the commonest co-occurring conditions; DSM-5, unlike DSM-IV, allows autism and ADHD to be diagnosed together. Hence language development is compromised most commonly in autism, not in ADHD or specific learning disability (JEMAS 2025), and no eye contact, delayed language and repetitive movements together point to autism spectrum disorder (IHBAS 2025). Autism, ADHD, intellectual disability and specific learning disorder are all neurodevelopmental disorders; OCD, borderline personality disorder and generalised anxiety disorder are not (IHBAS 2024).

From Kanner to the refrigerator mother: how autism was discovered

In October 1938 a lawyer from the small town of Forest, Mississippi, brought his five-year-old son Donald to the child psychiatry clinic of the Johns Hopkins Hospital in Baltimore. Donald seemed happiest when he was left alone. He did not look up when his father came home, he paid no attention to other children, and he spent hours spinning blocks, pans and lids. He repeated words and phrases he had heard, often long after he had heard them, and flew into a rage if anything in his routine was changed. Yet he could recite long passages and lists from memory. The psychiatrist who saw him was Leo Kanner (1894 to 1981), an Austrian-born physician who had come to the United States in 1924 and had written the first English-language textbook of child psychiatry, Child Psychiatry (1935). Over the next few years Kanner collected ten more children who resembled Donald.

Key study: Kanner's eleven children (1943)

Question. Did these children, until then labelled feeble-minded, schizophrenic or deaf, share a single condition of their own? Method. Kanner described in detail eight boys and three girls whom he had seen at Johns Hopkins, drawing on his own observation and on long histories from their parents. Result. In "Autistic disturbances of affective contact", published in the journal Nervous Child in 1943, he identified two cardinal features present from early life: an "extreme autistic aloneness", a powerful desire to be left alone and an inability to relate in the ordinary way to people, and "an anxiously obsessive desire for the maintenance of sameness". Around them clustered other features: delayed or unusual language, with echolalia (repeating others' words), reversal of pronouns ("you want a biscuit" meaning "I want a biscuit") and very literal use of words; fascination with objects rather than people; and islands of excellent rote memory. Conclusion. Kanner argued that this was a distinct syndrome, an inborn inability to form the usual affective contact with people, which, from 1944, he called early infantile autism. Criticism. The sample was small and highly selected, mostly children of educated, professional parents. Kanner's remarks about those parents, whom he described as intellectual, serious and undemonstrative, seeded a disastrous theory of cause, as you will see next.

A year later, in wartime Vienna, the paediatrician Hans Asperger (1906 to 1980), who had not read Kanner's paper, described four boys whom he called "autistic psychopaths". They spoke fluently, sometimes in an adult, pedantic way, but their conversation was one-sided; they had intense, narrow interests, poor understanding of other people, odd eye contact and clumsy movement. Asperger's 1944 paper, written in German, was hardly known in the English-speaking world until the British psychiatrist Lorna Wing (1928 to 2014) drew attention to it in 1981 and proposed the name "Asperger's syndrome". Asperger's disorder entered DSM-IV in 1994; DSM-5 (2013) folded it, together with autistic disorder and pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified, into a single autism spectrum disorder. In 2018 the historian Herwig Czech published evidence that Asperger had cooperated with the Nazi regime's programme for "euthanasia" of disabled children, referring some children to the Am Spiegelgrund clinic in Vienna where children were killed (some later scholars dispute that he knowingly took part in the killings), and many clinicians now avoid his name as a label. Two older names sometimes appear in the options. The word "autism" itself was coined by the Swiss psychiatrist Eugen Bleuler in 1911 for the withdrawal into an inner world that he saw in schizophrenia, one of his "four As" (the Disorders book). And in 1925, in Moscow, the child psychiatrist Grunya Sukhareva had described six boys with very similar features, a paper that became widely known only recently.

Wing also did something of great practical importance. In 1979, with Judith Gould, she surveyed all the children with special needs in the London borough of Camberwell and found that the children with autistic features shared a triad of impairments: in social interaction, in communication, and in imagination (with rigid, repetitive behaviour in its place). The triad shaped DSM-IV's three criteria; DSM-5 merged social interaction and communication into one domain, leaving the two you met at the start of this section.

Now to the theory of cause that shaped treatment for twenty years. In 1949 Kanner wrote that the children he saw had been "kept neatly in refrigerators which did not defrost", and out of remarks like this grew the refrigerator mother hypothesis: that autism is a child's withdrawal from a cold, rejecting mother. Its loudest champion was the Austrian-born psychoanalyst Bruno Bettelheim (1903 to 1990), who ran the Orthogenic School in Chicago. In The Empty Fortress (1967) he compared children with autism to prisoners in concentration camps, who had withdrawn from an environment they experienced as deadly, and he treated them by separating them from their parents. Psychodynamic treatments aimed at building the child's ego did not help, and the theory caused a generation of parents, mothers above all, to feel guilty for their children's disability. The counter-attack came from research and from parents. Bernard Rimland (1928 to 2006), a research psychologist whose own son had autism, argued in Infantile Autism (1964) that the condition is biological, and helped found the Autism Society of America in 1965. Eric Schopler, whom you will meet in a moment, showed that parents of children with autism did not differ from other parents and could be their children's best teachers. In 1977 Susan Folstein and Michael Rutter reported a much higher concordance for autism in identical than in fraternal twins, the first strong evidence of its heritability. By the 1980s the blaming of parents had been abandoned, and the questions became practical ones: what can these children learn, and how should they be taught?

Exam trap

"Infantile autism was first described by..." Leo Kanner, in 1943 (IHBAS 2020 and IHBAS 2025). Hans Asperger (1944) described the verbally fluent form, now inside autism spectrum disorder. Eugen Bleuler (1911) coined the word autism, but as a symptom of schizophrenia, not as a childhood syndrome. Kurt Schneider is known for the first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia; Norman Cameron for over-inclusive thinking in schizophrenia; Kurt Goldstein for the loss of the abstract attitude (concrete thinking) after brain injury; and "Jasper" in the IHBAS 2020 options most likely means Karl Jaspers, the founder of phenomenological psychopathology (General Psychopathology, 1913). Lovaas treated autism; he did not describe it. Bettelheim blamed "refrigerator" parents; Rimland (1964) argued for a biological cause. And the double-bind theory (Gregory Bateson and colleagues, 1956) was a family theory of schizophrenia, not of autism (Chapter 7).

The behavioural foundation: early intensive teaching and functional communication

Section 4.6 told the story of Ole Ivar Lovaas at the University of California, Los Angeles: his 1987 report that 9 of 19 children who received about 40 hours a week of one-to-one behavioural teaching before the age of four reached normal functioning, against 1 of 40 controls, and the criticism that cut that claim down. What survives is early intensive behavioural intervention (EIBI), built on discrete trial training, which helps many children a great deal and cures none. The same section introduced functional communication training, devised by Edward Carr and V. Mark Durand in 1985, in which a child whose tantrums are maintained by escape from difficult tasks is taught to ask for a break or for help instead. Rather than retell those stories, this subsection adds the idea that turned them into everyday clinical practice.

The idea is that problem behaviour is communication. A child with little or no speech who bangs his head, bites or screams is usually doing so because the behaviour works: it gets him something or gets him out of something. Behaviour analysts sort the possible functions into four. Attention: the behaviour brings adults running. Escape: it ends or postpones a demand, a noise or a crowded room. Tangible: it produces a toy, a food or an activity. Sensory (or automatic): the behaviour is reinforcing in itself, as rocking or flicking fingers in front of the eyes may be. To identify the function, clinicians use direct observation of antecedents and consequences (the ABC chart of Section 2.8), interviews and rating scales such as the Motivation Assessment Scale (Durand and Daniel Crimmins, 1988), and sometimes a formal functional analysis in which the suspected consequences are arranged one at a time under controlled conditions. Once the function is known, the replacement response must meet two tests. It must be functionally equivalent, producing the same reinforcer as the problem behaviour; and it must be more efficient, easier and faster, so that the child has no reason to go back to screaming. A child who escapes by biting will not give up biting for a "break" card that is honoured only after five minutes of argument.

When functional communication training is combined with making demands easier, with extinction of the old route to the reinforcer, and with lots of reinforcement for everything else, severe behaviour often falls dramatically. Punishment-based procedures such as overcorrection (Section 4.6) are now reserved for dangerous behaviour that has not responded to these positive methods, under supervision. This combination is the core of the positive behaviour support approach you met in Section 4.6, and it is the first thing a clinician should reach for when a child with autism or intellectual disability is referred for "aggression".

Aarav, the boy from Bengaluru whose functional communication training you followed in Section 4.6, is now four and a half. His head-banging is rare, he uses his "break" and "top please" cards many times a day, and he has a few dozen words. His parents want him to join an ordinary preschool next year, to talk more, to cope with the barber and the doctor, and to play with his cousins. The interventions in the rest of this section are the tools his team now draws on.

Exam trap

"A child with autism is taught to request a 'break' using a picture card instead of engaging in a tantrum. This intervention is a key component of..." applied behaviour analysis (Real PYQ 2024). The specific technique is functional communication training (Carr and Durand, 1985), a form of differential reinforcement of alternative behaviour. It is not CBT (which needs the verbal reasoning many such children lack), not play therapy and not psychoanalytic psychotherapy. The "Lovaas programme" uses behaviour modification for autism (RML 2022). Overcorrection is keyed for repetitive aggressive behaviour in autistic children (IHBAS 2020; Section 4.6). The four functions of problem behaviour are attention, escape, tangible and sensory.

Structured teaching: TEACCH

While Lovaas was building his programme in California, a very different one was taking shape on the other side of the United States. Eric Schopler (1927 to 2006) was born in Fürth, Germany, and came to the United States as a refugee child. He trained in clinical psychology and joined the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill in 1964, where he and the psychiatrist Robert Reichler ran a research project on children with autism. Two of their findings went against the received wisdom of the time. First, parents were not the cause of the problem; working alongside the therapists, they were the most effective teachers their children had. Second, the children understood what they saw far better than what they heard. In 1971 the project became TEACCH, short for Treatment and Education of Autistic and Related Communication Handicapped Children, and in 1972 its methods were extended across North Carolina's schools and clinics as a statewide programme. Gary Mesibov, who later directed it, and Schopler set out its methods in a series of books.

TEACCH starts from a different question from Lovaas. Lovaas asked how to change the child so that he would fit the world; TEACCH asks, in addition, how to change the world so that it makes sense to the child. Its writers speak of a "culture of autism": a way of thinking that is strong on visual processing, rote memory and attention to detail, and weak on understanding language, time, sequence and other people's intentions. Instead of fighting this style, TEACCH builds on it. The programme serves people of all ages, from diagnosis to adulthood, and treats parents as co-therapists. Its central method is structured teaching, which has four components.

  1. Physical structure. The classroom or home is divided into clearly marked areas, each for one purpose (a work table, a play corner, a snack area, a quiet place to calm down), with visual boundaries such as shelves or coloured tape and as few distractions as possible, so that the place itself tells the child what happens there.
  2. Visual schedules. The sequence of the day is shown in a form the child can understand: real objects for a child at an early level (a cup means snack), then photographs, line drawings or written words. The child checks the schedule at each transition, which makes the day predictable and transitions less frightening.
  3. Work systems. Each work session is organised so that it answers four questions without words: What work am I to do? How much? How will I know when I have finished? What comes next? Typically, tasks are placed in trays on the left, the child does each one and puts it in a "finished" basket on the right, and a picture at the end shows the reward or the next activity.
  4. Visual structure of tasks. Each task carries its own visual instructions (a picture of the completed product, materials arranged in order, the relevant part highlighted), so the child can work independently instead of waiting for an adult's prompt.

Independence is the point. A child who has learned to follow a schedule and a work system can work alone in a classroom, and later at a job, in a way a child who depends on an adult's spoken prompts cannot. For Aarav, the team builds a picture schedule for the mornings (toilet, bath, breakfast, shoes, school van) and a simple work system for his puzzles and matching tasks; tantrums at transitions fall as soon as he can see what is coming.

How good is the evidence? Reviews describe TEACCH's results as positive but modest: gains in some skills, such as social behaviour and fine motor skills, and reductions in parents' stress, with inconsistent effects on communication and daily living skills. It has fewer randomised trials than the behavioural and naturalistic programmes, but its visual supports have been absorbed into almost every autism programme in the world, including those that call themselves ABA.

Exam trap

TEACCH = Eric Schopler, University of North Carolina (1971; statewide from 1972), structured teaching with physical structure, visual schedules, work systems and visual task organisation. Do not attribute it to Lovaas (UCLA, discrete trials) or to Bondy and Frost (PECS). Schopler was also one of Lovaas's sharpest critics (Section 4.6), and the Childhood Autism Rating Scale (CARS) is his group's assessment tool, which is not the Indian certification instrument (ISAA; Section 9.9). IHBAS 2024 nevertheless keyed CARS as "the Indian tool to assess the severity and disability of children with autism"; the correct fact is ISAA, and Section 9.9 explains how to handle that key. The paper's other two options are not autism tools at all: the Children's Global Assessment Scale (CGAS) is a single rating, from 1 to 100, of a child's overall functioning, and the Child Behavior Checklist (CBCL) is Achenbach's broad parent-report checklist of behavioural and emotional problems (Section 10.1). Expand the acronym correctly: Treatment and Education of Autistic and Related Communication Handicapped Children.

Communication without speech: PECS and augmentative communication

Perhaps a quarter to a third of children with autism, in different estimates, never develop enough speech to meet their daily needs. For them, clinicians use augmentative and alternative communication (AAC): any method that supplements or replaces speech. Unaided AAC uses only the body (gestures, signs). Aided AAC uses something outside the body: a board or book of pictures, a speech-generating device, or an app on a tablet that "speaks" a word when its picture is touched. Parents often fear that a picture system will stop their child from ever talking. The research says the opposite: reviews have found no evidence that AAC inhibits speech, and some evidence that it helps speech emerge.

The best-known aided system began with a practical frustration. In 1985 Andrew Bondy, a psychologist, and Lori Frost, a speech and language therapist, were working in the Delaware Autistic Program in the United States. They noticed that children who had been taught to point to pictures or to label them on request still did not initiate communication: they waited for an adult to ask. So they designed the Picture Exchange Communication System (PECS), in which, from the first day, the child goes up to another person and hands over a picture in exchange for what he wants. In behavioural terms the child is taught a request (Skinner called it a mand) that is reinforced by the very thing requested; in everyday terms, the child learns that communication is something you do to another person and that it pays. PECS is taught in six phases (Figure 10.7), each mastered before the next.

  1. Phase 1, how to communicate. The child sees something he wants (a biscuit) and is physically guided to pick up its picture and put it in the adult's hand, and gets the biscuit at once. Two adults are used: the communicative partner in front, who holds the biscuit, and a physical prompter behind the child, who guides his hand and fades the help as quickly as possible. No spoken prompts ("What do you want?") are used, so that the child initiates.
  2. Phase 2, distance and persistence. The child learns to fetch the picture from a communication book and to travel to a partner who is across the room, or a different person, or in a different place, and to persist until he is noticed.
  3. Phase 3, picture discrimination. The child learns to choose the right picture from two and then from many, so that "biscuit" brings a biscuit and "ball" brings a ball.
  4. Phase 4, sentence structure. The child builds a sentence on a strip, an "I want" card followed by the picture, and hands over the strip. The adult reads it aloud and pauses before giving the item, which often draws out the child's own speech. Attributes (big, red, two) are added later.
  5. Phase 5, answering a direct question. The child learns to answer "What do you want?" with the sentence strip.
  6. Phase 6, commenting. The child learns to answer "What do you see?", "What do you hear?" and "What is it?" ("I see a bus"), and to comment spontaneously, which shares an experience rather than asking for a thing.

The evidence for PECS is good for what it was designed to do. Meta-analyses find that it reliably teaches children to request, often within weeks; the evidence is weaker for maintenance over time, for generalisation to new people and settings, and for the later phases, and it is best seen as a first communication system that may later be replaced by speech or by a more flexible device. For Aarav, whose few words are not enough to ask for most things, PECS begins at Phase 1 with his favourite foods and toys; within two months he is carrying his book to his grandmother in the kitchen, and the delay built into Phase 4 brings out "want" and "juice" in his own voice.

Exam trap

PECS = Andrew (Andy) Bondy and Lori Frost, Delaware, 1985, six phases: (1) how to communicate (the physical exchange), (2) distance and persistence, (3) picture discrimination, (4) sentence structure ("I want" strip), (5) answering "What do you want?", (6) commenting. It teaches requesting first and commenting last, and the child initiates without a spoken prompt in Phase 1. PECS does not stop children from learning to speak. Do not confuse it with social stories (Carol Gray, next subsection) or with facilitated communication (discredited, as you will see shortly), in which another person holds the child's hand while he types.

Teaching social understanding: social stories and social skills training

Imagine trying to get through a wedding, a birthday party or a haircut if nobody had ever explained to you what happens there, why people do what they do, and what is expected of you, and if you could not pick these things up by watching. That is the position of many children with autism, and it is the problem that Carol Gray, an American teacher of children with autism in Jenison, Michigan, set out to solve at the start of the 1990s (1991 is the year usually cited; Gray's own account dates her first stories to 1990). A social story is a short, individualised story, written from the child's point of view and often illustrated, that describes a particular situation, the cues that matter in it, what other people are thinking and feeling, and what the child might do. It is read with the child before the situation, often for several days. Gray specified the kinds of sentences a story should contain: descriptive sentences, which state the facts (where, who, what happens, why); perspective sentences, which describe other people's thoughts and feelings; directive sentences, which suggest a response, gently ("I will try to..."); affirmative sentences, which state a shared value or a rule; cooperative sentences, which say what others will do to help; and control sentences, which the child writes himself to remember the strategy. Her rule is that a story should describe much more than it directs: two to five descriptive, perspective, cooperative or affirmative sentences for every directive or control sentence.

Here is the story Aarav's mother writes for his first visit to the barber. "Sometimes my hair grows long. Amma takes me to the barber shop to cut it. The barber has a big chair and a pair of scissors. The scissors make a snip-snip sound. Many children feel a little worried at the barber. That is okay. The barber wants my hair to look nice. I will try to sit still in the big chair. Amma will hold my hand and sing my song. When the haircut is finished, I can play with my top." Count the sentences: one directive ("I will try to sit still") among nine others, comfortably inside Gray's ratio.

Social stories are cheap, safe and easy for parents and teachers to write, and they are used everywhere. The research evidence, however, is limited: reviews find inconsistent and generally modest effects, and because stories are usually combined with other methods it is hard to say how much of the change they produce. They are best treated as one helpful ingredient, not a treatment.

For verbally fluent older children and adolescents, the main approach is social skills training in small groups, using instruction, modelling, role-play, feedback and homework in real settings (the general method is described in Section 4.7). Video modelling, in which the child watches a short film of someone (sometimes himself, edited to show success) performing a skill such as greeting or taking turns, exploits the visual strengths TEACCH builds on. Peer-mediated interventions train typically developing classmates to invite, prompt and respond to the child with autism, which takes the teaching into the playground where it is needed.

Learning through play: naturalistic and developmental approaches

Discrete trial training at a table is efficient, but, as Section 4.6 noted, skills learned there often stay there. From the 1980s onwards, two groups of researchers moved the teaching onto the floor. Behaviour analysts began to teach during play and daily routines, following the child's interests and using natural consequences; developmental psychologists began to build programmes around the social and emotional milestones that children with autism miss, such as joint attention (looking back and forth between an adult and an object to share interest in it), imitation and pretend play. The two streams met in what are now called naturalistic developmental behavioural interventions. Three of them appear in examinations.

The first, pivotal response treatment (PRT), was developed by Robert L. Koegel (who had worked with Lovaas) and Lynn Kern Koegel, from a "natural language paradigm" for teaching speech that they described in 1987. Its premise is that a few pivotal areas, once improved, produce widespread gains in many untaught behaviours. The pivotal areas are motivation (the most important), responding to multiple cues (attending to more than one feature of a situation, which children with autism often fail to do), self-management and self-initiation of social interaction. To raise motivation, PRT lets the child choose the toy or activity, varies the tasks, intersperses easy, already-mastered tasks among new ones, reinforces reasonable attempts and not only perfect responses, and uses natural reinforcers: when the child says "ball", he gets the ball, not a sweet. A 2020 meta-analysis of randomised trials found a benefit for expressive language, and possibly for social interaction, while rating the quality of the evidence as low.

The second, the Early Start Denver Model (ESDM), was developed by Sally J. Rogers and Geraldine Dawson for toddlers aged about 12 to 48 months. It grew from a play-based preschool programme that Rogers and her colleagues began in Denver in the 1980s, and it adds techniques from PRT. Teaching takes place inside "joint activity routines", playful exchanges such as blowing bubbles or singing action songs, in which the adult follows the child's interest, keeps positive emotion high, and works through a developmental curriculum of imitation, joint attention, communication, play and social skills, using behavioural principles (prompting, shaping, reinforcement) to teach each step. Parents are coached to use the same routines at home.

Key study: Dawson's randomised trial of the Early Start Denver Model (2010)

Question. Can an intensive, play-based, developmental and behavioural programme begun in toddlerhood improve the outcome of autism, tested in a properly randomised trial? Method. Geraldine Dawson, Sally Rogers and their colleagues randomly assigned 48 children diagnosed with autism at 18 to 30 months of age either to ESDM or to the intervention ordinarily available in their community, for two years. The ESDM children received a mean of about 15 hours a week of therapy from trained therapists at home, and their parents reported using ESDM strategies for about 16 hours a week more. Result. Over two years the ESDM group's mean composite cognitive score rose by 17.6 points, against 7.0 points in the comparison group; adaptive behaviour improved more; and seven ESDM children (30 percent), against one comparison child (5 percent), moved from a diagnosis of autistic disorder to the milder pervasive developmental disorder not otherwise specified. Conclusion. Intensive early intervention that combines developmental and behavioural methods can improve intellectual and adaptive outcomes in toddlers with autism. Criticism. The sample was small and from one research centre, and the comparison group received much less intervention. A larger, three-site trial published in 2019 replicated the advantage for language only, with no significant differences in developmental quotient, autism severity or adaptive behaviour, and a brief parent-delivered version tested in 2012 did no better than community care.

The third approach comes from outside behaviour analysis altogether. The American child psychiatrist Stanley Greenspan (1941 to 2010), working with the psychologist Serena Wieder, built the DIR/Floortime model. DIR stands for Developmental, Individual-difference, Relationship-based: it describes the child's level on a ladder of "functional emotional" capacities (from shared attention and engagement, through two-way gestural communication and problem-solving, to the use of ideas in pretend play and logical, emotional thinking), the child's individual differences in sensory processing and motor planning, and the relationships through which development happens. Floortime is the method: the adult literally gets down on the floor, follows the child's lead, joins whatever he is doing (even lining up cars) and playfully extends it, so that the child must respond, "opening and closing circles of communication" that grow longer over time. The evidence for DIR/Floortime is thinner than for ESDM or PRT, consisting of a few small trials. A related, more tightly researched programme, JASPER (developed by Connie Kasari and her colleagues), targets joint attention, symbolic play, engagement and regulation directly.

What do these approaches mean in a country with very few autism specialists? Their common thread is that the most important "therapists" are the people who spend the most time with the child. In the Preschool Autism Communication Trial (PACT), published in 2010 by Jonathan Green and colleagues in the United Kingdom, parents of 152 children aged two to four were filmed playing with their children and coached, using the videos, to notice and respond to their child's communication. Parent-child interaction improved, and a follow-up published in 2016 found a modest reduction in the children's autism symptoms about six years later.

Indian context

The PACT approach was adapted for South Asia as PASS (Parent-mediated intervention for Autism Spectrum disorders in South Asia) by Atif Rahman, Gauri Divan of the Goa-based organisation Sangath, and their colleagues. Non-specialist health workers, not autism professionals, delivered 12 sessions of video-feedback coaching to the parents of children with autism aged two to nine in Goa (India) and Rawalpindi (Pakistan). In a randomised trial of 65 children, published in The Lancet Psychiatry (2016), PASS improved the quality of parent-child interaction compared with usual treatment, and 81 percent of families completed the programme. It is an example of the task-shifting you met in Section 9.6: a treatment designed for a rich country, carried by ordinary health workers to families who would otherwise receive nothing.

What does not work: the lesson of facilitated communication

Parents of a child with a lifelong condition are understandably vulnerable to anyone who promises a cure, and the history of autism is crowded with treatments that were sold before they were tested. Clinicians need to know them, both to advise families and to understand why evidence matters.

Key study: Testing facilitated communication (1993)

Question. In 1977 the Australian special educator Rosemary Crossley began to support the hands of people with severe disabilities as they pointed at letters, and in 1990 the American education professor Douglas Biklen introduced the method, called facilitated communication, to the United States. Through a "facilitator" steadying their hand or arm over a keyboard, young people who had never spoken appeared to type poems, essays and conversations, and some typed accusations that their parents had sexually abused them. Were the messages really theirs? Method. At the O. D. Heck Developmental Center in New York, Douglas Wheeler and his colleagues (1993) tested clients with autism together with their usual facilitators. Client and facilitator sat on either side of a screen and were each shown a picture; sometimes the pictures were the same, sometimes different, and neither could see the other's. Result. The clients typed correct answers only when the facilitator had seen the same picture; when the pictures differed, the typed answer named the picture the facilitator had seen. Conclusion. The facilitators, sincerely and without realising it, were producing the messages, through the ideomotor effect (the tiny, unconscious muscle movements that also move a Ouija board). By the mid-2000s dozens of controlled studies had reached the same conclusion, and in 1994 the American Psychological Association resolved that facilitated communication had no scientifically demonstrated support. Criticism. Supporters argued that testing made the clients anxious, but no controlled test has ever shown authorship by the disabled person. Meanwhile families had been torn apart by abuse allegations that came from the facilitator's keystrokes.

Facilitated communication is the field's great cautionary tale, and its lesson reaches far beyond autism: the sincerity of practitioners and the delight of families are not evidence, and a claim that can be tested by a simple control must be tested. Other unsupported or harmful treatments are worth naming. Sensory integration therapy, developed by the American occupational therapist A. Jean Ayres (1920 to 1988), uses swings, brushing, weighted vests and similar activities on the theory that problems in processing sensory input underlie the child's difficulties; reviews have found little evidence that it changes behaviour or learning, although simply accommodating real sensitivities (ear defenders in a noisy hall) is sensible. Special diets such as gluten-free and casein-free diets have no consistent support in controlled trials. Chelation, which removes heavy metals from the blood, is dangerous and has caused deaths. The fear that the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine causes autism came from a 1998 paper by Andrew Wakefield that was retracted in 2010; large population studies, including those from Denmark, have found no link, and refusing vaccination puts children at real risk.

Medicines have a limited but genuine place. No drug treats the core social and communication features of autism. Two antipsychotics, risperidone and aripiprazole, were approved in the United States (in 2006 and 2009) for the severe irritability, aggression and self-injury that some children show, and stimulants can help co-occurring ADHD. They are adjuncts to teaching, not substitutes for it. Table 10.12 brings the interventions of this section together.

Table 10.12 The main interventions for autism compared.

TEACCHSchopler, North Carolina; 1971, statewide 1972Structured teaching: physical structure, visual schedules, work systemsPositive but modest; less parental stress
