Exam trap

The best-supported psychosocial interventions for autism are behavioural and naturalistic developmental behavioural ones (EIBI, PRT, ESDM, parent-mediated programmes), with PECS for early communication and TEACCH-style visual structure widely used. Facilitated communication is discredited by controlled tests (Wheeler and colleagues, 1993); sensory integration (Ayres) and special diets lack support; the vaccine link was a retracted 1998 paper. Risperidone and aripiprazole treat irritability and aggression, not the core features. Psychoanalytic treatment based on the refrigerator-mother theory did not help.

Intellectual disability: training, education and support

In January 1800 a boy of about twelve, naked and scarred, came out of the woods near the village of Saint-Sernin in the Aveyron region of southern France. He did not speak, ate like an animal and seemed indifferent to heat and cold. He was taken to the National Institute for Deaf-Mutes in Paris, where the great psychiatrist Philippe Pinel examined him and pronounced him an incurable "idiot". A young physician at the institute, Jean Marc Gaspard Itard (1774 to 1838), disagreed. He believed the boy's state was the product of isolation, and for five years he tried to educate "Victor", training his senses, his attention and his social responses with an ingenuity that anticipated many modern methods. Victor learned to recognise some written words, to understand simple requests and to show affection, but he never learned to speak more than a word or two, and Itard, in his reports of 1801 and 1806, counted the project a failure. His student Edouard Seguin (1812 to 1880) drew the opposite lesson: if systematic training could achieve that much, children with intellectual disability could be taught. Seguin's "physiological method" of graded sensory, motor and practical training, set out in his treatise of 1846 and taken to the United States after he emigrated, became the basis of special education, and Maria Montessori (1870 to 1952) adapted his materials, first for children with intellectual disability in Rome and then for all children. From the start, the answer to intellectual disability was education, not cure.

The definition of intellectual disability (significant limitations in both intellectual functioning, roughly two standard deviations below the mean, and adaptive behaviour, beginning in the developmental period) and its levels belong to the Disorders and Assessment books. Two ideas from the field's history, however, govern how clinicians work. The first is normalisation, formulated in Scandinavia by Bengt Nirje (1969) and spread in North America by Wolf Wolfensberger (1972): people with intellectual disability should have patterns of daily life as close as possible to those of everyone else, living in ordinary homes, going to school, working and having relationships, rather than spending their lives in institutions. The second is the shift from asking what is wrong with the person to asking what support he needs. In 1992 the American Association on Mental Retardation (now the American Association on Intellectual and Developmental Disabilities, AAIDD) described levels of support: intermittent (help as needed, at times of transition or crisis), limited (consistent help for a limited time, such as job training), extensive (regular, long-term help in some settings, such as daily support at home) and pervasive (constant, high-intensity help across all settings). DSM-5 similarly grades severity by adaptive functioning rather than by IQ.

Adaptive skills training. The core clinical work is teaching the conceptual, social and practical skills of daily life. DSM-5's three domains give a useful map: conceptual skills (language, reading, money, time), social skills (friendships, judgement, safety with strangers) and practical skills (self-care, cooking, travel, work). The method is the behavioural one taught in Chapter 4. The clinician assesses what the person can already do, chooses functional goals with the family (skills that will be used every day and will make life more independent), breaks each skill into its steps by task analysis, teaches the chain forwards or, often, backwards (Section 4.5), prompts and fades the prompts, reinforces, and teaches in the real settings where the skill is needed so that it generalises. Brushing the teeth may have a dozen steps; buying milk at the corner shop may have twenty. Toilet training by Azrin and Foxx's rapid method (1971) and the reduction of challenging behaviour by functional assessment and positive behaviour support were described in Section 4.6.

Two clinical cautions. People with intellectual disability have higher rates of mental illness than the general population, but their depression, anxiety or psychosis is easily missed, because changes in behaviour are put down to the disability itself. In 1982 Steven Reiss and his colleagues called this diagnostic overshadowing. A young man with moderate intellectual disability who stops eating and sleeping and begins hitting himself may be depressed, in pain or being mistreated, not simply "behaving badly". The second caution is about abuse: children and adults with disabilities are more often abused than others and are less able to report it, which is why the POCSO Act treats taking advantage of a child's disability as an aggravating factor (Section 10.6). Teaching body safety, the difference between private and public, and whom to tell is part of adaptive skills training.

Education. Three models have succeeded one another. In special schools, children with disabilities are taught separately by trained special educators, with small classes and adapted curricula. In integrated education, they attend an ordinary school, often in a separate resource room or special class, and are expected to fit in. In inclusive education, the ordinary school and classroom change to fit the child: curriculum, teaching methods, assessment and the physical environment are adapted so that children with and without disabilities learn together. Inclusion became international policy with UNESCO's Salamanca Statement (1994), and India's Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016 (Chapter 1) defines inclusive education in exactly these terms and directs the government to work towards it in every school it funds or recognises. The usual instrument is an individualised education programme (IEP), a written plan for each child that records the present level of performance, annual goals broken into short-term objectives, the teaching methods and supports to be used, who is responsible, and when progress will be reviewed. In early childhood, home-based programmes such as the Portage Guide to Early Education, a developmental checklist and home-teaching method from Portage, Wisconsin, that has been widely adapted in India, teach parents to be the child's first teachers.

Vocational training and work. For adolescents and adults the goal shifts to work. Training begins with prevocational skills (getting to work on time, following instructions, staying on task, working alongside others) and moves to specific trades. Some people work in a sheltered workshop, a protected setting where they do simple paid work, and others, with the help of a job coach, in supported employment in ordinary jobs; the evidence and the debate between "train then place" and "place then train" were covered in Section 9.8.

Kabir is sixteen and has moderate intellectual disability. He lives with his parents and grandmother in Hyderabad and attends a special school. He can dress, bathe and eat on his own, reads a few words, and can count to twenty; he is cheerful and loves cricket and Telugu film songs. His parents' questions are about the future: what will he do, and who will look after him when they are gone? The clinical psychologist assesses his adaptive behaviour and helps the school write goals for his last two years. A task analysis of making tea and of travelling by bus to his aunt's house turns these into teachable chains, practised with his father in the real kitchen and on the real bus. In the school's vocational unit he learns to pack and label paper bags, and later he begins a supported job, with a job coach, stacking shelves in a supermarket. His parents are helped with their own grief, which often returns at each stage of life (Simon Olshansky, writing about parents of children with intellectual disability in 1962, called it "chronic sorrow"), and with long-term planning, including legal guardianship once Kabir turns eighteen.

That last point brings in Indian law. Under the National Trust Act 1999 (taught with the RPwD Act in Chapter 1), a Local Level Committee in each district can appoint a legal guardian for an adult with autism, cerebral palsy, intellectual disability or multiple disabilities. The National Trust also runs schemes for these four groups, among them Disha (early intervention and school readiness for children up to ten), Vikaas (day care for older children and adults), Samarth (respite and residential care), Gharaunda (group homes for adults) and Niramaya (health insurance).

Indian context

India's national institute for intellectual disability is the National Institute for the Empowerment of Persons with Intellectual Disabilities (NIEPID) in Secunderabad, under the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment. It was registered in 1984 as the National Institute for the Mentally Handicapped (NIMH; not to be confused with the American National Institute of Mental Health) and has regional centres at Noida, Kolkata and Mumbai. It trains special educators and rehabilitation professionals, runs model special-education, early-intervention and vocational services, has trained tens of thousands of parents, and develops Indian tools and teaching materials (ISAA, the Indian autism scale of Section 9.9, was developed there). Special educators and rehabilitation professionals in India are trained in courses recognised by the Rehabilitation Council of India.

Exam trap

"Which disabilities are explicitly covered under the National Trust Act (1999)?" The Act's full name gives the answer: the National Trust for the Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act. So "both autism and intellectual disability" is correct (Real PYQ 2024); specific learning disability and mental illness are not covered (they are among the 21 specified disabilities of the RPwD Act 2016). Conversely, ADHD is not one of the RPwD Act's 21 specified disabilities, although intellectual disability, specific learning disabilities and autism spectrum disorder are (Recall set). For certification, autism uses ISAA, not CARS (Section 9.9). The old DSM-IV-TR IQ criterion for mental retardation was about two standard deviations below the mean (RML 2021), but under DSM-5 an IQ score alone never makes the diagnosis: adaptive deficits are required and set the severity. Normalisation = Nirje and Wolfensberger; levels of support = intermittent, limited, extensive, pervasive; diagnostic overshadowing = Reiss.

Specific learning disability: remediation and accommodation

Sneha is nine and in Class IV at an English-medium school in Mysuru. In class discussions she is quick and funny, and she can explain the water cycle better than most of her friends. But she reads aloud haltingly, guessing at words from their first letter, confuses b and d, spells "because" as "bikos", and takes twice as long as anyone else to copy from the board. Her teacher's report says "careless and lazy; can do better if she tries". After assessment (Section 9.9 explains why specific learning disability is not certified before the age of eight, and the Assessment book describes the tests), she is found to have average intelligence and a specific learning disability in reading and spelling, the condition usually called dyslexia. What now?

There are two broad strategies, and good practice uses both. The first, remediation, means teaching the deficient skill directly, intensively and systematically, to close the gap as far as possible. The second, accommodation, means changing the conditions of learning and examination so that the disability does not hide what the child knows: extra time, a reader or a scribe, oral rather than written answers, audiobooks, reduced copying, typed rather than handwritten work. To these the older student adds compensatory strategies of his own, such as spell-checkers and text-to-speech software. Remediation matters most in the early school years, while the brain's reading networks are still being built; accommodation becomes more important as the demands of school outrun what remediation can achieve. Accommodation without remediation leaves a child dependent on help he may not always get; remediation without accommodation leaves a bright child failing examinations that test his reading speed rather than his knowledge.

What works in remediation. For reading, the strongest evidence supports teaching that is explicit, systematic, intensive and given in small groups or one to one. It usually starts with phonological awareness, the ability to hear and manipulate the sound structure of words: rhyme, then syllables, then individual phonemes (blending "c-a-t" into "cat", splitting "ship" into "sh-i-p", deleting or swapping sounds). It then teaches systematic phonics (the links between letters and sounds, in a planned order), followed by fluency through repeated reading of the same passage, and comprehension strategies such as predicting and summarising. Barlow and Durand describe two broad kinds of educational help, specific skills instruction (vocabulary, finding the main idea, finding facts) and strategy instruction (decision making and critical thinking), and highlight Direct Instruction, a programme originated by the American educator Siegfried Engelmann that uses scripted lessons, small groups formed by progress, and teaching to mastery. Imaging studies have shown that successful remediation changes the activity of the left-hemisphere reading areas, so it is more than a trick for getting round the problem.

Multisensory methods. Two classic methods, much used by Indian special educators, rest on the idea that learning is stronger when several senses are linked at once. In the 1920s the American neuropsychiatrist Samuel Torrey Orton (1879 to 1948) studied children who could not learn to read and called their difficulty "strephosymbolia" (twisted symbols), believing it arose from incomplete dominance of one cerebral hemisphere. With the educator and psychologist Anna Gillingham (1878 to 1963), who published a teaching manual with Bessie Stillman in 1936 (some sources give 1935), he developed the Orton-Gillingham approach: letters and sounds are taught explicitly, in a structured, sequential and cumulative order, and each is learned through several channels at once, the child seeing the letter, saying its sound, hearing it and tracing or writing it. At the University of California, Los Angeles, the psychologist Grace Maxwell Fernald (1879 to 1950), who had opened a clinic school for remedial teaching there in 1921, developed the Fernald method, described in Remedial Techniques in Basic School Subjects (1943). It is often called the VAKT method, for visual, auditory, kinaesthetic and tactile. The child chooses a word he wants to learn; the teacher writes it in large letters; the child traces it with a finger while saying each part aloud, again and again, until he can write it from memory; the words are then used in the child's own sentences and stories, and the tracing is gradually dropped as the child learns to recognise words by sight. Both methods are widely recommended and intuitively appealing, but neither has much rigorous research behind it: reviews in the United States have found few studies of Orton-Gillingham that meet modern standards. Their active ingredient is probably the explicit, systematic phonics they contain.

Writing and mathematics are remediated on the same principles. A child with difficulty in written expression is taught to plan with graphic organisers and to type, and may dictate answers. A child with difficulty in arithmetic is taught number sense and moves through a concrete-representational-abstract sequence: objects first (beads, matchsticks), then pictures and number lines, and only then written numbers and symbols.

Response to intervention. For decades a child was diagnosed with a learning disability when there was a large discrepancy between IQ and achievement. Critics called this a "wait to fail" model: because the gap takes years to grow large, children were identified only in the middle school years, long after early help would have worked best. In 2004 the reauthorised Individuals with Disabilities Education Act in the United States allowed schools to use response to intervention (RTI) instead. Every child receives good, evidence-based teaching and is screened (Tier 1); children who lag behind receive extra small-group teaching with frequent checks of progress (Tier 2); and those who still do not respond receive intensive individual help and a full evaluation for specific learning disability (Tier 3), as Figure 10.8 shows. The child's failure to respond to good teaching becomes part of the evidence for the diagnosis, and help begins before the child has failed. Formal RTI is rare in Indian schools, but the 2024 Indian guidelines, which ask teachers to screen children at eight years or in Class III (Section 9.9), move in the same direction.

Accommodations in India. The RPwD Act 2016 directs the government and local authorities to modify the curriculum and examination system for students with disabilities, naming in particular extra time for completing the examination paper, the facility of a scribe or amanuensis, and exemption from second and third language courses. School boards, including the CBSE and many state boards, grant such concessions to students with a disability certificate for specific learning disability, and the details (how much extra time, which language is exempted, whether a reader is allowed) differ from board to board. Sneha's plan therefore has three parts: four sessions a week of small-group phonological and phonics teaching with a special educator, using multisensory letter work; extra time and reduced copying in class, with a disability certificate to secure examination concessions later; and work with her parents and teacher. That last part may matter most. Children with specific learning disability are often called lazy or stupid for years before anyone identifies the problem, and many lose confidence, avoid school and become anxious or depressed. Explaining to Sneha, her parents and her teacher that her difficulty is specific, real and treatable, and that it has nothing to do with intelligence or effort, is itself an intervention. If she also had ADHD, as many children with learning disability do, its treatment (Section 10.4) would be added.

Exam trap

Remediation teaches the weak skill directly; accommodation changes the conditions (extra time, scribe, reader, language exemption) without teaching the skill; good practice uses both. Orton-Gillingham (Samuel Orton and Anna Gillingham) is a structured, sequential, multisensory phonics approach; the Fernald method (Grace Fernald, 1943) is the VAKT tracing method, visual, auditory, kinaesthetic and tactile. Response to intervention replaces the "wait to fail" IQ-achievement discrepancy with three tiers of increasingly intensive teaching. The RPwD Act names extra time, a scribe and exemption from second and third languages. Specific learning disability requires normal intelligence; if a child's difficulties are explained by intellectual disability, the diagnosis is intellectual disability, not SLD.

10.6 Child Abuse, Child Protection and AdolescentsMedium yield · 2 Qs

Most of the children in this chapter are brought to the clinic by adults who love them and want help. This section begins with children who are harmed by the very adults who should protect them, and it ends with the adolescents who are often reluctant to come at all. The two belong together for a practical reason. A clinical psychologist is frequently one of the few adults outside the family to whom a child or a teenager speaks privately, and what is said in that room can create a legal duty to act. To do this work well you need three things: knowledge of what abuse is and how it shows itself, the skill to listen to a child without contaminating what the child says, and an exact grasp of what Indian law, above all the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act 2012, requires of you.

The discovery of child abuse

Children have been beaten, starved, exploited and sexually used throughout history, but as a medical and psychological problem child abuse was discovered surprisingly late. In 1946 the American paediatric radiologist John Caffey drew attention to infants who had both chronic subdural haematomas (bleeding between the brain and its outer covering) and fractures of the long bones of the arms and legs, with no disease to explain either. He was puzzled, and cautious. Doctors of his generation, faced with a toddler with a broken arm and a parent who said he had fallen off the bed, treated the arm and accepted the story, or looked for some rare bone disease. It took a paediatrician willing to say the unthinkable to change that.

Key study: Kempe and the battered-child syndrome (1962)

Question. Why did some young children arrive at hospital again and again with fractures, bruises and brain injuries that no illness could account for? Method. C. Henry Kempe (1922 to 1984), a German-born paediatrician who chaired the department of paediatrics at the University of Colorado, brought together the evidence from his own department and from enquiries to hospitals and law officers across the United States. His co-authors were the psychiatrist Brandt Steele, the radiologist Frederic Silverman, William Droegemueller and the paediatrician Henry Silver. Result. In "The battered-child syndrome", published in the Journal of the American Medical Association in 1962, they described a recognisable clinical picture: a child, usually under three, with fractures at different stages of healing, subdural haematoma, soft-tissue swelling and bruises, often poor hygiene and failure to thrive, and a history from the parents that did not fit the injuries. Many of these children died or were left with permanent brain damage. Conclusion. The injuries had been inflicted by parents or other carers, and physicians had a duty not merely to treat them but to recognise them for what they were and to report them. Impact and criticism. The deliberately striking label made abuse visible: by the end of the 1960s every American state had passed a law requiring doctors to report suspected abuse. Kempe went on to found a national centre for the prevention and treatment of child abuse and neglect in Denver (1972), to help found the International Society for the Prevention of Child Abuse and Neglect (1977), and to edit The Battered Child (1968) with Ray Helfer. The focus on visible injury, however, meant that sexual abuse, emotional abuse and neglect were recognised only later.

Today the World Health Organization defines child maltreatment as the abuse and neglect that occurs to children under 18 years of age, including "all types of physical and/or emotional ill-treatment, sexual abuse, neglect, negligence and commercial or other exploitation, which results in actual or potential harm to the child's health, survival, development or dignity in the context of a relationship of responsibility, trust or power". Notice the last phrase: what makes harm maltreatment is that it comes from someone the child should be able to trust. The WHO estimates that one in five women and one in seven men report having been sexually abused as children. In India, the Ministry of Women and Child Development's Study on Child Abuse: India 2007, which surveyed 12,447 children in 13 states, found that 53.22 percent reported having faced one or more forms of sexual abuse, that boys were affected as well as girls, that most abuse happened within the family and was committed by people the children knew and trusted, and that most children had told nobody.

The forms of maltreatment

Maltreatment is usually divided into four forms, with exploitation (child labour, trafficking, commercial sexual exploitation) often added as a fifth. They frequently occur together: a child who is beaten is usually also emotionally abused, and a neglected child is more vulnerable to sexual abuse. Table 10.13 sets them out.

The first form, physical abuse, is the non-accidental use of force that injures or endangers a child: hitting, kicking, burning, biting, throwing, poisoning or shaking. Shaking an infant can tear the veins that bridge the surface of the brain and cause bleeding and lasting brain damage without a mark on the skin; this is now called abusive head trauma (formerly shaken baby syndrome). A rarer and stranger form was described in 1977 by the British paediatrician Roy Meadow, who called it Munchausen syndrome by proxy: a carer, usually the mother, fabricates or induces illness in a child (inventing symptoms, falsifying samples, poisoning or suffocating the child) and presents the child repeatedly for medical care. In DSM-5 the carer's condition is called factitious disorder imposed on another, and child-protection services speak of fabricated or induced illness. Where the line between discipline and physical abuse falls is partly cultural, and many Indian parents and teachers still regard beating as ordinary discipline. The law has moved, however: the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act 2009 prohibits physical punishment and mental harassment of children in schools.

The second, child sexual abuse, is the involvement of a child in sexual activity that he or she does not fully understand, cannot give informed consent to, or is not developmentally prepared for. It includes contact abuse (touching, penetration) and non-contact abuse (exposing oneself, showing a child pornography, making a child undress or pose, sexual talk, and the same acts online). Most abusers are known to the child, and many prepare the ground by grooming: winning the trust of the child and the family, singling the child out with attention and gifts, gradually crossing small boundaries so that each step seems normal, isolating the child and insisting on secrecy. Grooming explains why so many children do not resist or tell, and why the abuse is so often discovered late.

The third, emotional abuse (psychological abuse), is a persistent pattern of behaviour that tells a child he is worthless, unloved, unwanted or in danger. In The Psychologically Battered Child (1986) James Garbarino and his colleagues described five forms: rejecting (belittling, shaming, refusing to acknowledge the child's worth), isolating (cutting the child off from friends and normal social experience), terrorising (threatening the child with violence or abandonment), ignoring (being psychologically unavailable and unresponsive) and corrupting (encouraging antisocial or criminal behaviour). Exposing a child to violence between parents is now treated as emotional abuse too. Emotional abuse is probably the most common form and the least often reported, because it leaves no physical marks.

The fourth, neglect, is the persistent failure of a carer to meet a child's basic physical, medical, educational, emotional or supervisory needs, when the carer is able to meet them or to obtain help in meeting them. That last condition matters in a country with much poverty. A family that cannot afford food or medicine is a family in need of support, not necessarily a neglectful one; neglect is a failure of care, not a lack of money. Neglect is usually divided into the categories in Table 10.14, and it is the form of maltreatment most often reported to child-protection services in many countries.

Table 10.13 The forms of child maltreatment.
FormWhat it isExamplesSigns that should raise concern
Physical abuseNon-accidental force that injures or endangersBeating, burning, shaking, poisoning; fabricated or induced illnessInjuries the history does not explain; patterned marks; fractures of different ages; delay in seeking care
Sexual abuseInvolving a child in sexual activity he or she cannot consent toTouching, penetration, exposure, pornography, online abuseGenital injury or infection; sexual behaviour or knowledge beyond the child's age; fear of a particular person
Emotional abuseA persistent pattern that tells the child he is worthless or in dangerRejecting, isolating, terrorising, ignoring, corrupting; witnessing domestic violenceVery low self-esteem; anxiety; extreme compliance or aggression
NeglectPersistent failure to meet basic needsNo food, medical care, schooling or supervisionPoor growth and hygiene; untreated illness; frequent absence from school; child often alone
ExploitationUsing a child for others' gainChild labour, trafficking, commercial sexual exploitationChild out of school and working; unexplained money or gifts
Table 10.14 The categories of child neglect.
CategoryThe carer fails to provideExample
Physical neglectAdequate food, clothing, shelter and hygieneA child repeatedly sent to school hungry, dirty and inadequately dressed
Medical (health) neglectNecessary health careFailing to immunise the child, to seek treatment for a serious illness or to follow medical advice
Educational neglectSchooling and attention to special educational needsKeeping a child at home to work; ignoring chronic truancy
Supervisory neglectSupervision suited to the child's ageLeaving a four-year-old alone at home for hours
Emotional neglectAffection, attention and emotional supportPersistent coldness and unresponsiveness to the child's distress

What does maltreatment do to children? The answer is: a great deal, but not the same thing to every child. Section 9.7 described the Adverse Childhood Experiences study, in which the number of categories of abuse and household dysfunction a person had experienced in childhood predicted adult depression, substance misuse, suicide attempts and physical disease in a graded, dose-response way. In infancy, maltreatment is strongly associated with disorganised attachment, in which the child, frightened of the very person who should be his source of comfort, shows contradictory, freezing or apprehensive behaviour on reunion (the fourth category, added by Mary Main and Judith Solomon, Section 3.5; the attachment classifications are taught in the Developmental book). Even in the absence of blows, severe emotional deprivation is damaging: René Spitz's descriptions of "hospitalism" (1945) and anaclitic depression (1946) in infants who were fed and kept clean in institutions but rarely held or played with (Chapter 3) are early evidence of what emotional neglect does. Yet many maltreated children grow up without disorder. The protective factors of Section 10.1, above all a stable relationship with at least one caring adult, help explain why, and they are what intervention tries to supply.

Exam trap

"All of the following are categories of child neglect EXCEPT..." The answer is use of licensed day care (RML 2020): arranging care by a licensed, responsible provider is a way of meeting the child's need for supervision, not a failure to meet it. Health (medical) neglect, physical neglect (nutrition, housing, clothing) and supervisory neglect are all genuine categories, as are educational and emotional neglect. Do not confuse neglect with poverty. Kempe described the battered-child syndrome in 1962; Caffey (1946) noticed the fractures and haematomas before him; Meadow (1977) described Munchausen syndrome by proxy, now factitious disorder imposed on another, whose motive is the sick role and not external gain (Section 9.1).

Recognising abuse and responding to a disclosure

Clinicians, teachers and school counsellors rarely see abuse happen. They see its traces, and the signs in Table 10.13 are only signs. A child may be withdrawn because of bullying, a bruise may come from a cricket ball, and sexualised play may have been learned from a film. No single sign proves abuse, and some abused children show no signs at all. The clinician's task is therefore not to investigate and decide whether abuse occurred, which is the job of the police and the courts, but to recognise when there is reason to suspect it, to keep the child safe, and to report. Concern should rise when an explanation does not fit the injury or the child's stage of development (a baby who cannot yet crawl does not bruise himself), when injuries are on protected areas such as the ears, neck, buttocks or inner thighs, when marks have the shape of a hand, a belt or a cigarette, when the carer delayed seeking help, and when a child's behaviour changes suddenly: new fearfulness, regression (bed-wetting in a child who was dry), refusal to go home or to a particular place, self-harm or running away.

Often the first sign is something the child says, and it rarely comes as a clear, complete account. In 1983 the American psychiatrist Roland Summit described a pattern he called the child sexual abuse accommodation syndrome, with five elements: secrecy (the abuser insists on it, with threats or bribes), helplessness (a child cannot resist an adult in authority), entrapment and accommodation (unable to escape, the child adapts, sometimes blaming himself), delayed, conflicted and unconvincing disclosure, and retraction (once disclosure brings chaos to the family, the child often takes it back). Summit's purpose was to explain why genuinely abused children often delay telling, tell only part of the story, or retract, so that professionals would not dismiss them. It is not a diagnostic test: the same behaviour occurs in children who have not been abused, and courts have rightly refused to treat the "syndrome" as proof that abuse happened. Its lesson for the clinician is simply that a hesitant, partial or withdrawn disclosure is not evidence that the child is lying.

Pooja is fourteen and in Class IX in Nagpur. She has been referred to the school counsellor, a clinical psychologist, because her marks have fallen and she has started missing her evening tuition classes. In the third session, after a long silence, she says that her tuition teacher "does things" when the other students have left, and that he told her nobody would believe her. Then she begs: "Promise you won't tell my parents. Papa will stop my studies." What should the counsellor do?

  • Stay calm and listen. Shock, anger or disbelief on the adult's face teaches the child to stop talking.
  • Take her seriously and tell her she has done the right thing by telling, and that what happened is not her fault.
  • Do not promise secrecy. Explain honestly, in words she can understand, that this is something you must tell people whose job is to keep her safe, that you will tell her what happens next, and that you will stay with her through it.
  • Ask only enough to understand whether she is safe now, using open prompts ("Tell me more about that"). Do not question her in detail, ask leading questions or ask her to repeat the story; the formal interview belongs to trained investigators, and every extra conversation risks changing her account, as the next subsection shows.
  • Write down, as soon as possible, exactly what she said in her own words, with the date and time, and what prompted it.
  • Report, as the POCSO Act requires (described below), and make sure she is safe now: she should not go back to that tuition class.
  • Never confront the alleged abuser yourself.
Clinical link

Every clinician who works with children should have a sentence ready for the moment a child asks for a promise of secrecy. One that works: "I can keep most things private, but if you tell me that someone is hurting you, I will have to tell people whose job is to make it stop. I will tell you before I do it, and I will help you through it." Saying this early, as part of explaining confidentiality (see later in this section), means that a child who discloses later does so knowing what will happen, and the clinician is never forced to choose between breaking a promise and breaking the law.

Interviewing children: the science of suggestibility

In the 1980s a series of criminal trials in the United States turned on the testimony of very young children who, after many interviews by social workers, therapists, parents and police, described bizarre abuse at their day-care centres. Several convictions were later overturned, and when the interview recordings were examined, the questioning was found to be heavily suggestive: interviewers had asked the same leading questions again and again, praised children for "disclosing", told them that other children had already told, and used anatomically detailed dolls. These cases raised an urgent scientific question: how far can a young child's report of events be trusted, and what makes it go wrong? The researchers who did most to answer it were the developmental psychologist Stephen J. Ceci (born 1950) of Cornell University and Maggie Bruck, whose 1993 review in the Psychological Bulletin, "Suggestibility of the child witness: a historical review and synthesis", and 1995 book, Jeopardy in the Courtroom, are still the starting point. In this context, suggestibility is the degree to which a person's memory and report of events can be altered by information, expectations and pressures introduced after the event.

Key study: The Sam Stone study (Leichtman and Ceci, 1995)

Question. How do stereotypes and suggestive questions affect what preschool children report about an event? Method. A stranger called Sam Stone visited a day-care classroom of children aged three to six for about two minutes. He said hello, walked round the room and left, without touching or harming anything. In the stereotype condition, children had been told stories before the visit, over several weeks, about how clumsy Sam was and how he broke things. In the suggestion condition, they were interviewed four times after the visit with questions that falsely implied that Sam had ripped a book and soiled a teddy bear. A third group got both, and a control group got neither. Some weeks later a new interviewer asked each child what had happened. Result. In the control condition, only about 10 percent of the three- and four-year-olds claimed that Sam had done anything to the book or the bear, and about 5 percent claimed to have seen it. In the combined stereotype and suggestion condition, 72 percent of the three- and four-year-olds claimed one or both misdeeds, 44 percent said they had seen him do it, and 21 percent still insisted they had seen it when gently challenged. Many embellished their accounts with vivid, invented detail. Five- and six-year-olds were misled less often. Conclusion. Young children's free, unprompted recall was accurate, though sparse; it was the combination of prior expectation and repeated suggestive questioning that produced false reports, and false reports could be detailed and confidently held. Criticism. A two-minute visit and a torn book are far from the experience of abuse, and studies of genuinely stressful events find children often more resistant to suggestion. The study does not show that children's accounts of abuse are false; it shows how interviewers can make them unreliable.

Other experiments point the same way. In Ceci's "mousetrap" studies, preschool children were asked once a week for ten weeks to "think very hard" about whether certain events had happened to them, some real and some invented (one was getting a finger caught in a mousetrap and going to hospital); in one study 58 percent eventually described at least one invented event as real, often in convincing detail. In a study by Bruck, Ceci and their colleagues (1995), three-year-old children were asked to show, with an anatomically detailed doll, where a doctor had touched them during a routine medical check-up; many who had had a genital examination did not show it, and some who had not showed the doctor touching or inserting fingers into the doll's genitals. Anatomical dolls are therefore not a reliable way to establish what happened to a young child.

What does good interviewing look like? The best-researched answer is the investigative interview protocol developed by Michael Lamb, Yael Orbach and their colleagues at the United States National Institute of Child Health and Human Development, known as the NICHD protocol. It builds rapport, explains ground rules (it is fine to say "I don't know" or "I don't understand", and to correct the interviewer), and has the child practise giving a narrative about a neutral event such as a festival or a birthday. It then moves to the topic with an open prompt ("Tell me why you came to talk to me today") and relies as far as possible on open-ended invitations. Figure 10.9 ranks the types of question. The more open the question, the more accurate the answer; the more the question contains its own answer, the more it risks shaping what the child says. Good practice also means interviewing the child as few times as possible, recording the interview (the POCSO Act asks for audio-video recording wherever possible), and keeping the interviewer free of assumptions about what happened. Section 2.2 defined the leading question; this is where it does the most damage.

Exam trap

Children are most suggestible in the preschool years, and suggestibility is produced by the interviewer: leading questions, repeated interviews, stereotypes planted beforehand, praise for "disclosing" and anatomical dolls. A child's free recall is the most accurate part of the account. The Sam Stone study is Leichtman and Ceci (1995); the classic review is Ceci and Bruck (1993). Summit's accommodation syndrome explains delayed disclosure and retraction but cannot prove that abuse occurred. In the hierarchy of questions, open-ended invitations come first and suggestive questions never.

The POCSO Act 2012

Before 2012, Indian law on the sexual abuse of children was scattered and incomplete. The Indian Penal Code's offence of rape protected only women and girls, sexual abuse of boys had to be squeezed into the offence of "unnatural offences", and much non-penetrative abuse was covered only by the vague offence of insulting or outraging a woman's modesty. The trial process, in which a child might have to describe the abuse repeatedly, in open court, in front of the accused, under hostile cross-examination, was itself a second trauma. The Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act 2012 (POCSO) was passed to remedy both problems. It received the President's assent on 19 June 2012 and came into force on 14 November 2012, India's Children's Day.

The Act's foundations are simple to state. A "child" is any person below the age of eighteen years (section 2(1)(d)). The Act is gender-neutral: both the child and the offender may be of any gender. It defines five groups of offences, each with its punishment: penetrative sexual assault (sections 3 and 4); aggravated penetrative sexual assault (sections 5 and 6); sexual assault, meaning sexual touching without penetration (sections 7 and 8); aggravated sexual assault (sections 9 and 10); and sexual harassment, which includes sexual remarks, gestures, showing pornography and stalking a child, including online (sections 11 and 12). Using a child for pornographic purposes, and storing such material, are offences under sections 13 to 15. An offence is "aggravated" when the offender is in a position of trust or authority (a police officer, a member of the armed forces, a public servant, a member of the staff of a school, hospital, religious or other institution, or a relative or member of the household), when it is committed by a gang, with a weapon, repeatedly, or causes serious injury, or when the child is below twelve years or has a mental or physical disability that the offender takes advantage of.

For the clinician, the most important part of the Act is Chapter V, on reporting. Under section 19(1), any person (including the child) who has an apprehension that an offence under the Act is likely to be committed, or has knowledge that one has been committed, shall provide that information to the Special Juvenile Police Unit or to the local police. This is mandatory reporting, and it is universal: there is no exception for doctors, psychologists or counsellors, and no exception because the information was given in confidence or because the family does not want the matter reported. The police must record the report in writing and read it back to the informant, and within 24 hours must report the matter to the Child Welfare Committee and the Special Court (section 19(6)). A person who reports in good faith incurs no civil or criminal liability (section 19(7)). Under section 20, personnel of the media, hotels, lodges, hospitals, clubs, studios and photographic facilities who come across sexually exploitative material involving a child must report it.

The penalty for silence is in section 21. Any person who fails to report an offence as required by section 19(1) or section 20, or who (as a police officer) fails to record it, can be punished with imprisonment of up to six months, or a fine, or both (section 21(1)). A person in charge of a company or institution who fails to report an offence committed by a subordinate under his or her control can be punished with imprisonment of up to one year and a fine (section 21(2)): a principal who quietly transfers a teacher accused of abuse, instead of reporting him, commits this offence. A child is never punished for failing to report (section 21(3)). To protect against misuse, section 22 punishes a false complaint made solely to humiliate, extort, threaten or defame, but a child who makes a false complaint is not punished.

The rest of the Act makes the legal process gentler for the child. The child's statement is to be recorded at the child's home or a place of the child's choice, as far as practicable by a woman police officer not below the rank of sub-inspector, who is not in uniform; the child must never come into contact with the accused and must not be kept in a police station at night (section 24). The statement is recorded in the presence of a parent or another person the child trusts, with an interpreter or a special educator if needed, and wherever possible by audio-video means (section 26). A girl is medically examined by a woman doctor (section 27). Each district has a designated Special Court (section 28), which presumes that the accused committed the offence unless the contrary is proved (section 29). In court, questions are put to the child through the judge, frequent breaks are allowed, the child is not called repeatedly, aggressive questioning and character assassination are forbidden, and the child's identity is never disclosed (section 33). The child's evidence must be recorded within 30 days of the Special Court taking cognisance of the offence, and the trial completed as far as possible within one year (section 35). The child must not be exposed to the accused while testifying, through screens, one-way mirrors or video links (section 36), and trials are held in camera, closed to the public (section 37). Section 39 asks the states to frame guidelines for professionals with knowledge of psychology, social work, mental health and child development to assist the child before and during the trial, which is where clinical psychologists enter the process as supporters.

The Act was amended in 2019 (in force from 16 August 2019) to increase punishments: the minimum sentence for penetrative sexual assault was raised from seven to ten years, and to twenty years if the child is under sixteen; aggravated penetrative sexual assault now carries a minimum of twenty years, which may extend to imprisonment for the rest of the offender's natural life, or death; and child pornography was defined and the offence of storing such material was widened. Table 10.15 summarises the sections a clinician should know.

Table 10.15 The POCSO Act 2012: the provisions a clinician must know.
SectionProvisionWhat it means in practice
2(1)(d)A child is any person below 18A 17-year-old client is a child under the Act
3 to 15Penetrative and non-penetrative sexual assault, aggravated forms, sexual harassment, pornographyGender-neutral; aggravated when by a person in trust or authority, or the child is under 12 or disabled
19Anyone who knows of or apprehends an offence must inform the Special Juvenile Police Unit or local policeNo exception for confidentiality; police inform the Child Welfare Committee within 24 hours; good-faith reporting is protected
20Media, hotel, hospital, studio and similar personnel must report exploitative materialCovers staff who come across such images
21Failure to report: up to 6 months or fine or both; person in charge of an institution: up to 1 year and fineNot reporting is itself an offence; children are exempt
24, 26, 27Statement at home by a woman officer not in uniform; trusted person present; audio-video recording; woman doctor for a girlThe child should tell the story as few times as possible
28, 29, 33, 35 to 37Special Courts; presumption of guilt; questions through the judge; evidence within 30 days, trial within a year; no sight of the accused; in cameraPrepare and accompany the child; explain what court will be like
2019 amendmentHigher minimum sentences; death penalty for aggravated penetrative sexual assault; child pornography defined, storage offence widenedIn force from 16 August 2019

Two real dilemmas follow from the Act's wording. First, because every person under eighteen is a child and the Act contains no exception for sexual activity between adolescents of similar age, a clinician who learns that a sixteen- or seventeen-year-old client is in a consensual sexual relationship is, on a plain reading, bound to report it, and doctors treating pregnant adolescents face the same duty. Courts and commentators have expressed concern that this deters adolescents from seeking help, and the question is unsettled; until the law changes, the clinician must know the duty, explain it to adolescent clients in advance, and seek supervision and legal advice in difficult cases. Second, families sometimes ask the clinician to help them "settle the matter" within the family or community. The Act does not allow this, and a clinician who goes along with it commits an offence under section 21.

Exam trap

POCSO: Act of 2012, in force 14 November 2012; child = under 18; gender-neutral for victim and accused. Section 19 imposes the duty to report on any person, to the Special Juvenile Police Unit or local police; section 21 punishes failure to report (up to six months, or fine, or both; up to one year and fine for the person in charge of an institution); a child is exempt. Statement by a woman police officer not below sub-inspector, not in uniform; girl examined by a woman doctor; Special Courts; evidence within 30 days, trial within one year; trial in camera. The 2019 amendment added the death penalty for aggravated penetrative sexual assault. Section 20 is the duty of media and similar establishments, not the general duty. Do not confuse POCSO with the Juvenile Justice Act 2015, which deals with children in need of care and protection and children in conflict with the law (Section 10.9).

Helping abused children and their families

The first priority after abuse is the child's safety: medical care, protection from further contact with the abuser and, where the family cannot protect the child, the Child Welfare Committee constituted under the Juvenile Justice Act (Section 10.9). Only then does treatment begin, and not every abused child needs formal therapy. Many do well with support, monitoring and a parent who believes them. For children with post-traumatic stress symptoms, depression or behavioural problems, the first-line treatment is trauma-focused cognitive-behavioural therapy (TF-CBT), developed by Judith Cohen, Anthony Mannarino and Esther Deblinger and first tested with sexually abused children. Section 10.3 described its components and its evidence; here, note that it includes a non-offending parent in every phase, because how that parent responds to the disclosure is one of the strongest influences on the child's recovery. A mother who believes her daughter, protects her and stays calm does more for her than any therapist; a mother who doubts her, or blames her for "bringing shame on the family", can undo months of treatment. Work with the non-offending parent's own shock, guilt and divided loyalties (when the abuser is her husband or brother) is therefore part of the treatment, not an extra.

David Finkelhor and Angela Browne (1985) offered a useful map of what sexual abuse does, which tells the therapist what needs repairing. They described four traumagenic dynamics: traumatic sexualisation (the child's sexuality is shaped in developmentally inappropriate and confusing ways), betrayal (the discovery that someone trusted caused harm, or that a parent failed to protect), stigmatisation (shame, guilt and the sense of being "spoiled", which in India is often reinforced by talk of family honour) and powerlessness (the repeated experience of having one's will and body overridden). A twelve-year-old who now distrusts every adult needs help with betrayal; one who believes she is "dirty" needs help with stigmatisation.

For physical abuse and neglect, the focus moves to the parents. Behavioural parent training (Sections 4.10 and 10.3) teaches alternatives to harsh punishment, and adaptations of parent-child interaction therapy for physically abusive parents have reduced repeat abuse: in a trial by Mark Chaffin and his colleagues (2004), about one in five parents who received PCIT were re-reported for physical abuse over the following years, against about one in two who received a standard community parenting group. Treatment for neglect usually has to address the parent's own depression, substance use or isolation, and the family's practical needs, at the same time.

Prevention is better still. Home-visiting programmes, in which nurses visit young, poor, first-time mothers from pregnancy to the child's second birthday, have been tested in randomised trials by David Olds and his colleagues in the United States, beginning in Elmira, New York, in the late 1970s; the 15-year follow-up found fewer verified reports of child abuse and neglect in the visited families. School programmes on personal safety (often called "good touch, bad touch" in India) teach children the correct names for their body parts, that they may say no to unwanted touch even from adults they know, that secrets about touching should not be kept, and whom to tell. They increase children's knowledge and their willingness to tell, though whether they prevent abuse itself is harder to show. Working with abuse also takes a toll on the professional, and the vicarious traumatisation described in Chapter 8 and the self-care it calls for apply with special force here.

Working with adolescents

Adolescence was long described as a time of inevitable turmoil. The American psychologist G. Stanley Hall, in his two-volume Adolescence (1904), called it a period of "storm and stress", and psychoanalysts such as Anna Freud and Peter Blos argued that healthy development required the adolescent to detach emotionally from the parents. Research since the 1970s has revised both ideas. Most adolescents get on reasonably well with their parents, and serious turmoil is a sign that something is wrong, not a normal stage to be waited out. Autonomy develops best not by cutting ties but within a continuing, warm relationship in which parents gradually hand over responsibility; continued interdependence with parents, far from being immature, is healthy. Nor is detachment simply moved earlier, into middle childhood, or handed over to friends: peers do matter more in adolescence, above all in everyday matters such as dress, music and leisure, but they add to the parents' influence rather than replacing it, and parents remain the main influence on values and on long-term plans such as education and career. Families also differ in what they hope for from their adolescents, which is one more reason to ask rather than assume. Erik Erikson's account of identity versus role confusion, and James Marcia's identity statuses, are taught in the Developmental book.

Why do adolescents take more risks than children or adults, driving fast, experimenting with alcohol and drugs, having unprotected sex? The developmental psychologist Laurence Steinberg has proposed a dual systems model: a socioemotional, reward-seeking brain system becomes much more active around puberty, while the cognitive control system that restrains impulses matures slowly, into the early or middle twenties. In between lies a period of heightened sensitivity to reward, especially in the presence of peers. In one well-known experiment (Margo Gardner and Steinberg, 2005), adolescents playing a video driving game took more risks when friends were watching, while adults did not. Adolescents are not ignorant of risk; they know the facts about smoking or drink-driving as well as adults do. That is why lectures and scare tactics achieve little, and why approaches that build autonomy, competence and good relationships work better.

Engaging an adolescent is a skill of its own. Adolescents rarely refer themselves. They are brought by parents or sent by schools, often for problems the adult has defined ("he is always on his phone"; "she is rude"), and they may arrive suspicious that the psychologist is an agent of the parents. Experienced clinicians do several things.

  • They explain confidentiality and its limits clearly at the start, to the adolescent and the parents together (see the next subsection), and they see the adolescent alone for at least part of every session.
  • They are genuine, curious and non-judgemental, neither another parent nor a pretend friend who uses the adolescent's slang, and they show respect by asking the adolescent's view of the problem before anyone else's.
  • They negotiate goals the adolescent actually wants ("getting my parents off my back" is a legitimate starting point), and they use motivational interviewing (Section 6.9) to work with ambivalence instead of arguing with it.
  • They start with safe topics and interests (music, cricket, games, friends) before the difficult ones, and they offer choices and flexible formats: walking while talking, drawing, writing, or short check-ins between sessions where the setting allows.
  • They involve parents in ways the adolescent agrees to, usually by sharing general progress and coaching the parents, rather than reporting what the adolescent has said.

A structured way to cover the ground is the HEADSS assessment, a psychosocial interview for adolescents set out by John M. Goldenring and Eric Cohen in 1988: Home, Education and employment, Activities (and peers), Drugs, Sexuality and Suicide and depression. An expanded version, HEEADSSS (Goldenring and David Rosen, 2004), adds Eating and Safety (including violence, bullying and online risks). The order is deliberate: it moves from the least to the most sensitive topics, so that trust is built before the hardest questions are asked.

Pooja, a few weeks after the report to the police, illustrates how these skills come together. She is angry with the counsellor for "telling" and refuses to talk. The counsellor does not defend herself; she acknowledges that it felt like a betrayal, explains again why she had to act, and asks what Pooja would like from their meetings now. Pooja wants two things: to stop the nightmares and to make her father let her continue her studies. The counsellor agrees to both goals, begins TF-CBT's skills phase for the nightmares, and holds a joint session in which Pooja, not the counsellor, tells her parents what she needs from them.

Consent and confidentiality with minors

Who decides about the treatment of a child or an adolescent? In Indian law a person attains majority at eighteen (the Indian Majority Act 1875), and the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 defines a minor as a person who has not completed eighteen years (section 2(t)). For a minor, the legal guardian acts as the nominated representative (section 15), and a minor may be admitted to a mental health establishment only on the nominated representative's application, after two independent examinations (by two psychiatrists, or a psychiatrist together with a mental health professional or medical practitioner) find that admission is in the minor's best interests and that community alternatives have failed or are unsuitable (section 87). The guardian or an attendant must stay with the minor throughout, a female attendant must stay with a minor girl if the nominated representative is male, treatment is given with the informed consent of the nominated representative, and the Mental Health Review Board must be informed within 72 hours (Chapter 1 teaches the Act; electroconvulsive therapy for minors is prohibited except with the guardian's consent and the Board's prior permission). In outpatient work the same principle applies: the parent or guardian gives legal consent and the child gives assent, a principle introduced in Sections 1.8 and 10.1.

Confidentiality is harder. Legally, parents may be entitled to know what happens in their child's treatment; clinically, an adolescent who believes everything will be repeated to his parents will tell the therapist nothing that matters. The usual solution is an explicit agreement made at the first meeting, with the adolescent and the parents present. What the adolescent says will be kept private; the parents will be told about general progress and about anything the adolescent agrees to share; and there are exceptions, which are named in advance. The exceptions are serious risk to the adolescent's own life or safety (a suicidal plan, for example), serious risk to others, abuse (which the POCSO Act and child-protection duties require to be reported), and orders of a court. The clinician also promises that, if an exception arises, he or she will tell the adolescent first and involve the adolescent in how the information is shared. Between the clear cases lie grey ones: occasional drinking, a first cigarette, superficial self-harm without suicidal intent, a romantic relationship. Here clinicians weigh the seriousness and immediacy of the risk, the adolescent's maturity and the likely effect of disclosure, and they consult a supervisor. Indian parents, often used to being told everything by teachers and doctors, may need the reasons for this arrangement explained with care: a private space is what allows their child to talk.

Exam trap

In India the age of majority and of consent to treatment is 18; below it the parent or guardian consents and the child assents. Under the MHCA 2017 the legal guardian is a minor's nominated representative (section 15) and minors are admitted only under section 87. Confidentiality with adolescents is confidentiality with stated exceptions (serious risk to self or others, abuse, court orders), agreed at the outset, with a promise to tell the adolescent before disclosing. Mandatory reporting under POCSO overrides any promise of secrecy. The view that healthy adolescence requires detachment from parents (Anna Freud, Blos) has given way to the finding that continued interdependence is healthy (RML 2022).

LGBTQ adolescents and affirmative therapy

A seventeen-year-old boy is brought by his parents after they found messages between him and another boy on his phone. They want him "treated" before his cousins find out. He sits silently, looking at the floor. What should the psychologist do? Section 4.4 told the history of attempts to "cure" homosexuality by aversion and the reasons the profession now condemns them: homosexuality is not a disorder (it was removed from DSM in 1973 and from the ICD with ICD-10), change efforts do not work, and they cause harm. In India, the Supreme Court decriminalised consensual same-sex relations between adults in Navtej Singh Johar v. Union of India (2018), recognised transgender people as a third gender in National Legal Services Authority v. Union of India (2014), and Parliament passed the Transgender Persons (Protection of Rights) Act 2019. In 2021 the Madras High Court, in S. Sushma v. Commissioner of Police, directed action against conversion therapy, and in 2022 the National Medical Commission declared conversion therapy to be professional misconduct. Any attempt to change a person's sexual orientation or gender identity is called conversion therapy (also reparative therapy, or sexual orientation change efforts), and offering it is unethical. So the psychologist will not try to change the boy's orientation. The question is what to offer instead.

The answer starts with understanding why LGBTQ adolescents have higher rates of depression, anxiety, substance use and suicide attempts than their peers. The minority stress model, set out by the American public-health researcher Ilan H. Meyer in the Psychological Bulletin in 2003, explains it as the result not of sexual orientation itself but of the chronic stress of belonging to a stigmatised minority. Meyer distinguished distal stressors, external events of prejudice, discrimination, rejection and violence, from proximal stressors, which work from inside: the expectation of rejection and constant vigilance against it, the strain of concealing one's identity, and internalised homophobia, the turning of society's negative attitudes against oneself. Family reactions matter enormously. Caitlin Ryan and her colleagues (2009) found that young lesbian, gay and bisexual adults who had experienced high levels of family rejection as adolescents were several times more likely to have attempted suicide and to report depression than those whose families had been accepting.

What the psychologist offers instead is affirmative therapy (LGBTQ-affirmative or gay-affirmative therapy). It is not a separate school of therapy but a stance that can be taken within any approach, CBT, person-centred or family therapy. It treats lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, queer and other sexual and gender-minority identities as normal and valid variations, and it directs therapy at the stresses the client faces rather than at the identity itself. In practice it involves the following.

  • The therapist examines his or her own attitudes and assumptions first, and learns about the client's communities.
  • The therapist does not presume heterosexuality, uses inclusive language ("Is there someone you are close to?") and the name and pronouns the client prefers, and does not treat the client's identity as the cause of every problem.
  • Therapy addresses minority stress directly: internalised shame, fear of discovery, bullying at school, family conflict and isolation.
  • Coming out is supported at the client's own pace and with attention to safety. An adolescent who depends on his family for food, shelter and education may reasonably decide not to come out yet, and the therapist respects that judgement.
  • Families are helped to move from rejection towards acceptance, which, as Ryan's research shows, is protective; parents' grief and fears ("Who will marry him? What will people say?") are heard and answered with accurate information.
  • The client is connected with supportive peers, groups and resources.

For the seventeen-year-old, this means seeing him alone, telling him plainly that nothing is wrong with him and that the psychologist will not try to change who he is, asking what he wants, and screening for depression and suicidal thoughts. With his agreement, it means meeting the parents to hear their fears and to give them the facts: that his orientation is not an illness, that attempts to change it fail and harm, and that their acceptance is the strongest protection he has. For gender-diverse adolescents, psychosocial support follows the same principles, while decisions about medical treatment for minors are complex, remain debated internationally, and require careful specialist assessment.

Exam trap

"Affirmative therapy is specifically designed to support..." LGBTQ+ individuals (NIMHANS mock), not elderly clients with cognitive decline (validation therapy and cognitive stimulation, Section 10.7), anxious clients or children with developmental delays. Do not confuse it with affirmations, the A of OARS in motivational interviewing (Section 6.9), or with the affirmative sentences of Carol Gray's social stories (Section 10.5). The minority stress model is Ilan Meyer's (2003): distal stressors (prejudice events) and proximal stressors (expected rejection, concealment, internalised homophobia). Conversion therapy is unethical everywhere and, in India, professional misconduct for doctors (National Medical Commission, 2022).

Scan Your Brain

  2. TEACCH, the structured-teaching programme founded at the University of North Carolina in 1971, is associated with Eric .
  3. The Picture Exchange Communication System was devised in 1985 by Andrew Bondy and Lori , and it is taught in six phases ending with commenting.
  4. Social stories, short individualised stories describing social situations, were devised in 1991 by Carol .
  5. Controlled tests in 1993 showed that the messages typed in communication were authored by the helpers, not by the people with autism.
  6. The National Trust Act 1999 covers autism, cerebral palsy, intellectual disability and disabilities.
