10.7 Working with Older AdultsLower yield · not asked yet

At the end of 1904 Sigmund Freud gave a lecture in Vienna, published the next year as "On Psychotherapy", in which he explained which patients psychoanalysis could not help. Age was one of his limits. "Near or above the fifties," he said, "the elasticity of the mental processes, on which the treatment depends, is as a rule lacking; old people are no longer educable." Freud was 48 when he said it, and he went on writing and treating patients until he died at 83. For half a century his pessimism set the tone: older people were thought too rigid to change, too close to death to be worth the investment, and their sadness and forgetfulness were put down to "just old age". Every part of that view has turned out to be wrong. Older adults benefit from psychological treatment about as much as younger adults do, and there are now far more of them. According to the India Ageing Report 2023 of the United Nations Population Fund and the International Institute for Population Sciences, India had about 149 million people aged 60 and over in 2022, 10.5 percent of the population, and the share is projected to double to 20.8 percent (about 347 million people) by 2050; by about 2046 the country will have more older people than children under 15. This section is about what a psychologist does for them: the field of geropsychology, dementia care and support for the family, the psychosocial therapies built for confused older people, late-life depression, and the assessment of capacity and the problem of elder abuse.

Geropsychology: the field and the older client

Three words are easily confused. The first, gerontology, is the scientific study of ageing in all its aspects, biological, psychological and social; the word was coined in 1903 by the Russian-born biologist Élie Metchnikoff (1845 to 1916). The second, geriatrics, is the branch of medicine concerned with the health care of older people; the term was introduced in 1909 by the Vienna-born American physician Ignatz Leo Nascher (1863 to 1944). The third, geropsychology, is the application of psychology to older adults: understanding them, assessing them and helping them and their families. Professional geropsychology took shape as a specialty only recently. In 2006 a national conference held in Colorado Springs set out the competencies a psychologist needs for work with older adults, published in 2009 by Bob Knight and colleagues as the Pikes Peak model of geropsychology training, and in 2010 the American Psychological Association recognised professional geropsychology as a specialty in professional psychology.

Who counts as old? In Indian law a senior citizen is anyone aged 60 or more (you will meet the 2007 Act that says so in Section 10.7.6), but a sixty-year-old schoolteacher and a ninety-year-old widow confined to bed have little in common. The American psychologist Bernice Neugarten (1916 to 2001) drew a distinction in the 1970s between the young-old, active, healthy and often still working, and the old-old, among whom frailty and dependence are common; later writers added the oldest-old, usually those over 85. A practical rule follows: older adults differ from one another more than any younger age group does, so the label "elderly" tells you very little about the person in front of you.

What gets in the way of good care is often not the client's age but the clinician's attitudes. In 1969 the American psychiatrist Robert Neil Butler (1927 to 2010) coined the word ageism for the systematic stereotyping of, and discrimination against, people because they are old, a prejudice he compared to racism and sexism. Butler went on to win the Pulitzer Prize in 1976 for Why Survive? Being Old in America (1975) and to serve as the founding director of the American National Institute on Aging (1975 to 1982). Ageism in the clinic has a recognisable sound: "What do you expect at your age?", the depressed seventy-five-year-old who is told that low mood is natural after retirement, the condescending, sing-song "elderspeak" that treats an older person like a child (Section 2.2), and the quiet assumption, Freud's assumption, that psychotherapy is wasted on the old. The result is therapeutic nihilism: treatable conditions such as depression, anxiety, alcohol misuse and the confusion caused by medicines go untreated because nobody expected anything better.

The clinician therefore has to keep normal ageing and disorder apart. Normal ageing brings slower processing, some difficulty in learning and recalling new information quickly, and poorer performance on timed tasks, while vocabulary, general knowledge and well-practised skills hold up well (the developmental changes themselves are taught in the Developmental Psychology book). Dementia is not normal ageing: it is a progressive disorder that erodes memory, reasoning and the ability to manage daily life, and its types and criteria belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book, as screening tests such as the Mini-Mental State Examination belong to the Assessment book. The trickiest boundary is the one you met in Section 2.6: depression in an older person can produce poor concentration and complaints of forgetfulness that look like dementia, the picture called pseudodementia, and the two conditions also occur together.

How is the work different? Much less than Freud supposed, and in predictable ways. The American geropsychologist Bob G. Knight, whose Psychotherapy with Older Adults first appeared in 1986, argued that older clients are not a different species: they are more mature in some respects, they belong to a cohort that grew up with different values and expectations (many of today's older Indians grew up in joint families, with arranged marriages and little talk of feelings), and they face specific challenges such as chronic illness, disability and the death of a spouse and friends. Therapy is adapted to those facts rather than to "age" as such. In practice that means attending to hearing and vision (Section 2.2), a slower pace and sometimes shorter sessions, written summaries and memory aids, close liaison with the physician because most older clients have several illnesses and take several medicines (polypharmacy), and, with the client's consent, involving the family. The settings are varied too: memory clinics, geriatric wards, primary care, day-care centres, old age homes and, very often, the client's own home. Erik Erikson's last psychosocial stage, integrity versus despair, gives a useful frame for this work and is taught in the Developmental Psychology book; the rest of this section turns to the clinical tasks themselves.

Exam trap

Gerontology (Metchnikoff, 1903) is the scientific study of ageing; geriatrics (Nascher, 1909) is the medical care of older people; geropsychology is psychology applied to older adults. Ageism was coined by Robert Butler in 1969, not by Erikson, who gave us integrity versus despair. Depression, anxiety and confusion are not normal parts of ageing; an option such as "depression is an expected consequence of old age" is a distractor. In India a senior citizen is a person of 60 or more.

Dementia care and the family caregiver

No psychological treatment can halt the brain changes of Alzheimer's disease or the other dementias, and the drugs available slow the decline only modestly. So what is a psychologist for? David Barlow and Mark Durand, in their textbook of abnormal psychology, put the goals of care plainly: to prevent what can be prevented (strokes, alcohol damage), to delay the onset of the worst symptoms so that the person has more years of good life, and to help the person and the family cope with the decline that remains. The last of these is where most psychological work lies, and it is aimed as much at the family as at the patient. In India, as in most low- and middle-income countries, almost all dementia care is given at home by relatives, most of them women: wives, daughters and daughters-in-law, many of them unpaid, untrained and unsupported.

The cost of that care to the carer has a name. In 1966 the psychiatric researchers Hoenig and Hamilton, studying the families of psychiatric patients living at home, distinguished two kinds of caregiver burden. What they called objective burden is the observable disruption that caring causes: lost income and working hours, money spent, broken sleep, abandoned social life, a household reorganised around the patient. By contrast, subjective burden is how much the carer feels burdened. The two are only loosely related, and it is subjective burden that is most closely linked to the carer's own depression.

The best-known measure of burden comes from Steven H. Zarit and his colleagues Reever and Bach-Peterson, who in 1980 published "Relatives of the impaired elderly: correlates of feelings of burden" in The Gerontologist. They interviewed the main carers of older people with dementia and built a burden interview asking how far caring had affected the carer's health, finances, social life and relationship with the patient. Their most quoted finding was a surprise: the severity of the patient's memory loss and behaviour problems was not closely related to how burdened the carer felt, whereas the support the carer received, in particular how often other family members visited, was. Burden, in other words, depends as much on the carer's circumstances as on the illness, which is good news, because circumstances can be changed. A later 22-item version, the Zarit Burden Interview, is now the standard measure of caregiver burden worldwide, including in Indian research.

A broader account came in 1990 from the sociologist Leonard Pearlin and his colleagues, whose stress process model of caregiving separates the primary stressors that come directly from the illness (the patient's care needs, memory loss, behaviour problems), the secondary stressors that spread from them (conflict with siblings over who should help, strain at work, the carer's sense of being trapped in the role and of losing herself), the mediators that soften the impact (coping skills and social support) and the outcomes (depression, anxiety, poorer physical health). The outcomes are real. Carers of people with dementia have high rates of depression and anxiety, and you saw in Section 9.3 that women caring for a relative with dementia healed a small standard wound about nine days more slowly than matched controls. The American caregivers' handbook by Nancy Mace and Peter Rabins, first published in 1981, captured the experience in its title: The 36-Hour Day.

Caregiver interventions follow directly from the stress process model. First, psychoeducation gives the family information about the illness, its likely course, what the behaviour means and what to expect next, and about legal, financial and practical matters; early in the illness, families need little else. Skills training teaches carers to manage behaviour problems and to communicate with a person whose memory is failing (Section 10.7.4). Counselling and cognitive-behavioural methods address the carer's own guilt, grief, anger and depression; dementia is a "living loss" whose grief begins long before death (the anticipatory and disenfranchised grief of Section 9.5). Support groups reduce isolation, and respite care (day-care centres, a paid attendant for some hours a week, short residential stays) gives the carer time off. Reviews find that single components help a little and multicomponent programmes, tailored to the family, help most.

Key study: counselling the spouse keeps the patient at home (Mittelman and colleagues, 2006)

Question: Can supporting the carer change what happens to the patient? Method: At New York University, Mary S. Mittelman and her colleagues randomly assigned spouses caring for a husband or wife with Alzheimer's disease either to usual care or to an enhanced programme: individual and family counselling sessions in the first months, a weekly support group, and counselling by telephone whenever a crisis arose, for as long as the carer needed it. They followed the couples for years and recorded when the patients entered nursing homes. Result: Patients whose spouses received the programme had a 28 percent lower rate of nursing-home placement; the difference in predicted median time to placement was 557 days, about a year and a half more at home. Improvements in the carers' satisfaction with social support, in their reactions to the patients' behaviour problems and in their depression accounted for about 61 percent of the effect. Conclusion: Helping the carer helps the patient. Criticism: The sample was of spouse carers in New York, a population with resources few Indian families have, which is why the Goa trial in the next box matters.

Indian context

In focus groups in Goa, Vikram Patel and Martin Prince (2001) found that dementia was widely regarded as a normal part of ageing, not as an illness needing care, and that both dementia and late-life depression were blamed on neglect by the children; they also found family care less reliable than the image of the devoted Indian joint family suggests, and sometimes conditional on inheriting the parent's property. Building on this, Amit Dias, Vikram Patel and their colleagues in the 10/66 Dementia Research Group ran a randomised trial in two talukas of Goa (published in 2008). Local community workers called home care advisors, supervised by a counsellor and a psychiatrist, visited families at home and gave them information about dementia, guidance on managing behaviour problems and, where needed, a psychiatric assessment and medication. Among 81 families, the programme significantly improved the carers' mental health and reduced their distress over the patient's behaviour; the fall in burden scores was smaller and not statistically significant. The lesson echoes the lay counsellor programmes of Section 9.6: low-cost, locally available people can deliver real help.

One more idea changed how dementia care is thought about. The British social psychologist Tom Kitwood (1937 to 1998), in Dementia Reconsidered: The Person Comes First (1997), argued that much of the decline seen in people with dementia comes not from the brain disease alone but from how other people treat them, which he called malignant social psychology: talking over their heads, infantilising them, labelling them, ignoring their wishes, hurrying them, outpacing them. Its antidote is person-centred dementia care, which aims to preserve the person's personhood, their standing as a person with a history, preferences and relationships, through recognition, respect and inclusion. With Kathleen Bredin, Kitwood developed dementia care mapping, a structured observation method in which a trained observer records, minute by minute, what happens to residents in a care setting and how it affects their well-being. Person-centred care is now the stated philosophy of dementia services in many countries.

Exam trap

The Zarit Burden Interview (Zarit, Reever and Bach-Peterson, 1980) measures the caregiver's burden; it is not a measure of the patient's dementia. Objective burden is the observable disruption (money, time, health, routine); subjective burden is the felt strain, and it is the one most closely linked to carer depression. Zarit's surprising finding was that burden was tied less to the severity of the patient's impairment than to the support the carer received. Person-centred dementia care and malignant social psychology are Kitwood's; do not attribute them to Rogers, though the spirit is similar.

Reality orientation, reminiscence, validation and cognitive stimulation

Imagine an 81-year-old woman, Mrs Venkatesan, who lives with her son and daughter-in-law in Mysuru and has had Alzheimer's disease for five years. Every evening at about five o'clock she packs a cloth bag and goes to the front door, insisting that she must go home because her mother is waiting with dinner. Her mother died more than forty years ago. What should her daughter-in-law say? The four psychosocial approaches in this subsection give four different answers, and the difference between them lies in one question: whose reality should the helper work in? Figure 10.10 arranges them on that dimension, and Table 10.16 compares them.

Reality orientation. The oldest of the four began in 1958 as a pilot rehabilitation programme on one ward of the Winter Veterans Administration Hospital in Topeka, Kansas, led by the American psychiatrist James C. Folsom. Its first patients were severely disturbed war veterans, not people with dementia; only later was it applied to confused older patients, and Taulbee and Folsom described it for geriatric patients in 1966. (Some accounts date the start to 1959.) The method of reality orientation rests on the idea that confusion is made worse by a lack of information and that repeated presentation of the basic facts of time, place and person will help the person understand their surroundings and regain a sense of control. It has two forms. In 24-hour or informal reality orientation, every member of staff, at every contact, restates the facts ("Good morning, Mr Rao. It's Monday, and I'm Asha, your nurse. Breakfast is in ten minutes"), and the environment is filled with cues: large clocks and calendars, signs on doors, and the reality orientation board giving the day, date, weather and the next meal. In classroom reality orientation, small groups meet for a short daily session to rehearse orientation information. Reviews, including one by Aimee Spector and colleagues for the Cochrane Collaboration (2000), found some benefit for cognition and behaviour, but the approach drew heavy criticism: applied mechanically, it becomes a quiz the person keeps failing, and it can be cruel. Told every evening that her mother died forty years ago, Mrs Venkatesan would grieve afresh each time and become more agitated, not less.

Reminiscence and life review. Until the 1960s, an old person's habit of talking about the past was usually treated as a symptom: living in the past, a sign of failing memory or of senility. Butler turned that view on its head.

Key study: Butler and the life review (1963)

In "The life review: an interpretation of reminiscence in the aged", published in the journal Psychiatry in 1963, Robert Butler drew on his psychiatric work with older people to argue that reminiscence is not a sign of decline but a normal, indeed universal, developmental process. As people become aware that death is approaching, he proposed, past experiences return progressively to consciousness, and especially unresolved conflicts, which can then be surveyed, re-evaluated and integrated. He called this the life review. Its outcome can be good or bad. Successfully completed, it can bring candour, serenity, acceptance of one's life and something like wisdom; where the past is full of guilt and failure that cannot be reconciled, it can bring anxiety, despair and depression, and in extreme cases suicide. The paper's impact was to make reminiscence something to encourage and work with rather than to correct, and it launched the reminiscence and life review therapies. Its criticism is the usual one for clinical theorising: Butler's claim that the process is universal was based on clinical observation, and later research found that only some kinds of reminiscence are good for people (next paragraph).

Two therapies grew from Butler's idea, and examiners expect you to tell them apart. The first, reminiscence therapy, uses the recall of past events, usually pleasant ones, to give pleasure, prompt communication and build social contact and self-esteem. It is often done in groups and uses triggers: old photographs and wedding albums, film songs from the person's youth, household objects, smells and tastes, stories of festivals and of the places they grew up. Because memories from long ago survive in dementia far better than recent ones, reminiscence is one of the few activities in which a person with moderate dementia can be the expert, and a "life story book" put together with the family gives staff something to talk about with the person. The second, life review therapy, is more structured and more evaluative. Usually individual, it moves through the whole of life in order, from childhood to the present, and deliberately includes the painful parts, aiming at integration and acceptance; it is used mainly with older people who are depressed or facing death (dignity therapy, Section 9.5, is a close relative). Research by the Canadian psychologist Paul Wong and Lisa Watt (1991) showed why the distinction matters. Comparing older people who were ageing well with those who were not, they found that successful agers engaged more in integrative reminiscence (accepting the past and finding meaning in it) and instrumental reminiscence (recalling how past problems were solved), and less in obsessive reminiscence, the bitter, ruminative replaying of guilt and failure. Their other types (transmissive, passing on lessons to the young; narrative, simple storytelling; and escapist, glorifying the past to escape the present) were less clearly related to adjustment. The evidence for the therapies is respectable: a meta-analysis by Ernst Bohlmeijer, Filip Smit and Pim Cuijpers (2003) found a large effect of reminiscence and life review on depressive symptoms in older people (an effect size of 0.84), larger in people who were already depressed (1.23) than in others (0.37).

Validation therapy. Naomi Feil (1932 to 2023) grew up in the Montefiore Home for the Aged in Cleveland, Ohio, where her parents worked, and returned to work there as a social worker after training at Columbia University. Running groups for very old, severely disoriented residents, she found that reality orientation made them withdraw or become angry, and between 1963 and 1980 she developed an alternative, set out in Validation: The Feil Method (1982) and The Validation Breakthrough (1993). Her validation therapy starts from the opposite premise to reality orientation: the helper accepts the reality and personal truth of the confused person's experience instead of correcting it, on the assumption that behind the confused words and behaviour lies a real feeling, an unmet need or an unfinished task from earlier life. Building on Erikson, Feil argued that very old disoriented people are struggling to resolve unfinished business before death, in a final stage of life beyond Erikson's eight that she called the stage of resolution (the person who cannot resolve the past withdraws further and further inward, towards vegetation), and she described four stages of increasing disorientation (later renamed the four phases of resolution): malorientation (the person is oriented but expresses old conflicts in disguised ways, often blaming others), time confusion (losing track of time and of present reality), repetitive motion (repeated movements and sounds replace words) and vegetation (withdrawal into almost total inactivity). Her techniques are concrete: centring yourself before the contact; asking non-threatening factual questions (who, what, where, when, how) but never "why", which demands an insight the person no longer has; rephrasing the person's words; asking about the extreme ("What is the worst thing about it?"); reminiscing; close, genuine eye contact; a clear, low, warm tone of voice; mirroring the person's movements; linking the behaviour to an unmet need such as love, usefulness or safety; touch; and music. Faced with Mrs Venkatesan at the door, a validating daughter-in-law might say: "You want to go to your mother. Tell me about her. What would she cook for you in the evening?" Mrs Venkatesan talks about her mother's rasam and the house in Thanjavur, the urgency drains away, and after a few minutes she agrees to help set out the plates. Nobody has told her a lie, and nobody has told her a painful truth she will forget within the hour.

Validation is popular with staff, who report that it makes their work more satisfying, but its evidence is thin. A Cochrane review by Neal and Barton Wright (2003) found too few good trials to say whether it improves behaviour or mood in people with dementia. The ethical question it raises is still debated: when a person with dementia asks for a dead mother, is it ever right to say "She'll be here soon"? Validation itself does not require deception, only that the helper respond to the feeling rather than to the facts.

Cognitive stimulation therapy. The approach with the strongest evidence was built partly out of the wreckage of reality orientation. Aimee Spector and her colleagues in London reviewed the earlier research, kept what seemed to work, discarded the mechanical quizzing, and designed a group programme they called cognitive stimulation therapy (CST): enjoyable, themed activities that engage thinking, memory, language and concentration in a social setting, without demanding that the person get facts right.

Key study: Spector and the cognitive stimulation trial (2003)

Method: Aimee Spector, Martin Orrell, Bob Woods and their colleagues ran a single-blind, multi-centre randomised controlled trial with 201 older people with dementia living in care homes and attending day centres in Britain; 115 received CST and 86 continued with their usual activities. CST groups of about five to eight people met for 14 sessions of 45 minutes, twice a week for seven weeks. Each session had a theme (among them childhood, food, current affairs, faces and scenes, word games, using money and a team quiz), began with a warm-up and a group song, and followed principles such as asking for opinions rather than facts, using all the senses, and using reminiscence as an aid to the here and now. Result: Compared with the controls, the CST group improved significantly on the Mini-Mental State Examination, on the cognitive scale of the Alzheimer's Disease Assessment Scale (ADAS-Cog) and on a quality-of-life scale. Six people needed to be treated for one to gain four or more points on the ADAS-Cog. Conclusion: The benefits compared favourably with those of the anti-dementia drugs. Since then: In 2006 the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) in England recommended cognitive stimulation for people with mild to moderate dementia, and CST has been adapted for use in many countries, with longer-term (maintenance) and individual, carer-delivered versions.

Do not confuse cognitive stimulation with two neighbours. Cognitive training is guided practice on standard tasks aimed at a particular function, such as memory drills; cognitive rehabilitation sets individual goals in the person's everyday life and works towards them with compensatory aids and techniques such as errorless learning (Section 9.9). The distinction was drawn by Linda Clare and Bob Woods in 2004. Simple compensatory aids belong here too: the American speech scientist Michelle Bourgeois showed in 1992 that a "memory wallet" of cards carrying sentences and pictures ("My husband John and I have three children") helped people with Alzheimer's disease hold better conversations.

Table 10.16 Four psychosocial approaches for older people with dementia compared.
ApproachOriginWhat the helper doesEvidence
Reality orientationFolsom, Topeka, from 1958; Taulbee and Folsom 1966Repeats time, place and person; orientation boards and cues; 24-hour or classroom formSome benefit for cognition and behaviour; criticised as mechanical and distressing
Reminiscence therapyButler's life review, 1963Prompts pleasant memories with photographs, music and objects, often in groupsImproves mood and engagement; reduces depressive symptoms
Life review therapyButler, 1963Structured, usually individual, chronological and evaluative review of the whole lifeLarge effects on depressive symptoms (Bohlmeijer 2003)
Validation therapyNaomi Feil, 1963 to 1980Accepts the person's reality and responds to the feeling and need behind the wordsInsufficient trial evidence (Cochrane 2003); liked by staff
Cognitive stimulation therapySpector and colleagues, 200314 themed group sessions; opinions rather than factsStrongest: gains in cognition and quality of life; NICE-recommended
Exam trap

Reality orientation (Folsom, for confused and disoriented older people) is not reality therapy (William Glasser, 1965, Section 6.6), and neither is the psychoanalytic ego function of reality testing. Validation therapy is Naomi Feil's; it is not the validation strategies of Linehan's DBT (Section 5.8), although both mean accepting the person's experience as real. The life review is Robert Butler's (1963); reminiscence therapy is the looser, usually group, pleasant-memory form, while life review therapy is structured, individual and evaluative. Of the four approaches, cognitive stimulation therapy (Spector, 2003) has the best evidence.

Agitation, wandering and other behaviour that challenges

For most families the hardest part of dementia is not the memory loss but the behaviour: agitation and restlessness, shouting, aggression during bathing, wandering, sleeplessness and night-time confusion, suspiciousness and accusations of theft, apathy, and disinhibited sexual or social behaviour. Since a consensus conference of the International Psychogeriatric Association in 1996 these have been grouped as the behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD). Almost everyone with dementia shows some of them at some stage, and they are the commonest reason a family gives up home care.

The psychologist's first rule is that the behaviour has a cause, and the first causes to look for are physical. A person who cannot say "my tooth hurts", "I am constipated" or "I need the toilet" may show it by shouting or hitting out. Pain, infections (especially of the urinary tract), dehydration, side effects of medicines, a lost hearing aid or spectacles, and above all delirium, a sudden, fluctuating confusion caused by physical illness, must be ruled out before anything else. The second rule is that the behaviour usually serves a purpose. The gerontologist Jiska Cohen-Mansfield's unmet needs model treats much agitation as the person's attempt to meet a need (for comfort, company, activity, rest, the toilet) or to communicate it when words have gone. The tool for finding the need is the functional analysis you learned in Section 2.8: an ABC chart recording the antecedents (time of day, place, who was present, what was being asked), the behaviour itself, and its consequences (what happened next, what the person got or escaped).

Mrs Venkatesan's family kept an ABC chart for two weeks. The pattern was clear. The packing and pleading came between five and seven in the evening, after a long afternoon nap, in a dimly lit room with the television news turned up loud, while her daughter-in-law was busy in the kitchen; the consequence was usually an argument in which she was told the facts, after which she became tearful and sometimes slapped whoever stood in the doorway. This is the late-day worsening often called "sundowning". The plan that followed changed the antecedents and the consequences. Her afternoon nap was shortened, the lights went on at half past four and the television was turned down; at five her son took her for a walk on the terrace, and afterwards she was given the task of folding the dried clothes, a job she had done all her life, with a cup of tea and a snack. When she still asked for her mother, the family used the validating response from Section 10.7.3 and then gently redirected her. Within three weeks the evening episodes fell from almost every day to about twice a week, and the slapping stopped.

Most behavioural strategies fall into a few families. Environmental changes reduce noise and crowding, improve lighting in the evening, mark the toilet door with a picture, and make walking safe by giving the person a secure route, a garden or a terrace rather than locking them in a room. Routine and simplification keep the day predictable and break tasks into single steps with one instruction at a time ("Put your arm in here"), so that bathing and dressing do not become battles. Meaningful activity, music, exercise and contact prevent the boredom and loneliness behind much restlessness; in trials led by the American psychologist Linda Teri, exercise for the patient combined with training for the carer in managing behaviour problems improved both physical health and depression in people with Alzheimer's disease. Communication matters most: carers learn to approach from the front, speak calmly, avoid arguing and correcting, acknowledge the feeling, and distract and redirect. Barlow and Durand note that teaching communication skills in ways similar to programmes for people with autism, in which a person learns an acceptable way of signalling a need instead of a disruptive one (the functional communication training of Section 10.5), may help reduce aggression in dementia too. Wandering needs special care: tying a person to a chair or bed, or sedating them, adds injury, distress and loss of dignity to the risks it is meant to prevent, while identity bracelets, door alarms and electronic location devices protect safety, though tracking technology raises its own questions of privacy.

Medication is a last resort, not a first one. Antipsychotic drugs are often given for agitation in dementia, but their benefit is modest and their risks are serious: in 2005 the United States Food and Drug Administration warned that the newer antipsychotics increase the risk of death in older people with dementia-related psychosis, a warning later extended to the older drugs. Guidelines everywhere now recommend that non-drug approaches be tried first, which is precisely the psychologist's territory.

Exam trap

When a person with dementia suddenly becomes agitated or aggressive, the first step is to look for a physical cause, above all pain, infection, medication effects and delirium, not to start a behaviour programme or a sedative. The behavioural approach rests on an ABC functional analysis; restraint and sedation are not the preferred answers to wandering. "Sundowning" is the late-afternoon and evening worsening of confusion and agitation. BPSD is the umbrella term for these behavioural and psychological symptoms.

Late-life depression

Mr Dutta is 72, a retired railway engineer who lives alone in a flat in Kolkata. His wife died eighteen months ago; his only son works in Bengaluru and phones on Sundays. He goes to his doctor complaining of poor sleep, aches in his back and knees, no appetite and, most of all, his memory: "It is going. I forget why I came into a room." He no longer goes to the evening adda with his old colleagues, and when asked about it he says, "What is the use? My work is done." His doctor's first thought is that this is what happens when a man of 72 loses his wife. It is not.

Depression is not a normal part of ageing. Major depression is in fact no more common among healthy older people living in the community than among younger adults, but depressive symptoms are common among older people who are medically ill, disabled, bereaved, isolated or living in institutions, and they are under-recognised for three reasons. Older people are less likely to describe themselves as "depressed" and more likely to present with bodily complaints, sleep problems, fatigue, anxiety and worry about memory, as Mr Dutta does. Their symptoms overlap with those of physical illness. And clinicians, like Mr Dutta's doctor, too often take sadness in old age for granted. The memory complaint needs particular care: a depressed person usually complains loudly of memory loss, gives up easily on memory tests ("I don't know") and has a clearly datable onset, while a person with early dementia more often minimises problems that the family describes. This is the pseudodementia picture of Section 2.6, and the two conditions also occur together, since late-life depression can be an early sign of dementia. The stakes are high. In many countries the highest suicide rates are found among older men, especially those who are widowed, living alone, physically ill and depressed, which describes Mr Dutta exactly; his risk must be asked about directly (Sections 2.7 and 9.7).

The good news is that the psychological treatments you studied in earlier chapters work in late life, usually with modest adaptations: a slower pace, written summaries and homework reminders, attention to hearing and vision, and coordination with the physician. Cognitive behaviour therapy (Chapter 5), problem-solving therapy (Section 5.5), behavioural activation, interpersonal psychotherapy (Section 3.7), whose problem areas of grief and role transition fit late life especially well, and reminiscence and life review (Section 10.7.3) all have controlled trials in older adults. The most instructive single study concerns not the treatment of an episode but the prevention of the next one.

Key study: maintenance therapy for depression after 60 (Reynolds and colleagues, 1999)

Question: Older people who recover from depression often relapse. What keeps them well? Method: At the University of Pittsburgh, Charles Reynolds, Ellen Frank and their colleagues treated 187 patients older than 59 (average age 67) who had recurrent major depression with the antidepressant nortriptyline plus interpersonal psychotherapy (IPT). The 107 who recovered fully were randomly assigned to one of four maintenance conditions for up to three years: monthly IPT with nortriptyline, monthly IPT with a placebo pill, nortriptyline in a medication clinic, or placebo in a medication clinic. Result: Over three years, depression recurred in 20 percent of those given combined IPT and nortriptyline, 43 percent of those on nortriptyline alone, 64 percent of those on IPT with placebo, and 90 percent of those on placebo. Conclusion: All three active treatments beat placebo, and the combination was best. Why it matters: Monthly psychotherapy on its own protected a substantial number of older patients, and adding it to medication gave the best protection of all.

The other lesson of late-life depression research is about where treatment happens. Most depressed older people never see a mental health professional; they see a primary care doctor, as Mr Dutta did. Two large American trials tested collaborative care, in which a depression care manager (usually a nurse, social worker or psychologist) works alongside the primary care doctor under the supervision of a psychiatrist. In the IMPACT trial (Improving Mood, Promoting Access to Collaborative Treatment), published by Jürgen Unützer and colleagues in 2002, 1,801 patients aged 60 or over with major depression or dysthymia in 18 primary care clinics were randomly assigned to usual care or to up to a year with a care manager, who offered education, followed the patient's progress, supported the doctor's prescribing, or delivered a brief psychotherapy, problem-solving treatment in primary care. At twelve months 45 percent of the care-managed patients had halved their depressive symptoms, against 19 percent with usual care. In PROSPECT (the Prevention of Suicide in Primary Care Elderly: Collaborative Trial), published by Martha Bruce and colleagues in 2004, the target was suicide risk itself. Among 598 depressed older patients in 20 practices, those whose care followed guidelines with the help of a care manager lost their suicidal ideation faster: at four months the proportion reporting it fell by 12.9 percentage points, against 3.0 points with usual care. The message for a country with very few geriatric psychiatrists and psychologists is the one you met in Section 9.6: a well-supported non-specialist, with specialist backing, can deliver much of what depressed older people need.

For Mr Dutta, the psychologist to whom his doctor refers him starts with a careful assessment (a depression screening scale designed for older people, a cognitive screen, a review of his medicines with the doctor, and direct questions about suicide), which shows a moderate depressive episode with intact cognition and passive thoughts that life is not worth living, without plans. Treatment combines three of the threads of this section. Behavioural activation rebuilds his days: a morning walk to the lake, a return to the adda twice a week, helping the para committee plan its Durga Puja. Interpersonal work focuses on his unfinished grief for his wife (Section 9.5) and on the role transition from engineer and husband to a man living alone. And a life review, which he turns into a handwritten memoir of his years building railway bridges, to be posted to his grandson in Bengaluru, helps him find again the sense that his life has counted for something. The memory complaints fade as the depression lifts.

Exam trap

Late-life depression is not normal ageing and responds to psychotherapy. It often presents with somatic and memory complaints rather than stated sadness; when it mimics dementia it is called pseudodementia. In Reynolds's 1999 maintenance trial, combined IPT and nortriptyline gave the lowest recurrence (20 percent against 90 percent on placebo). IMPACT (Unützer, 2002) and PROSPECT (Bruce, 2004) are trials of collaborative care in primary care; PROSPECT's special target was suicidal ideation. Older men who are widowed, alone and ill are the highest-risk group for suicide.

Capacity, elder abuse and the law

As Mrs Venkatesan's dementia advances, her elder son asks her to sign a gift deed transferring the family house to him "to avoid problems later". Her younger son objects, and the family's doctor asks the psychologist a question that comes up again and again with older clients: does she have the capacity to make this decision?

In clinical terms, decision-making capacity is a person's ability to make a particular decision for himself or herself. Clinicians assess capacity; competence, strictly, is a legal status that only a court can decide, though the words are often used interchangeably. Four principles guide the assessment. Capacity is presumed: the burden lies on showing that a person lacks it, not on the person to prove they have it. Capacity is decision-specific: Mrs Venkatesan may be perfectly able to decide what to wear, what to eat and that she wants to keep living with her family, and yet unable to make a complex decision about property. Capacity is time-specific: it can fluctuate, with delirium, with the time of day, with medication, so it is assessed for this decision at this time, and reassessed if things change. And an unwise decision is not proof of incapacity: people with full capacity make choices others think foolish, and the question is how the person decides, not what they decide.

What exactly is assessed? In an influential 1988 paper in the New England Journal of Medicine, the American psychiatrist Paul S. Appelbaum and the psychologist Thomas Grisso set out the four abilities that the legal standards for consent to treatment require: the ability to communicate a choice, to understand the relevant information, to appreciate the situation and its consequences for oneself, and to reason about the options, weighing risks and benefits. They later built a structured interview around these abilities, the MacArthur Competence Assessment Tool for Treatment (a test described in the Assessment book). England's Mental Capacity Act 2005 uses a closely similar functional test: a person lacks capacity for a decision if, because of an impairment of the mind or brain, he cannot understand the relevant information, retain it, use or weigh it, or communicate his decision.

Indian law takes the same approach for mental health decisions. Section 4 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (taught in full in Chapter 1) says that every person, including a person with mental illness, shall be deemed to have capacity to make decisions about his mental healthcare or treatment if he is able to understand the information relevant to the decision, to appreciate any reasonably foreseeable consequence of a decision or of a lack of decision, and to communicate the decision by speech, expression, gesture or any other means. (The Act's own wording links the three abilities with "or"; in practice clinicians assess all three.) The information must be given in simple language the person understands, or in sign language or with visual aids, and a decision that others think inappropriate or wrong does not by itself mean that the person lacks capacity. The same Act lets a person make an advance directive and appoint a nominated representative for the time when he may lose capacity (Chapter 1). Decisions about property and wills fall under other laws; the Indian Succession Act 1925, for example, allows any person of sound mind who is not a minor to make a will, and clinicians asked to judge a will-maker's capacity still use the questions of the old English case Banks v Goodfellow (1870), which asks whether the person understands that he is making a will and what it does, knows the extent of his property, and can bring to mind the people who might expect to benefit.

The psychologist's job is to give the person every chance to show capacity: to choose the best time of day, to explain the decision simply, with written notes, to treat anything reversible first, and to test understanding in the person's own words ("Tell me what this paper will do"). Seen at eleven in the morning, her best time, Mrs Venkatesan could say that the house was "for my sons", but could not explain that the deed would transfer ownership now, could not remember from one minute to the next what had been explained, and could not weigh the consequences for her younger son or for herself. The psychologist concluded that she lacked capacity for this decision at this time, while she retained it for many everyday decisions, and wrote the opinion in exactly those terms. The episode also raised a further concern, which leads to the last topic of this section.

The World Health Organization, following the Toronto Declaration of 2002, defines elder abuse as a single or repeated act, or lack of appropriate action, occurring within any relationship where there is an expectation of trust, which causes harm or distress to an older person. The key phrase is "expectation of trust": the abusers are usually not strangers but sons, daughters, daughters-in-law, spouses and paid carers. In a 2017 meta-analysis of 52 studies from 28 countries, Yongjie Yon and colleagues estimated that about one in six older people living in the community (15.7 percent) had experienced some form of abuse in the past year, roughly 141 million people worldwide. Table 10.17 lists the forms.

Table 10.17 Forms of elder abuse, with pooled past-year prevalence in community settings from Yon and colleagues (2017).
FormExamplesPooled prevalence
Psychological or emotionalInsults, threats, humiliation, isolating the person, threatening to send them to an old age home11.6 percent
Financial or materialTaking pension or savings, forcing a signature on a property deed, misusing a bank card6.8 percent
NeglectWithholding food, medicines, hygiene, spectacles or medical care by a responsible carer4.2 percent
PhysicalHitting, pushing, rough handling, inappropriate restraint or sedation2.6 percent
SexualAny sexual contact without consent, including with a person who cannot consent0.9 percent

The risk is highest when the older person is dependent and cognitively impaired, when the carer is exhausted, depressed, or misusing alcohol, when abuser and victim share a home and are isolated from others, and when the abuser depends on the victim financially. Barlow and Durand note that withholding food or medication and physical abuse are most common among carers of older people with cognitive deficits, which is one more reason to support carers before their strain turns into harm. Warning signs include unexplained injuries, weight loss and poor hygiene, fear or silence in the carer's presence, a carer who will not leave the clinician alone with the patient, missing money and sudden changes to a will or property. The clinician's response begins with interviewing the older person alone (Section 2.2), documenting carefully, assessing safety and capacity, and linking the person with help: social services, the police where a crime has been committed, the Maintenance Tribunal under the 2007 Act, and the national helpline for senior citizens, Elderline (14567), launched by the Ministry of Social Justice and Empowerment on 1 October 2021, the International Day of Older Persons. A capable older person's wishes must be respected even when they are frustrating; many abused parents refuse to act against a son. And because much abuse grows out of caregiver strain, helping the carer is often part of protecting the victim.

Indian context

The Maintenance and Welfare of Parents and Senior Citizens Act 2007 (assented to on 29 December 2007) defines a senior citizen as an Indian aged 60 or above. It obliges children and grandchildren to maintain parents and grandparents who cannot maintain themselves (a childless senior citizen can claim from relatives who will inherit his property), sets up Maintenance Tribunals that must decide applications within 90 days, and fixes the maximum maintenance allowance at Rs 10,000 a month. Under section 23, a transfer of property made on condition that the transferee will provide for the senior citizen can be declared void if that condition is not kept. Under section 24, abandoning a senior citizen is an offence punishable with imprisonment of up to three months, a fine of up to Rs 5,000, or both. State governments may set up old age homes, at least one in each district, for indigent senior citizens. The National Policy on Older Persons dates from 1999, and the National Programme for Health Care of the Elderly (NPHCE), launched by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare in 2010 to 2011, aims to bring preventive, curative and rehabilitative services for older people to every level of the health system, from sub-centres to district hospitals with dedicated geriatric wards and regional geriatric centres in medical colleges. Behind these laws lies social change: smaller families, the migration of adult children to cities and abroad, more older women living as widows because women live longer, and the slow loosening of the joint family that once took care of old age for granted.

Exam trap

Capacity is presumed, decision-specific and time-specific, and an unwise decision is not incapacity. Clinicians assess capacity; competence in the strict sense is a legal finding. Appelbaum and Grisso's four abilities are communicating a choice, understanding, appreciation and reasoning. Section 4 of the MHCA 2017 asks about three: understand the relevant information, appreciate foreseeable consequences, and communicate the decision. In elder abuse, psychological abuse is the commonest form and the abusers are usually family members. The 2007 Act: age 60, Tribunal decision within 90 days, maximum Rs 10,000 a month, abandonment punishable under section 24.

10.8 CounsellingMedium yield · 3 Qs

You already know a good deal about counselling. Section 6.10 defined it, traced its roots to Frank Parsons and the guidance movement, set out the traditional contrasts between guidance, counselling and psychotherapy (Table 6.21) and the modern view that they differ in degree rather than kind, and described the three great helping-skills models of Allen Ivey, Robert Carkhuff and Gerard Egan. Section 2.2 taught the individual skills: attending and SOLER, questions, minimal encouragers, paraphrasing, reflection of feeling, summarising, confrontation and empathy. This section completes the picture from the side of practice. It begins with the guidance programme from which counselling grew, and within which much counselling in Indian schools and colleges still happens; it then compares the three classic approaches to counselling, follows the counselling process from the first meeting to follow-up with the skills at work inside it, surveys the main areas in which counsellors work, and ends with the theories that guide career counselling.

Guidance and the guidance programme

Think of a Class 10 student in a government school in Mandya who has to choose between the science, commerce and arts streams. What she needs is partly information (Where does each stream lead? What marks do the courses need? What do the jobs at the end of them actually involve?), partly knowledge of herself (What am I good at? What do I enjoy?), and partly a conversation in which she can weigh the two against her worry about what her parents expect. A school guidance programme exists to provide all three. Guidance, as Section 6.10 explained, is the process of helping people understand themselves and their opportunities so that they can make wise choices and adjustments. It is traditionally divided into three kinds: educational guidance (choosing subjects, streams and institutions, study habits, learning difficulties), vocational or career guidance (choosing, preparing for and entering an occupation) and personal-social guidance (relationships, adjustment, health and emotional problems).

An organised guidance programme is usually described as a set of guidance services, each of which does a different job (Table 10.18). The orientation service helps newcomers settle into a new school or college. The appraisal or individual inventory service collects, organises and interprets information about each student, from tests, records, observations and interviews, so that the student and the counsellor have an accurate picture to work with. The information service supplies educational, occupational and personal-social information: what courses exist, what jobs require, where to find help. The counselling service offers individual and group counselling. The placement service helps the student into the next step, an educational placement in the right course or stream or a vocational placement in a job, apprenticeship or internship. The follow-up service keeps track of former students to see how they are faring and whether the programme helped them, and research and evaluation studies the programme itself so that it can be improved.

Table 10.18 The services of a school or college guidance programme.
ServiceWhat it doesExample
OrientationHelps newcomers adjust to a new institutionA first-week programme for Class 11 entrants
Appraisal (individual inventory)Collects and interprets information about each studentAptitude and interest testing, cumulative records
InformationProvides educational, occupational and personal-social informationA careers corner, talks by professionals
CounsellingHelps the student understand, decide and adjustIndividual sessions on choosing a stream
PlacementPlaces students in courses, jobs or internshipsStream allocation, campus recruitment
Follow-upTracks how students fare after placementA check six months into the new course
Research and evaluationStudies whether the programme worksA survey of former students

Which of these services matters most? Writers on guidance have long answered: counselling. The reason is clear when you look at what the others do. Appraisal and information gather facts; placement and follow-up act on a decision once it has been made. It is in counselling that the student brings knowledge of herself together with knowledge of the world, works through her doubts and her family's expectations, and actually makes the choice. That is why the counselling service is called the heart (or the hub) of the guidance programme. A school with an excellent battery of tests and a well-stocked careers library but no counselling has a filing cabinet, not a guidance programme.

Guidance workers collect their information with two families of tools. The testing techniques are the standardised tests of intelligence, aptitude, achievement, interests and personality, described in the Assessment book. Of these, the aptitude test deserves a definition here because it is so often confused with its neighbours: it measures a person's potential to acquire, with training, a particular skill or body of knowledge (mechanical, clerical, numerical, verbal, spatial), and so predicts future success; an achievement test measures what has already been learned, and an intelligence test measures general ability. The non-testing techniques include observation, questionnaires, rating scales, autobiographies, sociometry, the interview, the anecdotal record, the cumulative record and the case study. The interview, a purposeful face-to-face conversation, is used in guidance to gather facts, to give information and, above all, to counsel: the counselling interview is the vehicle through which counselling is done. The cumulative record is a longitudinal file kept by the school for each student over many years, recording identifying data and family background, attendance, marks year by year, test results, health, co-curricular activities, teachers' ratings of personal qualities and their remarks; it moves with the student from class to class and school to school, so that it gives a picture of development rather than a single snapshot. An anecdotal record is a brief, dated, factual description of one specific incident of a student's behaviour, written by a teacher who saw it, with any interpretation kept separate; many such anecdotes, often filed in the cumulative record, reveal patterns that a single observation cannot. The case study is the most comprehensive technique of all: an in-depth study of one individual that draws on every other source, used for students with serious or puzzling difficulties.

Indian context

Organised vocational guidance in India began before independence, with bureaus such as the Batliboi Vocational Guidance Bureau set up in Bombay in 1941. After independence the Secondary Education Commission (1952 to 1953, chaired by A. Lakshmanaswami Mudaliar) recommended guidance services in secondary schools, and in 1954 the Government of India set up the Central Bureau of Educational and Vocational Guidance, later merged into the National Council of Educational Research and Training (NCERT), which still trains teacher-counsellors through its Diploma Course in Guidance and Counselling. Three recent steps matter for a clinician. The Central Board of Secondary Education's affiliation bye-laws of 2018 require every affiliated secondary and senior secondary school to appoint a counsellor and wellness teacher (part-time in small schools). In 2020 the Ministry of Education launched Manodarpan, its psychosocial support programme and helpline for students (Section 10.3). And on 25 July 2025, in Sukdeb Saha v. State of Andhra Pradesh, a case arising from a student's death, the Supreme Court issued interim guidelines on student mental health, including that every educational institution with 100 or more enrolled students must appoint at least one qualified counsellor, psychologist or social worker trained in child and adolescent mental health, and that smaller institutions must set up referral links with outside professionals.

Exam trap

"Which technique or process involved in guidance is called the heart of the guidance programme?" (RML 2022) The phrase belongs to counselling: the counselling service is the heart of guidance, because it is where the student uses all the information to decide. The paper's options were case study, interview, cumulative record and aptitude test, and its key gave aptitude test. That key is doubtful. An aptitude test is only one tool of the appraisal service; the cumulative record is the main record of that service; the case study is the most comprehensive technique; and the interview, as the counselling interview, is the core tool of counselling itself, which makes it the most defensible of the four. If "counselling" appears among the options, choose it; if it does not, the interview is the best answer. Keep the definitions apart too: an aptitude test predicts potential with training, an achievement test measures what has been learned, and an anecdotal record describes one incident while a cumulative record covers years.

Directive, non-directive and eclectic counselling

In the middle of the twentieth century counsellors argued about who should lead the interview, the counsellor or the client. Three answers emerged, and they remain a staple of Indian guidance and counselling syllabi.

Directive counselling, also called counsellor-centred counselling or the Minnesota point of view, is Edmund Williamson's trait-and-factor method, which you met in Section 6.10. The counsellor is the expert. He gathers and interprets the facts, works out what is wrong, and tells the client what he thinks should be done, relying on the client's reason rather than his feelings. Williamson set the work out in six steps. Analysis collects data about the student from tests, records, interviews and other sources. Synthesis organises the data to show the student's strengths, weaknesses and characteristic patterns. Diagnosis identifies the problem and its causes. Prognosis predicts how the problem is likely to develop, or how the student is likely to fare on each of the paths open to him. Counselling (or treatment) helps the student understand the findings and act on them, often by advising him directly. And follow-up checks how things have gone and whether the problem has returned. The method is quick and suits problems that turn on information, such as choosing a course; its weaknesses are that it makes the client dependent, neglects feelings, and puts the decision in the counsellor's hands.

Non-directive counselling, also called client-centred counselling, is Carl Rogers's answer, launched in Counseling and Psychotherapy (1942) as an explicit challenge to Williamson and taught fully in Section 6.2. The client leads. The counsellor does not diagnose or advise but provides the conditions of empathy, unconditional positive regard and genuineness and reflects the client's feelings, trusting that a person who feels understood will find his own direction. It respects autonomy and builds self-reliance; its critics say it is slow, depends on clients who are verbally fluent and fairly mature, and suits problems that need information or quick decisions poorly.

A third position, eclectic counselling, took the view that both are right some of the time. Its best-known advocate was the American psychiatrist and psychologist Frederick C. Thorne (1909 to 1978), founder of the Journal of Clinical Psychology, whose Principles of Personality Counseling (1950) argued that the counsellor should select whatever methods, directive or non-directive, best fit the particular client, problem and stage of counselling. In practice an eclectic counsellor often begins non-directively, letting the client tell his story and express his feelings, and becomes more directive when information, interpretation or a plan is needed. The danger, as Section 8.11 explained in discussing technical eclecticism and integration, is that choice without a guiding theory becomes a grab-bag. Table 10.19 compares the three.

Table 10.19 Directive, non-directive and eclectic counselling.
FeatureDirectiveNon-directiveEclectic
Main figureE. G. Williamson (1939)Carl Rogers (1942)Frederick Thorne (1950)
CentreCounsellor-centredClient-centredVaries with the client and stage
Who decidesCounsellor advisesClient decidesShared, as the case requires
EmphasisIntellect, facts, diagnosisFeelings, the relationship, growthBoth, chosen deliberately
Typical methodSix steps from analysis to follow-upReflection and the core conditionsNon-directive start, directive when needed
Main criticismDependence; neglects emotionSlow; needs a fluent, mature clientCan lack a unifying theory
Clinical link

Many Indian clients arrive expecting a directive expert ("Madam, you tell me what I should do"), and a counsellor who only reflects feelings may seem evasive or unhelpful. Jaswant Singh Neki's proposal of the guru-chela relationship as a therapeutic model (Section 3.10), and the discussion of therapy in India in Section 8.11, describe this expectation. The skilled response is usually eclectic: begin by listening, so the client feels understood; give information and clear structure where they are needed; and still leave the decision with the client, because a decision someone else made for you is easily abandoned.

Exam trap

Williamson's six steps run analysis, synthesis, diagnosis, prognosis, counselling, follow-up; questions often swap synthesis and diagnosis or leave out prognosis. Directive counselling is counsellor-centred (Williamson, Minnesota); non-directive is client-centred (Rogers); eclectic counselling is associated with Frederick Thorne. Parsons founded vocational guidance but did not write the six steps.

The counselling process and its skills

Whatever the approach, counselling moves through a recognisable sequence. One widely used description, from the American counsellor educators Harold L. Hackney and Sherry Cormier in their textbook The Professional Counselor: A Process Guide to Helping, has five stages: rapport and relationship building, assessment or problem definition, goal setting, initiating interventions, and termination and follow-up. Other models cut the same journey differently (Egan's three stages and Ivey's five-stage interview in Section 6.10, Carkhuff's attending, responding, personalising and initiating), but the arc is the same: build a relationship, understand the problem, decide what the client wants, help him get there, and end well. Before the first stage comes the intake: the referral, the first contact, and the informed consent that tells the client what counselling involves and where its confidentiality ends (Chapter 1).

In the relationship stage the counsellor's aim is trust and a working alliance (Section 8.5). The skills are attending, minimal encouragers, open questions and primary empathy, and one more that beginners forget, structuring: explaining briefly what counselling is, how long sessions last, what the counsellor will and will not do, and the limits of confidentiality. In the assessment stage the counsellor helps the client tell the whole story and understand it in context: what the problem is, when and where it happens, how often, with whom, what the client has tried and what strengths he has. Questions, clarification, paraphrasing, reflection of feeling and summarising carry this stage; in guidance settings, tests and records add to it. In the goal-setting stage the problem is turned into goals that are specific, observable, realistic and the client's own. Hackney and Cormier distinguish process goals, the conditions the counsellor works to create (a safe, accepting relationship), from outcome goals, the changes the client wants in his life. In the intervention stage the counsellor draws on whatever methods fit the client and the goal: problem solving and decision making, cognitive techniques, information giving, role-play and assertiveness training, relaxation; here too the challenging skills come in, confrontation, immediacy, advanced empathy and self-disclosure (Section 6.10). In the last stage, termination is planned rather than allowed to happen: progress is reviewed, the client says what he has learned and how he will handle setbacks, sessions may be spaced out, and a follow-up contact weeks or months later checks that the gains have held (the ending of therapy is discussed in Section 8.10). Referral belongs here too: when a problem is beyond the counsellor's training or setting, such as psychosis, severe depression or high suicide risk, the counsellor's job is to refer the client to a psychiatrist or clinical psychologist and to make sure the referral is completed.

Watch the skills at work with one client. Neelam, 19, a second-year BSc student at a college in Mysuru, comes to the college counselling cell. She says: "My parents have fixed my engagement for next March. Everyone at home is so happy. I don't know why I can't sleep." Table 10.20 shows how a counsellor might respond with each skill, and what each response does.

Table 10.20 One client statement, many skills. Neelam: "My parents have fixed my engagement for next March. Everyone at home is so happy. I don't know why I can't sleep."
SkillWhat it doesExample response
Minimal encouragerInvites more without directing"Mm-hm. Go on."
Closed questionPins down a fact"When did the sleeplessness start?"
Open questionInvites the story"What has it been like for you since the engagement was fixed?"
ParaphraseRestates the content briefly, in the counsellor's own words"So the engagement is set for March, the family is delighted, and since then your sleep has gone."
Reflection of feelingNames the emotion, stated or implied"You sound confused, perhaps a little frightened, as if you're not allowed to feel anything but happy."
ClarificationMakes a vague message clear"When you say it's all decided, do you mean the date, or the marriage itself?"
SummaryDraws the threads of a longer stretch together"Today you've told me about the engagement, your wish to finish your degree and how hard it is to say so at home."
ConfrontationPoints out a discrepancy, supportively"You've said you're happy about it, and three times you've said you wish you could finish your degree first."
ImmediacyTalks about what is happening between you now"I notice you look away each time your father comes up. What's happening for you right now?"
Advanced empathyVoices what the client has only hinted at (later, once trust exists)"I wonder whether part of you fears that if you speak up, you'll lose your family's approval."

Look closely at the paraphrase, because it is the skill examiners ask about most. It restates the content of what Neelam said, the facts of her situation, in the counsellor's own, different words, and usually more briefly. It does not add an interpretation, does not ask anything, and does not name a feeling; it simply shows that she has been heard accurately and gives her the chance to correct the counsellor. Repeating her exact words ("Your parents have fixed your engagement for next March") would be parroting, which sounds mechanical. The reflection of feeling goes one level deeper, to the emotion beneath the content, and a summary does for a whole stretch of conversation what a paraphrase does for a single statement.

In Neelam's case the process ran its full course. Over the first two sessions the relationship and the story emerged: she liked the young man her parents had chosen but wanted to finish her degree before the wedding, and she had never in her life disagreed with her father. Her goals, in her own words, were "to know what I actually want" and "to tell Amma without a fight". The interventions were a simple decision balance of the costs and benefits of each option, and two sessions of role-play in which she practised what she would say to her mother, with the counsellor playing first her mother and then her father. She chose to enlist an aunt she trusted. Her family agreed to hold the wedding after her final examinations. Termination came after six sessions, with a review of what she had learned about speaking up, and at a follow-up meeting at the end of the semester she was sleeping well. Nothing in the counselling told Neelam to reject an arranged marriage; it helped her find, and say, what she wanted within the family she valued.

Exam trap

"When a counsellor repeats what the client says, but uses words that are different from those the client used, the counsellor is using what skill?" (RML 2021). The answer is paraphrasing (restatement, or reflection of content). Questioning asks for information rather than restating it. Empathy is the broader capacity to understand the client's world from inside and communicate that understanding; reflection of feeling is its main vehicle, and a paraphrase can express empathy, but "repeating what the client said in different words" defines the paraphrase. Nonverbal communication is posture, eye contact, tone and gesture, the territory of attending behaviour and SOLER. And keep the three reflecting skills apart: paraphrase = content, reflection = feeling, summary = many statements drawn together.

The main areas of counselling

Counselling has spread into almost every setting where people face problems of living. The skills are the same everywhere; what changes is the population, the typical concerns and the knowledge the counsellor needs. Table 10.21 gives an overview, and the paragraphs that follow pick out what each area adds.

School counselling is developmental, preventive and remedial at once: it helps every student with the normal tasks of growing up (study skills, choosing a stream, friendships, puberty), runs prevention programmes (on bullying, exam stress, substance use, online safety), and helps individual students in difficulty, working closely with teachers and parents. The school counsellor is often the first to notice a learning difficulty (Section 10.5), abuse (Section 10.6) or the warning signs of suicide, and school-based programmes are described in Section 10.3. College and university counselling deals with the transition to independent life, homesickness, examination and performance pressure, relationships, sexuality, identity and, increasingly, suicide prevention; the Supreme Court's 2025 guidelines described in Section 10.8.1 have made it a legal obligation for larger institutions. Career counselling is the subject of the next subsection.

Marital and premarital counselling helps couples with conflict, communication, infidelity, sexual problems, in-laws and decisions about separation; couples therapy is taught in Section 7.8, which also describes the counsellors attached to India's family courts, and sex therapy in Section 7.9. Premarital counselling is preventive: structured programmes teach couples who are about to marry to communicate, solve problems and handle conflict, and some use questionnaires that help each partner see where their expectations differ (Howard Markman and Scott Stanley's PREP programme and David Olson's PREPARE inventory are well-known American examples). Family counselling is covered in Chapter 7.

Rehabilitation counselling helps people with physical, sensory, intellectual and psychiatric disabilities to reach their personal, vocational and independent-living goals: adjusting to a disability, planning a return to work, using vocational training and supported employment, and claiming the benefits of disability certification. Its methods are described in Sections 9.8 and 9.9, and the rights it rests on (the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act 2016) in Chapter 1.

HIV counselling is the clearest example of counselling built into a medical procedure. Section 9.3 explained that in India testing at the Integrated Counselling and Testing Centres of the National AIDS Control Organisation is preceded and followed by counselling. Pre-test counselling (or, in the World Health Organization's newer guidance, brief pre-test information) explains what HIV is and how it is transmitted, what the test involves, what a positive, negative or indeterminate result would mean, and the window period, the weeks after infection during which a test can still be negative; it assesses the person's risk and obtains informed consent. Post-test counselling depends on the result. After a negative result it reinforces risk reduction and advises a repeat test if the person may still be in the window period. After a positive result the counsellor gives the result clearly and simply, allows time for the shock, deals with immediate fears (Will I die? Who must I tell? Will I lose my job?), plans disclosure to a partner, and, most importantly, links the person straight to treatment. The World Health Organization's 2015 guidelines sum up the ethics of testing as the five Cs: consent, confidentiality, counselling, correct test results, and connection to prevention, treatment and care.

De-addiction counselling rests on motivational interviewing and the stages of change (Section 6.9) and on relapse prevention and aftercare (Section 9.9), with the family drawn in wherever possible; in India it is delivered in de-addiction centres, hospitals and community programmes such as the Nasha Mukt Bharat Abhiyaan (launched in 2020). Grief counselling draws on the models of grief in Section 9.5; J. William Worden, in Grief Counseling and Grief Therapy (1982), distinguished grief counselling, which helps people through normal grief, from grief therapy, which treats complicated grief. Crisis counselling is described in Section 9.7, and the counselling of older adults and their families in Section 10.7. Two smaller areas complete the list. Genetic counselling helps families understand the risk of an inherited condition, such as thalassaemia or Down syndrome, and the choices open to them. Health counselling supports people with chronic illness and lifestyle change, the territory of Chapter 9.

Workplace counselling deals with work stress, burnout (Section 8.12), conflict with colleagues and managers, career transitions, and personal problems that spill into work. Much of it is delivered through employee assistance programmes (EAPs), which grew out of the occupational alcoholism programmes that American companies began in the 1940s and broadened, from the 1970s, to cover any personal problem that affects work. A typical EAP offers employees and their families free, confidential, short-term counselling and referral, and advises managers on how to handle a troubled employee; many large Indian companies, especially in information technology, now provide one. Workplace counselling is not the same as the branch of psychology that studies work itself. The branch called industrial-organisational psychology applies psychological principles and research to the workplace in order to optimise human behaviour there, improving both organisational effectiveness and employees' well-being. Its industrial side deals with fitting people to jobs (job analysis, selection and testing, training, performance appraisal, ergonomics); its organisational side deals with motivation, job satisfaction, leadership, teams and organisational change. Its founder is usually taken to be the German-American psychologist Hugo Münsterberg (1863 to 1916), whom you will meet again in Section 10.9 as a pioneer of forensic psychology, and whose Psychology and Industrial Efficiency appeared in English in 1913, and the Hawthorne studies you met in Section 2.8 are part of its history.

Table 10.21 The main areas of counselling.
AreaTypical concernsWhere to read more
SchoolStudy, streams, peers, bullying, exam stress, early signs of disorderSections 10.3 to 10.6
College and universityTransition, performance pressure, relationships, identity, suicide riskSection 10.8.1
CareerChoosing, entering and changing an occupationSection 10.8.5
Marital and premaritalConflict, communication, expectations, separationChapter 7
RehabilitationAdjustment to disability, work, independent livingSections 9.8 and 9.9
HIV pre-test and post-testRisk, consent, results, disclosure, linkage to treatmentSection 9.3
De-addictionMotivation, relapse prevention, family involvementSections 6.9 and 9.9
Grief and crisisBereavement, acute crisis, suicide riskSections 9.5 and 9.7
