Exam trap

"Which branch of psychology is concerned with optimising human behaviour in workplaces?" (IHBAS 2024). The answer is industrial-organisational psychology. Counselling psychology helps individuals with problems of living and personal growth, including at work through EAPs, but it does not study the organisation; forensic psychology applies psychology to the legal system (Section 10.9); developmental psychology studies change across the life span. The five Cs of HIV testing are consent, confidentiality, counselling, correct results and connection.

Career counselling and the theories of career development

Sameer is 17 and in Class 12 at a school in Lucknow, studying physics, chemistry and mathematics and attending an engineering entrance coaching class every evening because his father, an engineer himself, has always said that Sameer will be one too. His marks are falling, and after failing two mock tests he is sent to the school counsellor. He comes alive only when he talks about the things he does outside class: he captains the debating team, acts in the school's plays, designs the posters for the annual fest and organised it last year. What should a career counsellor do with him? Each of the theories in this subsection gives part of the answer, and Table 10.22 compares them.

The trait-and-factor tradition. The oldest answer is Frank Parsons's (Section 6.10): understand the person, understand the world of work, and use "true reasoning" to match the two. Parsons's Choosing a Vocation (1909) made him the father of vocational guidance, and Williamson turned his approach into directive counselling. The approach survives in every aptitude test and interest inventory, but on its own it is static. It assumes that each person has one right job, that people and jobs stay the same, and that the choice is made once, in adolescence; it says nothing about development, about chance, or about the family that, in India, so often makes the decision.

Holland's theory of vocational personalities and work environments. The most influential modern version of matching comes from John L. Holland (1919 to 2008) of Johns Hopkins University, who first set out his theory in "A theory of vocational choice" in the Journal of Counseling Psychology in 1959 and developed it in Making Vocational Choices (1973, revised 1985 and 1997). Holland made four assumptions: that most people can be described as resembling one or more of six personality types; that there are six matching kinds of work environment; that people search for environments that let them use their skills and abilities, express their attitudes and values and take on agreeable problems and roles; and that behaviour is determined by the interaction between personality and environment. The six types, known by their initials as RIASEC, are:

  • Realistic (the doers): prefer practical work with tools, machines, plants and animals; mechanics, farmers, electricians, civil engineers.
  • Investigative (the thinkers): prefer observing, analysing and solving intellectual problems; scientists, researchers, doctors.
  • Artistic (the creators): prefer unstructured, expressive, original work; artists, writers, designers, actors, musicians.
  • Social (the helpers): prefer informing, training, caring for and healing others; teachers, counsellors, nurses, social workers.
  • Enterprising (the persuaders): prefer leading, persuading and managing others for organisational or economic goals; managers, lawyers, salespeople, politicians.
  • Conventional (the organisers): prefer ordered, systematic work with data and records; accountants, bank clerks, office administrators.

A person is described by a Holland code of the two or three types he most resembles, in order: a counsellor might be SAE, an accountant CEI. Holland's types are arranged on a Holland's hexagon, first drawn in a 1969 research report by Holland and his colleagues, in which the distance between two types reflects how different they are (Figure 10.11). Adjacent types, such as Realistic and Investigative, have much in common; opposite types, Realistic and Social, Investigative and Enterprising, Artistic and Conventional, have least. Four concepts follow. Congruence is the degree of fit between a person's type and the environment (a Social person in a Social job); it predicts satisfaction, stability and achievement. Consistency is how close together on the hexagon the first two letters of a person's code lie; an RI code is consistent, an RS code is not. Differentiation is how clearly a profile is defined, one or two high peaks against a flat profile in which everything scores about the same. Identity is the clarity and stability of a person's goals, interests and talents. Holland also designed a self-scored interest inventory, the Self-Directed Search, and his six types are used in other interest inventories (the inventories are described in the Assessment book).

Applied to Sameer, an interest inventory gives a code of EAS: Enterprising, Artistic and Social, with low scores on Realistic and Investigative. Engineering is largely a Realistic and Investigative environment, on the far side of the hexagon from his code, so his congruence with it is poor, which helps to explain why the coaching class feels like a cage. His profile is well differentiated; his first two letters, E and A, are not neighbours on the hexagon but alternate corners with S between them, which Holland would call moderately consistent; and the code points towards environments such as law, management, journalism, advertising and design. Holland's theory has strong research support and is easy to use, but it is still a matching theory: it describes where a person might fit, not how he gets there, and it says little about the family, social class and gender pressures that narrow choices before any test is taken.

Super's life-span, life-space theory. The developmental answer comes from Donald E. Super (1910 to 1994) of Teachers College, Columbia University, whose "A theory of vocational development" (his presidential address to the American Psychological Association's Division of Counseling and Guidance, published in 1953) and The Psychology of Careers (1957) turned career choice from a single event into a lifelong process. Super's central proposition was that in choosing an occupation a person is implementing a self-concept: his picture of his own abilities, interests, values and personality, which develops through childhood play, school, part-time work and the reactions of others, and which keeps changing, so that career choice and adjustment are continuous. Careers unfold through a maxicycle of five life stages (Figure 10.12): growth (birth to about 14), when the self-concept and attitudes to work form through fantasy, play and interests; exploration (about 15 to 24), when the young person tries out roles in school, leisure and part-time work and moves through the developmental tasks of crystallising a vocational preference, specifying it and implementing it; establishment (about 25 to 44), when he secures a place in a field, stabilises and consolidates it and advances; maintenance (about 45 to 64), when he holds on to what he has achieved, updates his skills and innovates; and decline or disengagement (65 onwards), when work slows down and retirement brings new roles. The ages are flexible, and Super stressed that people recycle through smaller versions of exploration and establishment (minicycles) whenever they change jobs, lose one, migrate or return to study.

Two further ideas complete Super's theory. The first, career maturity (Super's term was vocational maturity), is a person's readiness to make the career decisions expected at his stage: planfulness, exploration, knowledge of occupations and decision-making skill. It is measured against age-mates, not against adults, and for adults Super and later writers preferred the term career adaptability, the readiness to cope with changing work and working conditions. The life-space half of the theory recognises that work is only one of the roles a person plays. In a 1980 paper Super presented the life-career rainbow, in which the outer arcs show the life stages and the inner bands show how much of a person's time and emotional involvement goes into each of nine major roles over the life span (child, student, leisurite, citizen, worker, spouse, homemaker, parent and pensioner), played in four principal "theatres": the home, the community, the school and the workplace. Sameer, in Super's terms, is in the exploration stage but has not explored at all: his preference was specified for him by his father before he ever crystallised one of his own, which is a problem of career maturity, not of ability. Counselling therefore starts with exploration: shadowing a lawyer and a design studio for a day, talking to people in the fields his code points to, and reflecting on what his theatre and debating say about his self-concept.

Ginzberg's developmental theory. Super's was not the first developmental theory. In Occupational Choice: An Approach to a General Theory (1951) the Columbia economist Eli Ginzberg (1911 to 2002) and his colleagues, the psychiatrist Sol Ginsburg, the sociologist Sidney Axelrad and the psychologist John Herma, proposed that occupational choice develops through three periods. In the fantasy period (up to about 11) children choose freely and playfully, without regard to ability or reality ("I will be a pilot"). In the tentative period (about 11 to 17) choices are based first on interests, then on capacities, then on values, ending in a transition substage in which the young person begins to face reality. In the realistic period (about 17 to the early twenties) the person moves through exploration, crystallisation and specification of a choice. Ginzberg first described the process as largely irreversible and as a compromise between wishes and possibilities; in a restatement in 1972 he concluded that occupational choice is a lifelong process of decision making and that "optimisation" describes it better than compromise. Super built his own theory partly as a reply, adding the self-concept and extending the stages across the whole of working life.

Krumboltz's social learning theory and planned happenstance. John D. Krumboltz of Stanford University applied Albert Bandura's social learning theory to careers. In "A social learning theory of career selection" (1976, with Anita Mitchell and G. Brian Jones) he identified four kinds of influence on career decisions: genetic endowment and special abilities (sex, physical characteristics, talents such as musical or athletic ability); environmental conditions and events (the job market, family resources, social and educational policies, natural events, largely outside the person's control); learning experiences, both instrumental (being reinforced or punished for an activity) and associative (observing others and pairing an occupation with positive or negative images, as Sameer has done with his engineer father and with an admired theatre teacher); and task approach skills, such as setting goals, gathering information and making decisions. Out of these come the person's beliefs about himself and about the world of work, and the actions he takes. Two decades later, in "Planned happenstance: constructing unexpected career opportunities" (1999), Kathleen Mitchell, Al Levin and Krumboltz added that unplanned events are inevitable and often decisive in careers, and that counselling should teach people to create and use them rather than fear them. They named five skills that turn chance into opportunity: curiosity, persistence, flexibility, optimism and risk taking. Krumboltz later combined the two ideas as the happenstance learning theory (2009). For Sameer, a chance invitation to a workshop by a visiting advertising agency could be just such an event, if he is curious enough to go.

Other theories in brief. The American psychologist Anne Roe (1904 to 1991), in The Psychology of Occupations (1956), proposed that early parent-child relations (emotionally concentrated, avoiding or accepting) shape whether a person becomes oriented towards people or away from them, and so towards occupational groups such as service and business contact or technology and science. Linda S. Gottfredson's theory of circumscription and compromise (1981) describes how children, from an early age, rule out occupations they see as wrong for their sex and then for their social class and ability, and later compromise among what is left; it explains why some options never even reach a career counsellor. And social cognitive career theory, proposed in 1994 by Robert Lent, Steven Brown and Gail Hackett, builds on Bandura's later social cognitive theory: career interests, choices and performance grow from self-efficacy beliefs ("Can I do this?"), outcome expectations ("What will happen if I do?") and personal goals, all shaped by the person's background and by the support and barriers around him.

Table 10.22 Theories of career development compared.
TheoryOriginator and dateCore ideaTerms to remember
Trait-and-factorFrank Parsons, 1909; WilliamsonMatch the person's traits to the job's requirementsKnow yourself, know the work, true reasoning
Developmental (three periods)Ginzberg and colleagues, 1951; restated 1972Choice develops through childhood and adolescenceFantasy, tentative, realistic
Life-span, life-spaceDonald Super, 1953, 1957, 1980Career choice implements a developing self-concept across lifeFive stages, career maturity, life-career rainbow, recycling
Vocational personalities and environmentsJohn Holland, 1959, 1973People seek work environments that fit their typeRIASEC, hexagon, congruence, consistency, differentiation, identity
Social learning; planned happenstanceKrumboltz, 1976; Mitchell, Levin and Krumboltz, 1999Choices are learned; chance events can be turned into opportunitiesFour factors, task approach skills, curiosity, persistence, flexibility, optimism, risk taking
Personality and early relationsAnne Roe, 1956Parent-child climate orients a person towards or away from peopleEight occupational groups
Circumscription and compromiseLinda Gottfredson, 1981Children rule options out by sex type and prestige, then compromiseCircumscription, compromise
Social cognitive career theoryLent, Brown and Hackett, 1994Self-efficacy and outcome expectations drive interests and choicesSelf-efficacy, outcome expectations, goals

Sameer's counselling shows how the theories work together in an Indian setting, where a career decision is rarely the student's alone. The counsellor met Sameer four times and his parents twice. Holland's code gave the family a common language ("He is an E and an A, and engineering is an R and an I"); Super's stages reframed the problem as one of exploration rather than failure; and Krumboltz's ideas helped Sameer's father see that his own path into engineering had been shaped by his learning experiences, which were not his son's. The family agreed that Sameer would finish Class 12 in his present subjects, which keep many doors open, but would also prepare for the entrance examination for five-year law programmes, open to students from any stream, and spend the summer volunteering with a theatre group. The counsellor's last step was a follow-up appointment after the results, because, as Super would remind us, the decision would not be the last one Sameer makes.

Exam trap

Parsons (1909) is the father of vocational guidance and of the trait-and-factor approach; the first developmental theory of occupational choice is Ginzberg's (1951: fantasy, tentative, realistic). Super is the self-concept, the five stages (growth, exploration, establishment, maintenance, decline or disengagement), career maturity and the life-career rainbow; do not give the rainbow to Holland. Holland is RIASEC and the hexagon, where the opposite pairs are R and S, I and E, A and C; congruence means person-environment fit. Krumboltz is social learning and planned happenstance; Roe is early parent-child relations; Gottfredson is circumscription and compromise.

10.9 Forensic Clinical PsychologyLower yield · 1 Q

Every clinical skill you have met in this book changes its meaning when the client is sent by a court. In an outpatient clinic the psychologist asks, "What is wrong, and how can I help?" In a forensic assessment the questions come from the law, and they are strange ones for a clinician. Can this man understand the charge against him? On the night of the killing, did he know that what he was doing was wrong? Which parent should a six-year-old live with? Is this seventeen-year-old mature enough to be tried as an adult? Will this prisoner offend again if he is released? The person in front of you may not want to be seen, may have every reason to exaggerate or to conceal, and will not be helped, in the ordinary sense, by what you write, because your report goes to a judge. This section follows clinical psychology into the legal system, from the first question a criminal court asks (is the accused fit to be tried?) to the last (what will stop him offending again?), and then into the family court, the juvenile justice system and the prison.

What forensic psychology is, and the roles it gives a clinician

The word forensic comes from the Latin forum, the public square of ancient Rome in which trials were held; anything forensic belongs to the courts. In the sense used in this book, forensic psychology is the application of psychological science and practice to questions that arise in the law and the legal system. A narrow definition confines it to clinical work for the courts: assessments of fitness to stand trial, criminal responsibility, risk and child custody, and the testimony that goes with them. A broad definition takes in everything psychology does where it meets the law, including research on eyewitness memory and juries, the investigation of crime, the treatment of offenders and the support of victims. Forensic clinical psychology is the clinical core of that wider field, and it is what an Indian clinical psychologist is most likely to be asked to do.

The field is older than many people think. In 1908 Hugo Münsterberg (1863 to 1916), the German experimental psychologist whom William James had brought to Harvard, published On the Witness Stand, a book of essays arguing that the courts ignored what the laboratory already knew about the unreliability of perception and memory, the effects of suggestion and the detection of deception. The lawyers were not impressed. In 1909 the American legal scholar John Henry Wigmore replied with a satirical account of an imaginary trial in which Münsterberg was sued for libelling the legal profession and lost, because psychology could not yet produce the precise, tested findings he had claimed for it. The exchange set the terms of a debate that still runs: when is psychological knowledge good enough to be heard in court?

American courts gave two famous answers. In Frye v. United States (1923) a court refused to admit the results of a systolic blood pressure "lie detector" test devised by the psychologist William Moulton Marston, ruling that a scientific technique must have gained general acceptance in its field before it could be used as evidence. This Frye standard governed American courts for seventy years, until Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals (1993) made the trial judge the gatekeeper who must decide whether the evidence is scientifically valid: whether the method has been tested, peer-reviewed and has a known error rate. Meanwhile, in Jenkins v. United States (1962), a federal appeals court held that a properly qualified psychologist could give expert opinion on mental disease, which until then had been treated as the psychiatrist's preserve. (India's own encounter with scientific lie detection, the Supreme Court's 2010 ruling in Selvi v. State of Karnataka on narcoanalysis, the polygraph and brain-mapping, is told in Section 3.10.) The American Psychology-Law Society, founded in the late 1960s, later merged with the Psychology and Law division of the American Psychological Association (APA) and is now its Division 41; in 2001 the APA recognised forensic psychology as a specialty, and its Specialty Guidelines for Forensic Psychology (2013) now set out the standards of practice.

Within this field a clinician can play five roles. As an evaluator, the psychologist assesses a person to answer a legal question and writes a report: fitness to stand trial, mental state at the time of an offence, risk of violence, parenting capacity, the psychological damage claimed in a compensation case. As an expert witness, the psychologist testifies in court. An ordinary witness may describe only what he or she saw and heard; an expert witness may give opinions, because the court accepts that the expert has specialised knowledge the judge lacks. The court decides whether to admit the testimony, the other side cross-examines it, and the expert's duty is to the court, not to the side that pays the fee. The public's suspicion that experts are "hired guns" whose opinions suit whoever engaged them is the reason that duty matters. As a consultant, the psychologist advises lawyers or the police. As a treating clinician, the psychologist works in a prison, a forensic psychiatric ward, a juvenile home or a probation service. And as a researcher, the psychologist studies the questions the law depends on.

The consulting role includes two investigative tools that appear as distractors in exam questions about offender treatment. In offender profiling, investigators attempt to infer the likely characteristics of an unknown offender (age, background, lifestyle, where he lives) from the way a crime was committed. It was developed in the 1970s by the Behavioral Science Unit of the American Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), and in Britain from the 1980s by David Canter, who called his more statistical approach investigative psychology. The cognitive interview, developed in the mid-1980s by Ronald P. Fisher and R. Edward Geiselman, is a way of questioning cooperative witnesses that uses principles of memory to help them recall more without increasing errors. Its four original instructions are to reinstate mentally the context of the event (where you were, what you felt), to report everything, however trivial it seems, to recall the events in different orders (for example backwards), and to recall them from a different perspective (as another witness would have seen them). Both tools belong to the investigation of crime; neither is a treatment for offenders.

The hardest lesson of forensic work is that the forensic role and the therapeutic role are different jobs. Stuart A. Greenberg and Daniel W. Shuman argued in 1997 that there is an "irreconcilable conflict" between them, and Table 10.23 shows why. The therapist's client is the patient; the forensic evaluator's client is the court or the lawyer who asked the question. The therapist is supportive and accepts the patient's account as the patient's truth; the evaluator is neutral and must test that account against records and informants, because the examinee may have strong reasons to distort it. The therapist promises confidentiality within its usual limits (Chapter 1); the evaluator must tell the examinee at the start that what is said will go into a report that may be read in open court and may be used against him. A therapist who agrees to give expert evidence about his own patient betrays one role or the other: either he advocates for the patient and stops being impartial, or he becomes the patient's judge and destroys the therapy. The rule that follows is simple: do not act as both treating therapist and forensic evaluator for the same person.

Table 10.23 The therapeutic and the forensic role compared (after Greenberg and Shuman, 1997).
FeatureTreating therapistForensic evaluator
Who the client isThe patientThe court, or the lawyer who asked the question
PurposeTo help the patient changeTo answer a legal question
StanceSupportive, empathic, on the patient's sideNeutral, objective, sceptical
The person's accountAccepted as the patient's experienceTested against records, informants and validity checks
ConfidentialityKept, within the usual legal limitsExplicitly limited: the report goes to court
Who started itUsually the patient seeks helpUsually ordered or requested by a court or lawyer

A last ethical question concerns the ultimate issue, the legal conclusion the court itself has to reach: was the accused legally insane, is he fit to plead, should the mother have custody? Many forensic psychologists hold that the expert should describe the person's mental state and explain how it bears on the legal test, and leave the legal conclusion to the judge, whose job it is. After the Hinckley verdict (Section 10.9.3) the American federal rules of evidence went further and barred experts from stating whether a defendant had the mental state that the crime requires.

Exam trap

The branch of psychology concerned with "optimising human behaviour in workplaces" is industrial-organisational psychology (Section 10.8), not forensic psychology; forensic psychology applies psychology to legal questions. Hugo Münsterberg wrote On the Witness Stand (1908). APA Division 41 is the American Psychology-Law Society (Division 12 is clinical psychology). Offender profiling and the cognitive interview (Fisher and Geiselman) are investigative tools, not offender treatments. Never be both the therapist and the forensic evaluator of the same person.

Fitness to stand trial

Before anyone can be tried, the law requires that he be able to take part in his own trial. A trial is a contest: the accused must understand what he is charged with, follow the evidence, tell his lawyer what really happened, decide whether to plead guilty and give evidence if he chooses. A person who cannot do these things is not really being tried at all; he is being processed. The question of competence to stand trial (in British and Indian usage, fitness to plead or fitness to stand trial) therefore comes first, and it concerns the person's mental state now, at the time of the proceedings.

The standard test comes from the United States Supreme Court's decision in Dusky v. United States (1960). Milton Dusky had been tried for kidnapping although there were doubts about his mental state, and the Court held that it was not enough for the trial judge to find that he was oriented in time and place and had some recollection of events. The test, the Court said, is whether the defendant has "sufficient present ability to consult with his lawyer with a reasonable degree of rational understanding" and "a rational as well as factual understanding of the proceedings against him". Read it slowly and you will find three abilities. Factual understanding means knowing the charge, the possible penalties and the roles of the judge, the prosecutor and one's own lawyer. Rational understanding means that this knowledge is not distorted by illness: a man who can name the judge's role but believes the trial is a test sent by God to prove his divinity has factual but not rational understanding. The ability to consult means being able to communicate with one's lawyer, follow the evidence and help with the defence.

Three features of competence surprise students. First, the threshold is low: a diagnosis of schizophrenia or intellectual disability does not make a person unfit, and most people with mental illness are fit to be tried. Second, competence is not about guilt or about the time of the offence; a person may have been floridly psychotic when he committed the act and be perfectly fit by the time of trial, or the reverse. Third, the usual outcome of a finding of unfitness is treatment, with the aim of restoration of competence, after which the trial goes ahead. Because treatment can drag on, the Supreme Court ruled in Jackson v. Indiana (1972) that a person found unfit cannot be held indefinitely: after a reasonable period he must either be restored to competence, committed under the ordinary civil law, or released. Structured instruments for assessing competence, such as the MacArthur Competence Assessment Tool for Criminal Adjudication, are described in the Assessment book.

Indian law handles the same problem in the chapter of the criminal procedure code titled "Provisions as to accused persons of unsound mind". This was Chapter XXV of the Code of Criminal Procedure 1973 (sections 328 to 339); since 1 July 2024 it has been Chapter XXVII of the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita 2023 (BNSS), sections 367 to 378, which replaced the Code. Section 367 of the BNSS (formerly section 328) covers a Magistrate holding an inquiry, and section 368 (formerly section 329) covers a trial before a Magistrate or a Court of Session. The steps are worth knowing in order:

  • If the Magistrate has reason to believe that the accused is of unsound mind and consequently incapable of making his defence, the accused is examined by the civil surgeon of the district or another medical officer named by the State Government.
  • If the civil surgeon finds unsoundness of mind, the accused is referred to a psychiatrist or clinical psychologist of a government hospital or government medical college for care, treatment and prognosis, who informs the Magistrate whether the accused is suffering from unsoundness of mind or intellectual disability. The accused may appeal against that finding to a Medical Board made up of the head of psychiatry at the nearest government hospital and a member of the psychiatry faculty of the nearest government medical college.
  • The Magistrate then decides whether the condition makes the accused incapable of making (in a trial, entering) his defence. If it does, the court looks at the prosecution's evidence. If there is no prima facie case, the accused is discharged; if there is, the proceedings are postponed for the period of treatment that the psychiatrist or clinical psychologist considers necessary. Where the incapacity is due to intellectual disability, the Magistrate orders the inquiry closed (in a trial, the court does not hold the trial) and deals with the accused under section 369.
  • In every case the person is then dealt with under section 369 (formerly section 330). It provides for release on bail where in-patient treatment is not needed and a relative or friend undertakes to obtain regular out-patient psychiatric treatment and to prevent harm; otherwise for the accused to be kept where regular psychiatric treatment can be provided; and, in some cases, for transfer to a residential facility for persons with unsoundness of mind or intellectual disability. When the person becomes fit again, the inquiry or trial resumes.
Indian context

Notice that Indian criminal procedure names the clinical psychologist, alongside the psychiatrist, as the professional to whom an accused of unsound mind is referred for treatment and prognosis. The words were added to the old Code by the Code of Criminal Procedure (Amendment) Act 2008 and carried into the BNSS. A clinical psychologist working in a government hospital in India can therefore expect to be asked by a court whether an accused person is of unsound mind or has an intellectual disability, and whether he can follow his trial. The report should answer the Dusky-type questions in plain words (what the person knows about the charge, the court and the possible outcomes; whether illness distorts that knowledge; whether he can instruct his lawyer) and give an opinion on whether and how soon treatment could restore fitness.

Push a case through these provisions. Raghav, a 29-year-old daily-wage worker in Bengaluru with a ten-year history of schizophrenia, stopped his medication, became convinced that his neighbour was poisoning the family's water tank, and struck the neighbour on the head with an iron rod, seriously injuring him. When he was produced before the Magistrate, Raghav spoke about radio signals and laughed when asked whether he understood the charge. The civil surgeon referred him to the clinical psychologist and psychiatrist at the district hospital. The psychologist found that Raghav knew he was in a court and that the man in the black robe was "the judge", but believed that the trial was a staged drama arranged by his neighbour's political party and that his lawyer was part of the plot; he refused to speak to the lawyer at all. He had some factual understanding, but neither rational understanding nor the ability to consult. The Magistrate recorded that Raghav was incapable of making his defence and postponed the proceedings. After eight weeks of antipsychotic treatment Raghav could describe the charge, accepted his lawyer and discussed what had happened, and the proceedings resumed. Whether he can be held responsible for the assault is a different question, about a different moment, which you will return to in Section 10.9.4. Table 10.24 sets the two questions side by side.

Table 10.24 Two different legal questions about an accused person's mind.
FeatureFitness to stand trialCriminal responsibility (insanity)
Time in questionThe present, during the proceedingsThe past, at the moment of the act
Question askedCan he understand the proceedings and help his lawyer?Because of mental illness, did he lack the knowledge (or control) the law requires?
American sourceDusky v. United States (1960)M'Naghten, ALI or Insanity Defense Reform Act tests
Indian lawSections 367 to 369 BNSS (formerly 328 to 330 CrPC)Section 22 BNS (formerly section 84 IPC)
Usual outcomeTrial postponed; treatment to restore fitnessAcquittal, usually followed by detention for treatment
Exam trap

Competence (fitness) looks at the present; insanity looks at the past. A person can be insane at the time of the act yet fit for trial, or sane at the time of the act yet unfit for trial. The Dusky test (1960) asks for a rational as well as factual understanding of the proceedings and the ability to consult with a lawyer; orientation and memory alone are not enough. A diagnosis does not make a person unfit. Jackson v. Indiana (1972) forbids indefinite detention for unfitness. In India, BNSS sections 367 and 368 name the psychiatrist or clinical psychologist as the expert to whom the accused is referred.

The insanity defence, from M'Naghten to Hinckley

The criminal law punishes people who choose to do wrong. Two things must therefore be proved: the actus reus, the guilty act, and the mens rea, the guilty mind (intention, knowledge or recklessness). A driver who kills a pedestrian who steps without warning in front of her car has caused a death but has no guilty mind, and is not a murderer. The insanity defence extends the same logic to people whose mental disorder at the time of the act was so severe that blaming them makes no sense. It is a legal concept, not a medical one: the question is never simply whether the accused had a mental illness, but whether the illness took away the particular capacities that the law's test names. The tests have changed over 180 years, and the changes form one of the most examined stories in this book (Table 10.25 and Figure 10.13).

The story begins in London. On 20 January 1843 Daniel M'Naghten, a woodturner from Glasgow who believed that the Tory party was persecuting him and following him wherever he went, shot Edward Drummond, the private secretary of the Prime Minister, Sir Robert Peel, in the mistaken belief that Drummond was Peel. Drummond died five days later. At his trial in March 1843 medical witnesses described M'Naghten's delusions, and he was found not guilty on the ground of insanity. The verdict caused an uproar. Queen Victoria, who had herself survived three attempts on her life in the previous three years, wrote to the Prime Minister about it, and the House of Lords put a series of questions on the law of insanity to a panel of judges led by Sir Nicolas Conyngham Tindal, Chief Justice of the Common Pleas. The judges' answers became the M'Naghten rules. Their core reads: "it must be clearly proved that, at the time of the committing of the act, the party accused was labouring under such a defect of reason, from disease of the mind, as not to know the nature and quality of the act he was doing; or if he did know it, that he did not know he was doing what was wrong". M'Naghten himself spent the rest of his life in hospital, at Bethlem and then at the new Broadmoor asylum, where he died in 1865.

The M'Naghten test has two limbs, and both concern knowledge. A person is excused if, because of disease of the mind, he did not know the nature and quality of his act (the classic textbook example is the man who strangles his wife believing that he is squeezing a lemon) or, if he did know what he was doing, did not know that it was wrong. The test is purely cognitive. The judges also answered a question about delusions: a person who acts under a delusion is to be judged as if the facts in his delusion were true. If he believed that a man was about to kill him and killed in what he thought was self-defence, he is excused; if he believed that the man had insulted him and killed in revenge, he is punishable, because revenge would be a crime even if the insult were real.

Critics soon argued that knowing is not the whole of the mind. A person may know perfectly well that an act is wrong and yet, because of mental illness, be unable to stop himself. The irresistible impulse test, adopted by the Alabama Supreme Court in Parsons v. State (1887; some sources give 1886) and in several other American states, added a volitional limb: the accused is excused if mental disease had so destroyed his power to choose that he could not avoid the act, even though he knew it was wrong. Courts sometimes put the question as the "policeman at the elbow" test: would he have done it even with a policeman standing at his elbow? The objection is obvious and has never been answered: nobody, psychologist or judge, can tell an impulse that could not be resisted from one that was simply not resisted.

In 1954 Judge David L. Bazelon (1909 to 1993) of the federal Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia tried a radically broader approach in Durham v. United States. Under the Durham rule an accused "is not criminally responsible if his unlawful act was the product of mental disease or mental defect". Mental health professionals welcomed it at first, because it let them describe the whole person to the jury rather than squeezing him into the narrow questions of knowledge and control. It soon became clear, however, that clinicians could not reliably say whether a crime was the "product" of an illness, that the experts were in effect deciding the verdict, and that the result depended on whatever the latest diagnostic manual counted as a disease. The rule was abandoned in the District of Columbia in 1972, in United States v. Brawner, in favour of the test described next.

That test was drafted by the American Law Institute (ALI), a body of judges, lawyers and law professors, for its Model Penal Code of 1962. The ALI test reads: "A person is not responsible for criminal conduct if at the time of such conduct as a result of mental disease or defect he lacks substantial capacity either to appreciate the criminality (wrongfulness) of his conduct or to conform his conduct to the requirements of law". Three words carry the weight. "Substantial" means that a total loss of capacity is not required. "Appreciate" is broader than M'Naghten's "know": it includes an emotional grasp of what one is doing, not just an intellectual one. And "conform" brings back the volitional limb of the irresistible impulse test. A second clause excludes any abnormality "manifested only by repeated criminal or otherwise antisocial conduct", so that a history of offending cannot by itself prove the illness that excuses it; the clause was aimed at psychopathy. By the 1970s the ALI test was used in most federal courts and many states.

Then came the case that changed everything. On 30 March 1981, outside the Washington Hilton Hotel, John W. Hinckley Jr. fired six shots at President Ronald Reagan. He wounded the President, his press secretary James Brady (who was left permanently disabled), a police officer and a Secret Service agent. Hinckley had become obsessed with the actress Jodie Foster after watching the film Taxi Driver, and said that he had tried to kill the President to impress her. At his trial in 1982, under the ALI test and a rule that required the prosecution to prove his sanity beyond reasonable doubt, the jury found him not guilty by reason of insanity on all thirteen charges. He was sent to St Elizabeths Hospital in Washington, where he stayed until 2016, when he was released to live with his mother under conditions; the last conditions were lifted in June 2022.

The public reaction was fury, and the law moved fast. About three-quarters of the American states tightened their insanity rules. Congress passed the Insanity Defense Reform Act of 1984 (IDRA) for the federal courts, which returned to a M'Naghten-like test: the accused is excused only if, "as a result of a severe mental disease or defect", he "was unable to appreciate the nature and quality or the wrongfulness of his acts". The volitional limb was dropped, the illness had to be severe, the burden of proof was moved to the defence, which must now prove insanity by clear and convincing evidence, and experts were barred from stating the ultimate conclusion. Some states went further. Michigan had already introduced, in 1975, an alternative verdict of guilty but mentally ill (GBMI), under which the accused is convicted and sentenced like any other offender, with treatment to be provided while he is in custody; several states followed after Hinckley. Research found that people given a GBMI verdict were more likely to be imprisoned, and received longer sentences, than defendants who pleaded insanity, and were no more likely to receive treatment than other mentally ill prisoners. Idaho, Kansas, Montana and Utah abolished the insanity defence as a separate defence altogether, and in Kahler v. Kansas (2020) the Supreme Court held that a state does not violate due process by refusing to excuse a defendant on the ground that mental illness left him unable to tell that his act was morally wrong (Kansas still lets evidence of illness show that the accused lacked the intent the crime requires).

A related idea, diminished capacity, works differently. It does not excuse the accused completely; it allows evidence of mental abnormality to show that he did not form the specific intent a particular crime requires, so that he is convicted of a lesser offence (manslaughter rather than murder, for example). English law has a statutory partial defence of diminished responsibility to a charge of murder; Indian law has no such general defence.

Table 10.25 The main tests of criminal responsibility.
TestYear and placeQuestion askedStatus
M'Naghten rules1843, England (House of Lords)Did a disease of the mind stop him knowing the nature and quality of the act, or that it was wrong?England; many US states; the basis of section 84 IPC and section 22 BNS
Irresistible impulse1887, Alabama (Parsons v. State)Even if he knew it was wrong, could he not stop himself?Added to M'Naghten in some states; not part of Indian law
Durham (product) rule1954, District of Columbia (Bazelon)Was the act the product of mental disease or defect?Abandoned in 1972 (Brawner)
ALI (Model Penal Code) test1962, American Law InstituteDid he lack substantial capacity to appreciate wrongfulness or to conform his conduct?Widely used until Hinckley; still used in some states
Insanity Defense Reform Act1984, US federal courtsBecause of severe mental disease, was he unable to appreciate the nature and quality or wrongfulness of his acts?Federal law; cognitive only; burden on the defence
Guilty but mentally ill1975, MichiganA verdict, not a test: guilty, but mentally ill at the timeSome states; sentence served as usual

Behind the outrage lay a set of beliefs about the insanity defence that turn out to be false, and the research that tested them is a model of how psychology can correct the law's assumptions.

Key study: the myths of the insanity defence (Silver, Cirincione and Steadman, 1994)

Eric Silver, Carmen Cirincione and Henry Steadman compared what the American public believed about the insanity defence with what actually happened in a large sample of American cases. The public estimated that 37 percent of felony indictments ended in an insanity plea; the true figure was 0.9 percent, fewer than one in a hundred. The public thought that 44 percent of insanity pleas succeeded; the true figure was 26 percent. The public believed that a quarter of those acquitted were simply set free; in fact about 85 percent were sent to a mental hospital. And the public guessed that acquittees were confined for about 22 months, when the average was about 32 months for all crimes and about 76 months for murder. The conclusion: the insanity defence is rarely used, usually fails, and when it succeeds it often leads to longer confinement than a prison sentence would have. The limitation is that the data are American, but the pattern has been found repeatedly: being found not guilty by reason of insanity is not "getting away with it".

Exam trap

Match each test to its key word. M'Naghten (1843): know the nature and quality of the act, or that it was wrong (cognitive). Irresistible impulse (1887): could not control (volitional). Durham (1954): act was the product of mental disease. ALI (1962): lacks substantial capacity to appreciate wrongfulness or to conform. IDRA (1984): back to cognitive only, after Hinckley. Hinckley was acquitted under the ALI test, not M'Naghten. Guilty but mentally ill is a verdict of guilt, not an acquittal. Insanity is a legal, not a medical, concept. M'Naghten shot Drummond, Peel's secretary, not Peel.

Unsoundness of mind in Indian law

India's rule is older than every American test except M'Naghten itself. The Indian Penal Code (IPC), drafted by the first Law Commission under Thomas Babington Macaulay (1800 to 1859) and enacted in 1860, wrote the M'Naghten rules into its chapter of general exceptions, seventeen years after the House of Lords had framed them. Since 1 July 2024 the IPC has been replaced by the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023 (BNS), and the old section 84 is now section 22, under the same heading, "Act of a person of unsound mind", with unchanged words: "Nothing is an offence which is done by a person who, at the time of doing it, by reason of unsoundness of mind, is incapable of knowing the nature of the act, or that he is doing what is either wrong or contrary to law."

Take the section phrase by phrase. "Unsoundness of mind" is the law's term, not a diagnosis; it can cover psychosis, severe intellectual disability, delirium and other conditions, and the law does not list them. "At the time of doing it" fixes the moment that matters: not the accused's state at trial, and not his history in general, though both are evidence of it. "Incapable of knowing the nature of the act, or that he is doing what is either wrong or contrary to law" is the M'Naghten test, with the addition of the words "contrary to law". The test is cognitive. There is no volitional limb, so an irresistible impulse is not a defence in India, and there is no general partial defence of diminished responsibility.

Three principles from the case law guide how the section is applied. First, the courts are concerned with legal insanity, not medical insanity: not every person with a mental illness is exempt, but only one whose illness made him incapable of the knowledge the section describes at the time of the act. Second, the burden of proof. In Dahyabhai Chhaganbhai Thakker v. State of Gujarat (decided on 19 March 1964), in which a man had killed his wife, inflicting many wounds, and pleaded insanity, the Supreme Court explained that the prosecution's general burden of proving the offence never shifts, but because the law presumes that the accused does not fall within an exception, he must establish his unsoundness of mind; he need not do so beyond reasonable doubt, because the burden on him is no higher than that resting on a party to civil proceedings (a preponderance of probabilities). The Court added that even if he fails to establish the defence, evidence about his mental state may still raise a reasonable doubt about whether he had the intention the offence requires, and then he is entitled to be acquitted. (Dahyabhai himself did not succeed: his conviction for murder and life sentence were upheld.) Third, the courts look closely at the accused's behaviour before, during and after the act: motive, preparation, concealment, flight and what he said afterwards. A man who hides the weapon and runs away is usually taken to have known that what he did was wrong or illegal.

What happens after an acquittal on this ground is also set out in the BNSS. The court's finding must state specifically whether the accused committed the act (section 373, formerly section 334 of the Code), and a person who did commit it is then detained in safe custody or handed over to a relative or friend who gives security that he will be looked after and prevented from harming himself or others (section 374, formerly section 335). Detention in a mental health establishment follows the rules under the Mental Healthcare Act 2017. For people with mental illness who are already in prison, whether awaiting trial or convicted, section 103 of the Mental Healthcare Act provides that an order admitting such a prisoner for treatment is met by transfer to the psychiatric ward of the prison's medical wing or, where the prison has no such ward, by transfer to a mental health establishment with the prior permission of the Mental Health Review Board; the prison's medical officer must send the Board a quarterly report, and every State and Union Territory must set up a mental health establishment in the medical wing of at least one prison. Mental illness that develops after conviction can matter even at the final stage: in Accused X v. State of Maharashtra (decided on 12 April 2019) the Supreme Court held that severe post-conviction mental illness is a mitigating factor that an appellate court must weigh before confirming a death sentence, and commuted to imprisonment for the rest of his life the death sentence of a prisoner found to have developed severe mental illness during his long years on death row.

Return to Raghav. Once fit, he pleaded unsoundness of mind under section 22. The question now was his state of mind on the morning of the assault, and it had to be reconstructed after the event, which is the hardest task in forensic assessment. The psychologist read the hospital records of his earlier admissions, interviewed his mother and two neighbours about the days before the assault, obtained the police account of what he said when arrested ("I had to stop him before he poisoned my mother"), and assessed his current mental state and his effort and honesty on testing. The picture was of a relapse of schizophrenia over three weeks, with a fixed persecutory delusion. Raghav knew that he was hitting a man with a rod, so he knew the nature of the act. The contested question was whether he was capable of knowing that it was wrong or contrary to law. The M'Naghten judges' answer on delusions suggests one way to think about it: if Raghav truly believed that his mother was about to be killed, then on the facts as he believed them he was defending her. The psychologist's report described the illness, the delusion and its timing, and his behaviour (he did not run, hide the rod or deny the act); the legal conclusion was left to the court.

Clinical link

A mental state at the time of the offence assessment rests on many sources, not on one interview: the account of the accused (taken more than once, to check consistency), informants, police and witness statements, medical and prison records, the timeline of symptoms before and after the act, and tests of symptom validity. Be honest about its limits: you are reconstructing a past state of mind, and your opinion is an inference. Write for a judge, not for a colleague: define terms, separate what you observed from what you were told and from what you infer, and explain how the clinical findings bear on each element of the legal test without claiming to decide it.

Exam trap

Section 84 IPC is now section 22 of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023 (in force 1 July 2024), with the same words. It follows the M'Naghten rules: it is cognitive, uses the phrase unsoundness of mind (not "insanity"), refers to the time of the act, and adds "or contrary to law". It has no irresistible impulse limb and India has no general diminished responsibility defence. The court applies legal, not medical, insanity; the accused bears the burden of establishing it (Dahyabhai, 1964), but not beyond reasonable doubt: a preponderance of probabilities, as in a civil case, is enough. The Code of Criminal Procedure is now the BNSS, and the Indian Evidence Act is now the Bharatiya Sakshya Adhiniyam 2023, both also from 1 July 2024.

Risk assessment, malingering and testimony

Courts, parole boards and forensic hospitals constantly ask about the future: is this person likely to be violent again? Section 2.7 has already taught the core of violence risk assessment: the MacArthur study, Monahan's finding that unaided long-term clinical predictions were right at most one time in three, the three approaches of unstructured judgement, actuarial tools such as the VRAG and structured professional judgement with the HCR-20, and the rule that past violence is the best single predictor of future violence. Here three forensic additions are needed.

The first is the history of the argument. In 1954 Paul Everett Meehl (1920 to 2003) published Clinical versus Statistical Prediction, in which he reviewed about twenty studies that had pitted clinicians' judgements against simple statistical formulas for predicting outcomes such as success on parole or response to treatment. In almost every study the formula did as well as or better than the clinician. Later reviews of many more studies have found the same: actuarial prediction, combining a fixed set of factors by an explicit rule, is rarely beaten by clinical prediction, the clinician's informal weighing of the same information. Forensic risk assessment grew directly out of Meehl's challenge, and structured professional judgement is the field's attempt to keep the reliability of the formula while allowing the clinician to plan how the risk will be managed.

The second addition is a group of specialised tools. The best known of them measures psychopathy, a personality pattern of callousness, lack of remorse, superficial charm, manipulation and impulsive, antisocial behaviour: it is the Psychopathy Checklist-Revised (PCL-R) of the Canadian psychologist Robert D. Hare, a 20-item rating made from an interview and file review. High PCL-R scores predict violent reoffending, which is why the PCL-R carries great weight in the VRAG, but the label is powerful and easily misused, especially with adolescents, whose personalities are still forming. For sexual offending, actuarial scales such as the Static-99 are used, alongside assessment of the dynamic factors that treatment can change. The instruments themselves belong to the Assessment book. Third, modern practice speaks of risk formulation rather than prediction: instead of a yes-or-no verdict, the report states how likely harm is, of what kind, against whom, in what circumstances, how soon, and, above all, what would reduce it.

Every forensic evaluation must also consider malingering: the intentional production of false or grossly exaggerated physical or psychological symptoms, motivated by an external incentive such as avoiding prosecution or punishment, obtaining compensation, avoiding military duty or obtaining drugs. Malingering is not a mental disorder; the DSM lists it among other conditions that may be a focus of clinical attention. In the clinic it is rare, but in forensic settings, where so much depends on the findings, it must always be considered, and evaluators look for symptoms that are improbable, rare, inconsistent with each other or with observed behaviour, or that appear only when the person is being observed. The structured interviews and symptom validity tests used to detect it are described in the Assessment book. Remember the distinction drawn in Section 9.1: in malingering the motive is an external incentive; in factitious disorder, including factitious disorder imposed on another, the person deliberately produces symptoms without any obvious external reward, in order to occupy the sick role.

Finally, the psychologist must be able to explain all this in court. A good forensic report answers the question that was asked, in plain language; separates the facts observed from the information reported by others and from the expert's inferences; states the methods used and their limits; and considers alternative explanations. Under cross-examination the expert's best protection is to have stayed within what the evidence supports. Research on eyewitness memory, the other great contribution of psychology to the courtroom (including Elizabeth Loftus's work on misleading post-event information), is taught in the General Psychology book, and the suggestibility of child witnesses is discussed in Section 10.6.

Exam trap

Meehl (1954) showed that statistical (actuarial) prediction equals or beats clinical prediction. The PCL-R is Robert Hare's; the HCR-20 is a structured professional judgement scheme (Section 2.7); the VRAG is actuarial. Malingering = deliberate symptoms for an external incentive, and it is not a mental disorder; factitious disorder = deliberate symptoms with no obvious external reward, to occupy the sick role. A nurse who secretly injects patients with insulin to cause emergencies, for the satisfaction of the attention, shows factitious disorder imposed on another, not malingering (there is no external gain), not conversion disorder (in which symptoms are not produced deliberately, and are the person's own), and not, on this evidence alone, antisocial personality disorder (a pervasive pattern of disregard for and violation of others' rights since the age of fifteen; the disorders themselves are in the Psychiatric Disorders book).

Child custody evaluations

When parents separate and cannot agree about where their children will live, a family court must decide, and it may ask a psychologist to help. The legal standard almost everywhere is the best interests of the child: the court's decision must be governed by what is best for the child's welfare, not by the rights or wishes of either parent. Earlier doctrines treated children as the father's property, and later the "tender years" doctrine presumed that young children belonged with their mothers; the best-interests standard replaced both with a question about the particular child. In Indian law, the Guardians and Wards Act 1890 and, for Hindus, the Hindu Minority and Guardianship Act 1956 govern custody. Section 6(a) of the 1956 Act provides that the custody of a minor who has not completed five years of age shall ordinarily be with the mother, and section 13 makes the welfare of the minor the paramount consideration, so that the welfare of the child overrides even the presumption about young children. The Family Courts Act 1984 allows family courts to use counsellors to help families settle such disputes.

The APA's Guidelines for Child Custody Evaluations in Family Law Proceedings (first issued in 1994 for divorce proceedings and revised under the present title in 2010) describe how the work should be done. The child's psychological best interests and well-being are paramount; the evaluator is impartial and must not be the therapist of the child or of either parent; and the evaluation focuses on three things: the parenting attributes of each parent, the psychological needs of the child, and the fit between them. In practice the evaluator interviews each parent about the child's history and their own; observes each parent with the child, often at home; interviews the child in a way that suits his or her age, without forcing the child to choose between parents; gathers information from teachers, doctors and relatives; reviews records; and, where it helps answer a specific question, tests the parents. Particular weight is given to allegations of abuse or domestic violence, to the continuity of the child's attachments, schooling and friendships, to keeping siblings together, and to each parent's willingness to support the child's relationship with the other.

Consider a case. Farida, a 34-year-old schoolteacher in Hyderabad, separated from her husband after eight years of marriage. Each sought custody of their six-year-old son, and the husband alleged that Farida's treatment for depression made her unfit to care for him. The evaluator found that Farida's depression had been treated and was in remission, that she had been the boy's main caregiver since birth, that the boy was securely attached to both parents but turned to his mother when distressed, and that his school reports were good. The father was a warm and involved parent too, but his long working hours meant that the boy would in practice be cared for by his grandparents. The recommendation was that the boy live with Farida, with generous regular time with his father, and that both parents attend sessions with the court's counsellor to agree on how they would make decisions together. Notice what the evaluator did not do: treat a psychiatric history as proof of unfitness. The question is always how a parent actually functions as a parent.

Custody disputes also produce claims that one parent has turned the child against the other. The American psychiatrist Richard A. Gardner (1931 to 2003) proposed a "parental alienation syndrome" in 1985, but it is not recognised as a disorder in the DSM and has been strongly criticised for lacking scientific support and for being used to discredit children's and mothers' reports of abuse. A careful evaluator asks why a child resists contact, which may be alienation, may be the child's own reaction to a parent's behaviour, or may be both.

Exam trap

The custody standard is the best interests (welfare) of the child, not the rights of the parents. Under the Hindu Minority and Guardianship Act 1956, section 6(a) says a child under five shall ordinarily be in the mother's custody, but section 13 makes the child's welfare paramount. The custody evaluator must not also be the family's therapist. "Parental alienation syndrome" (Gardner, 1985) is not a DSM diagnosis.

Juvenile justice

Children who break the law have been treated differently from adults since the first juvenile court opened in Chicago in 1899, on the principle that a child who offends needs care and correction rather than punishment. Clinical psychology was there almost from the start: in 1909 the psychiatrist William Healy (1869 to 1963) set up the Juvenile Psychopathic Institute in Chicago to study the young people who came before the court, one of the roots of the child guidance movement described in Section 10.1.

India's present law is the Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act 2015, which replaced the Act of 2000 (itself the successor of the Juvenile Justice Act 1986). Its background is the gang rape and murder of a young woman on a bus in Delhi in December 2012. One of the accused was a few months short of eighteen; as a juvenile, he was sentenced in August 2013 to three years in a reform home, the most the law then allowed, and the public outrage that followed led to the new Act, which received the President's assent on 31 December 2015 and came into force on 15 January 2016. Its key ideas are these (Figure 10.14 traces the path of a case):

  • A child is a person who has not completed eighteen years of age. The Act deals with two kinds of children: a child in conflict with law, who is alleged or found to have committed an offence while under eighteen, and a child in need of care and protection, for example a child who is homeless, abused, exploited, working illegally or at risk of child marriage. The first comes before a Juvenile Justice Board, the second before a Child Welfare Committee in each district.
  • The Juvenile Justice Board (section 4) consists of a Metropolitan Magistrate or Judicial Magistrate of the first class with at least three years' experience and two social workers, at least one of them a woman. A social worker may qualify by seven years of work in children's health, education or welfare, or as a practising professional with a degree in child psychology, psychiatry, sociology or law.
  • Offences are graded by punishment: petty offences (maximum punishment up to three years), serious offences (three to seven years) and heinous offences, those with a minimum punishment of seven years' imprisonment or more. (An amendment in 2021 widened the serious category.)
  • For a child aged sixteen or above who is alleged to have committed a heinous offence, the Board conducts a preliminary assessment under section 15 of the child's mental and physical capacity to commit the offence, ability to understand its consequences, and the circumstances in which it was allegedly committed. The Board may take the assistance of experienced psychologists, psycho-social workers or other experts. An explanation in the section says that the assessment is not a trial. It must be completed within three months.
  • If the Board decides that the child need not be tried as an adult, it holds the inquiry itself and may pass the orders listed in section 18: advice or admonition with counselling for the child and parents, group counselling, community service, a fine, probation for up to three years, or a stay of up to three years in a special home, together with directions to attend school, vocational training, a therapeutic centre or a de-addiction programme.
  • If the Board decides that there is a need for trial as an adult, it transfers the case to the Children's Court (section 18(3)). That court may itself decide either way (section 19). A child found guilty is kept in a place of safety until the age of twenty-one, with education, skill training, counselling, behaviour modification and psychiatric support; at twenty-one the court reviews whether the young person has reformed and may release him on conditions or send him to jail for the rest of the term (section 20).
  • No child may be sentenced to death or to life imprisonment without the possibility of release (section 21). While an inquiry is pending, a child is kept in an observation home, not in a police lock-up or jail.

Why should a seventeen-year-old be treated differently from an eighteen-year-old at all? Developmental science gives the answer (taught in the Developmental book). The prefrontal systems that support planning, impulse control and the weighing of long-term consequences go on maturing into the early twenties, while sensitivity to reward and to the presence of peers peaks in mid-adolescence, a pattern described in the dual systems model of Laurence Steinberg (Section 10.6). Adolescents therefore take more risks, especially in groups, and most of them grow out of offending as they mature. Arguments of this kind were accepted by the United States Supreme Court in Roper v. Simmons (2005), which abolished the death penalty for crimes committed under eighteen.

The preliminary assessment puts the psychologist in a difficult position, because "capacity to commit" an offence has no agreed clinical meaning; almost any physically healthy sixteen-year-old is physically capable of committing a crime. In Barun Chandra Thakur v. Master Bholu (2022), a case arising from the killing of a seven-year-old schoolboy in Gurugram, the Supreme Court held that the assessment calls for a holistic evaluation; that the child's mental capacity to commit the offence and his ability to understand its consequences are different questions, which the Board and the court below had wrongly treated as one; that a Board with no member who is a practising professional with a degree in child psychology or child psychiatry must take the help of experienced psychologists, psycho-social workers or other experts (the "may" in section 15 operates as "must"); and it left it open to the Central Government and the national and state commissions for protection of child rights to issue guidelines for the assessment. Consider how it is done. Tushar, aged seventeen, was arrested in Pune after a fight between two groups of boys; he was alleged to have stabbed a boy from the other group, who died. The psychologist assessing him for the Board interviewed Tushar on three occasions, assessed his intellectual functioning (in the borderline range), screened for mental disorder (he met criteria for conduct disorder and was using cannabis daily), took a developmental history from his mother (his father had left when he was four; he had dropped out of school in the ninth standard), and spoke with his former teacher. On the question of understanding consequences, Tushar knew that a stab wound could kill and that stabbing someone was a serious crime, but his account showed how strongly he had been carried along by older boys in the group. The report described these findings, their bearing on each element of section 15, and the treatment and educational needs that would apply whichever way the Board decided. The interventions for conduct problems, including multisystemic therapy, are described in Section 10.4; the protection of children who are themselves victims, under the POCSO Act 2012, is covered in Section 10.6.

Exam trap

Under the Juvenile Justice Act 2015 (in force 15 January 2016), only a child aged 16 to 18 accused of a heinous offence (minimum punishment of seven years or more) can be considered for trial as an adult, and only after the Board's preliminary assessment under section 15 of mental and physical capacity, ability to understand the consequences and the circumstances; experienced psychologists may assist, and the assessment is not a trial. The Board, not the psychologist, decides. No child may be given death or life imprisonment without release (section 21). A child in conflict with law goes to the Juvenile Justice Board; a child in need of care and protection to the Child Welfare Committee. Observation home = during inquiry; special home = after a finding; place of safety = a secure facility for older children, including those tried as adults (never a police lock-up or jail).

Correctional psychology and offender rehabilitation

The term correctional psychology refers to the practice of psychology in prisons, probation services and other parts of the criminal justice system that hold or supervise offenders. A prison psychologist screens new prisoners for mental illness, intellectual disability and suicide risk (suicide is far more common in prison than outside, especially in the first days of custody); manages crises; treats mental illness and substance use; runs programmes aimed at offending itself; assesses risk for parole and release; trains staff; and does research. Behind all of this lies a question that the field once answered with despair: can anything be done to make offenders less likely to offend again?

Key study: Martinson and "nothing works" (1974)

In the late 1960s the State of New York commissioned a review of every evaluation of offender rehabilitation it could find. Douglas Lipton, Robert Martinson (1927 to 1979) and Judith Wilks examined 231 studies published between 1945 and 1967, covering education, vocational training, counselling, psychotherapy, changes to the institutional environment, medical treatment and supervision in the community. The state was slow to release the report, and in 1974 Martinson published its conclusions on his own in an article in The Public Interest titled "What works? Questions and answers about prison reform". His verdict was that, with few and isolated exceptions, the rehabilitative efforts reported so far had had no appreciable effect on reoffending. The message was soon shortened to "nothing works", and it suited both those who wanted harsher punishment and those who distrusted coerced treatment. The full report appeared as a book in 1975. Critics pointed out that many of the studies were too weak to detect any effect, and that some programmes had worked. In 1979 Martinson himself published a revision, "New findings, new views", conceding that some programmes did reduce reoffending; he died later that year. The lesson for clinicians is that a negative verdict based on poor studies is as dangerous as a positive one.

The revival began in Canada. In 1979 Paul Gendreau and Robert Ross answered Martinson with a review of successful programmes pointedly titled "Effective correctional treatment: bibliotherapy for cynics". In 1990 Donald A. Andrews and his colleagues, among them James Bonta, Robert Hoge, Gendreau and Francis Cullen, published a meta-analysis titled "Does correctional treatment work?" that produced 154 effect sizes (phi coefficients) from studies of juvenile and adult offenders. Criminal sanctions without rehabilitative services did not reduce reoffending (mean phi of minus 0.07); nor did services that ignored the principles of effective rehabilitation (minus 0.06). But "appropriate" treatment, which followed those principles, clearly reduced reoffending (mean phi of 0.30, against 0.13 for services of unspecified type). And the setting mattered: appropriate treatment worked in diversion, community and residential programmes alike, but its effects were weaker in residential settings than in the community.

The principles became the risk-need-responsivity model (RNR), first set out by Andrews, Bonta and Hoge in 1990 and developed in Andrews and Bonta's textbook The Psychology of Criminal Conduct. It has three principles (Table 10.26). The risk principle says that the intensity of treatment should match the offender's risk of reoffending: intensive services for high-risk offenders, minimal ones for low-risk offenders. Intensive programmes for low-risk offenders can actually make them worse, by mixing them with high-risk offenders and pulling them away from the jobs, families and friends that were keeping them out of trouble. The need principle says that treatment should target criminogenic needs, the dynamic (changeable) risk factors that are directly linked to offending, rather than problems that do not drive offending, such as low self-esteem, anxiety or vague emotional distress. The responsivity principle says that treatment should be delivered in the way offenders learn best: in general, with structured cognitive-behavioural and social learning methods (general responsivity), and in particular, tailored to the person's motivation, ability, learning style, gender and culture (specific responsivity).

Andrews and Bonta identified eight major risk and need factors, the "central eight". The first four, the "big four", are the strongest predictors:

  • A history of antisocial behaviour (the only static factor of the eight: it cannot be changed, only taken into account).
  • An antisocial personality pattern (impulsivity, restless aggression, callousness, a taste for risk).
  • Antisocial cognition: attitudes, values, beliefs and rationalisations that support crime ("he deserved it", "everyone takes bribes").
  • Antisocial associates: friends and companions who offend, and isolation from those who do not.
  • The remaining four: family or marital problems, problems at school or work, lack of rewarding prosocial leisure activities, and substance misuse.
Table 10.26 The risk-need-responsivity model of offender rehabilitation (Andrews, Bonta and colleagues).
PrincipleWhat it saysIn practice
RiskMatch the intensity of treatment to the risk of reoffendingAssess risk first; concentrate resources on higher-risk offenders; do not over-treat the low-risk
NeedTarget criminogenic (dynamic) needsWork on antisocial attitudes, associates, substance use, employment, family; not on self-esteem alone
Responsivity (general)Use methods offenders learn fromStructured cognitive-behavioural and social learning programmes
Responsivity (specific)Fit the programme to the personAdjust for motivation, ability, literacy, gender, culture, mental health

Put the research together and you get a description of what successful rehabilitation programmes look like. They are community-based where possible, because offending happens in the community and new skills must be practised where the temptations are. They are cognitive-behavioural and skills-based, teaching problem-solving, self-control, anger management and prosocial thinking, as in the Reasoning and Rehabilitation programme of Robert Ross and Elizabeth Fabiano (1985). They target criminogenic needs and concentrate on higher-risk offenders. They are structured, delivered as designed by trained staff who model prosocial behaviour and combine warmth with firm, fair limits, and they are followed by aftercare. For young offenders, interventions that work with the family and the neighbourhood, such as multisystemic therapy (Section 10.4), have the strongest evidence. What does not work is just as instructive: punishment and deterrence alone, military-style boot camps and "Scared Straight" programmes, in which delinquent youths visit prisons to be frightened by inmates. A Cochrane review by Anthony Petrosino and colleagues found that Scared Straight programmes actually increased later offending compared with doing nothing.

The RNR model has been criticised for concentrating on deficits and risks. The Good Lives Model of the New Zealand psychologist Tony Ward (2002) argues that offenders, like everyone else, seek basic human goods (relationships, competence, autonomy, meaning, pleasure) and that offending is often a harmful way of pursuing them; rehabilitation should therefore help offenders build a way of life that meets those needs without harming others. Most programmes now combine the two: RNR tells you whom to treat and what to target, and the Good Lives Model helps with motivation and the shape of a life after release. A wider movement, therapeutic jurisprudence, associated with the American law professors David Wexler and Bruce Winick, asks how the law itself can be applied in ways that help rather than harm the people it touches; drug courts and mental health courts, which supervise treatment instead of imposing ordinary sentences, are its best-known products.

Indian context

Indian prisons hold far more undertrial prisoners than convicts, so correctional work in India often means caring for people who have not yet been tried. The Mental Healthcare Act 2017 (section 103) provides for prisoners with mental illness to be treated in the psychiatric ward of the prison's medical wing or, with the Mental Health Review Board's permission, in a mental health establishment, and requires every State and Union Territory to set up a mental health establishment in at least one prison. India also gave the world a well-known correctional experiment: from 1993, when Kiran Bedi was Inspector General of Prisons, Delhi's Tihar Jail offered ten-day courses of Vipassana meditation in the tradition taught by S.
