N. Goenka, including one course for about a thousand prisoners, an experiment made famous by the 1997 documentary Doing Time, Doing Vipassana. The courses were welcomed by many prisoners and staff, but controlled evidence about their effect on reoffending is thin, which is exactly the question the RNR research teaches you to ask.

The criminal justice system also has to care for those whom offenders harm. The word victimology is usually credited to the lawyer Benjamin Mendelsohn in the 1940s, and the criminologist Hans von Hentig's The Criminal and His Victim (1948) began the systematic study of victims. Early victimology asked how victims contributed to crimes against them (victim precipitation), an approach later criticised as victim-blaming. Today victimology is the study of victims and their needs: the psychological effects of crime, secondary victimisation (the further harm caused by insensitive police, hospital and court procedures), victim support, compensation, and restorative justice, which brings victims and offenders together, when both agree, to repair the harm. The child-friendly procedures that the POCSO Act requires for child victims (Section 10.6) are an example of victimology in law.

Exam trap

Asked for "the qualities of successful rehabilitation programmes to treat offenders" (RML 2020), choose being community-based. Cognitive interviews and offender profiling are investigative tools used to solve crimes (Section 10.9.1), not features of treatment, and a focus on the relationship with parents is not a defining principle of effective programmes (family problems are only one of the central eight, and family-based work matters mainly for juveniles). Martinson (1974) = "nothing works"; Andrews and colleagues (1990) and the RNR model = "what works". Risk = who to treat, need = what to treat, responsivity = how to treat. Self-esteem is a non-criminogenic need. Scared Straight increases offending.

Neighbouring specialties: neuropsychology and rehabilitation psychology

Two other specialties often work alongside forensic and clinical psychologists. The first, clinical neuropsychology, is the assessment and treatment of people whose behaviour or cognition is affected by brain injury or disease: head injury, stroke, epilepsy, dementia, tumours and the effects of alcohol and toxins. The clinical neuropsychologist measures memory, attention, language, perception and executive functions with standardised batteries, describes the pattern of strengths and deficits, relates it to what is known about brain-behaviour relationships, and advises on diagnosis, capacity, return to work and rehabilitation. In forensic settings neuropsychologists assess people claiming compensation after head injuries, which is why tests of effort and symptom validity are a routine part of their work. The tests and batteries belong to the Assessment book and the underlying brain science to the Physiological book. The second, rehabilitation psychology, helps people with chronic illness, injury or disability achieve the greatest possible independence and participation; its methods, including the restorative and compensatory approaches to cognitive rehabilitation after brain injury, are described in Section 9.9.

Exam trap

Do not confuse the specialties by setting. Forensic psychology answers legal questions; correctional psychology works with offenders in custody or under supervision; clinical neuropsychology assesses brain-behaviour relationships; rehabilitation psychology maximises function after illness or disability; industrial-organisational psychology deals with the workplace; counselling psychology with the normal-range problems of living (Section 10.8). A neuropsychologist may work in a court, and a forensic psychologist in a prison, but the specialty is defined by the kind of question it answers.

10.10 Creative, Adjunct and Digital TherapiesLower yield · 1 Q

Almost every therapy in this book works through words. The client talks, the therapist listens, and change happens in conversation. But some experiences have no words yet: the terror of a child who was abused before she could speak, the grief of a woman who cannot say her dead son's name, the inner world of a man with chronic schizophrenia who has long since stopped trying to explain himself. Other clients have words but cannot use them with a stranger in an office. For them, clinicians have borrowed from the arts (painting, music, dance, drama, literature), from the bond between people and animals, from India's own traditions of yoga and meditation, and, most recently, from the smartphone. These approaches are usually called adjunct therapies, because in most clinical settings they are added to, not substituted for, the main treatment. The creative ones are often grouped as the arts therapies or expressive therapies; in several countries art, music, drama and dance movement therapy are separate professions with their own postgraduate training. This section describes each of them, what they claim, and what the evidence does and does not show, and ends with the promise and the ethical risks of digital mental health.

Art therapy: Naumburg, Kramer and the image

In art therapy the client makes images (drawings, paintings, sculptures, collages) in the presence of a trained art therapist, and the making of the image and the relationship around it are the means of change. The term was coined in 1942 by the British artist Adrian Hill (1895 to 1977), who discovered the value of drawing and painting while recovering from tuberculosis in a sanatorium and went on to teach art to other patients; he described the work in Art Versus Illness (1945), praising its power of "completely engrossing the mind (as well as the fingers)". In the United States, two women gave the field its two great theories, and they disagreed.

Margaret Naumburg (1890 to 1983), a New York educator who in 1914 had founded the progressive school later renamed the Walden School, worked from 1941 to 1947 at the New York State Psychiatric Institute, using art with disturbed children and adults. She called her approach dynamically oriented art therapy and grounded it in psychoanalysis. For Naumburg, spontaneous art is symbolic speech: the unconscious, which censors words, expresses itself more freely in images. The client is encouraged to draw spontaneously and then to free-associate to the picture; the therapist withholds interpretation so that the client can discover its meaning, and the images become a bridge to talking, to insight and to the transference. Art here is a tool in therapy, a royal road to the unconscious alongside dreams.

Edith Kramer (1916 to 2014), a painter born in Vienna who had studied as a girl with the Bauhaus artist Friedl Dicker-Brandeis, worked for seven years with delinquent and disturbed boys aged eight to thirteen at the Wiltwyck School for Boys in New York, and described the work in Art Therapy in a Children's Community (1958) and Art as Therapy with Children (1971). Kramer argued that the healing lies in the creative process itself. Drawing on Freud's concept of sublimation, the transformation of primitive impulses into socially valued activity, she held that when a child turns rage or fear into a well-made picture, the act of giving it form contains and transforms the feeling. Interpretation is less important than helping the child make good art, and the therapist acts as a "third hand", helping the child's own artistic process (mixing a colour, steadying a clay figure) without taking it over. Kramer called this art as therapy, against Naumburg's art in therapy, and the two phrases still mark the main division in the field. The British Association of Art Therapists was founded in 1964 and the American Art Therapy Association in 1969.

See how a session works. Leela, a 41-year-old woman in Kochi, had not spoken about the death of her teenage son in a road accident for a year; in her sessions with a clinical psychologist she answered questions politely but said nothing about him. She agreed to join an art therapy group on the ward. In her third session she painted a closed door, black, with a small yellow shape at its foot. Asked about it, she said only that the yellow was his school shoe, which she had never put away. Over the following weeks the paintings changed, the door half open, a figure beside it, and in the sixth session she began to talk, for the first time, about the morning he left the house. A Naumburg-style therapist would emphasise the symbolic meaning of the door and the shoe and Leela's associations to them; a Kramer-style therapist would emphasise that the act of painting allowed her to give form to what she could not yet say. In practice most art therapists do both.

Art therapy is used with children (for whom it overlaps with the play therapies of Section 10.2, especially sandplay), with survivors of trauma and abuse, with people with psychosis, in dementia care and in palliative care. Its evidence base, however, is small. The most important test is described below. Do not confuse art therapy with projective drawing tests such as the House-Tree-Person or the Draw-a-Person, which use drawings to assess personality and are described in the Assessment book; art therapists generally avoid reading fixed meanings into features of a drawing.

Key study: the MATISSE trial of group art therapy in schizophrenia (Crawford and colleagues, 2012)

Small studies had suggested that art therapy helps people with schizophrenia, especially with negative symptoms, and British guidance on psychosis had encouraged it. Mike Crawford and colleagues tested it properly. At 15 sites across the United Kingdom, 417 adults with schizophrenia were randomly assigned to twelve months of weekly 90-minute group art therapy plus standard care, twelve months of weekly activity groups (games, outings and other activities with no art materials) plus standard care, or standard care alone. Raters who did not know the group assignment measured global functioning and symptoms two years after randomisation. There were no differences: art therapy did no better than activity groups or standard care on functioning, symptoms or social functioning, and attendance at both kinds of group was low (almost 40 percent of those offered art therapy never attended a session). A 2019 meta-analysis of nine randomised trials (Laws and Conway) later found a small to moderate effect of art therapies on negative symptoms, but only in the lower-quality trials; it did not appear in trials whose raters were blind. The conclusion is not that art therapy is useless for everyone, but that its claims for schizophrenia have not survived a large, well-controlled test.

Exam trap

Adrian Hill coined "art therapy" (1942). Margaret Naumburg: psychoanalytic, art as symbolic speech, free association to the image, art in therapy; often called the mother of American art therapy. Edith Kramer: art as therapy, healing through the creative process and sublimation, the therapist's "third hand". Do not swap them. Projective drawings (House-Tree-Person, Draw-a-Person) are assessment tools, not art therapy.

Music therapy

By music therapy clinicians mean the use of music and musical activity, within a therapeutic relationship with a trained music therapist, to reach clinical goals: expressing feelings, building communication and social interaction, reducing anxiety, improving mood, or maintaining memory and movement. It takes two main forms. In active music therapy the client makes music: improvising on drums, xylophones or piano with the therapist, singing, writing songs. In receptive music therapy the client listens to live or recorded music, chosen and used by the therapist, and responds to it with relaxation, imagery, movement or discussion.

Music as medicine is ancient, but music therapy as a profession grew from the work of musicians in American veterans' hospitals during and after the two world wars. The first university degree programmes in music therapy opened in 1944 (Michigan State College was among them), the National Association for Music Therapy was formed in 1950, and the American Music Therapy Association was created by merger in 1998. Two approaches are often named. In the 1950s and 1960s the American composer and pianist Paul Nordoff (1909 to 1977) and the British special educator Clive Robbins (1927 to 2011) developed creative music therapy (now called Nordoff-Robbins music therapy), in which the therapist improvises at the piano with a child with autism, intellectual disability or other disabilities, meeting and extending whatever sound or rhythm the child produces, on the view that a "music child" capable of responding lies within every child. In the 1970s the American music therapist Helen Lindquist Bonny (1921 to 2010) developed Guided Imagery and Music, a receptive method in which a relaxed client listens to sequences of classical music and describes the images, memories and feelings that arise, with the therapist as guide.

Music therapy should be distinguished from music medicine, in which doctors or nurses play recorded music to patients, for example to reduce anxiety before surgery, without a music therapist or a therapeutic relationship. The evidence for music therapy proper is among the best of the arts therapies. A Cochrane review in 2017 (Aalbers and colleagues) found moderate-quality evidence that music therapy added to usual care produces short-term improvements in depressive symptoms, anxiety and functioning in people with depression. Another Cochrane review in 2017 (Geretsegger and colleagues) found that music therapy added to standard care improves global state, mental state including negative symptoms, social functioning and quality of life in schizophrenia, with effects that depend on the number of sessions and on the quality of the therapy, and the 2022 Cochrane review of music therapy for autistic people (Geretsegger and colleagues) found moderate-certainty evidence that it probably increases the chance of global improvement and likely reduces overall autism severity and improves quality of life, though with no clear effect on social interaction or verbal and non-verbal communication. India has its own tradition, often called raga chikitsa, which holds that particular ragas heal particular ailments; it is culturally meaningful to many clients, but its specific claims have not been tested in rigorous trials. Music is also widely used in dementia care, alongside the approaches described in Section 10.7.

Exam trap

Active music therapy = the client makes music (improvisation, singing, song-writing). Receptive music therapy = the client listens and responds. Nordoff-Robbins = improvisational, with children with disabilities and autism; Guided Imagery and Music = receptive, Helen Bonny. Music therapy needs a trained therapist and a relationship; playing background music in a ward is music medicine, not music therapy.

Dance and movement therapy

As its name suggests, dance movement therapy uses movement and dance, within a therapeutic relationship, to further the emotional, cognitive, social and physical integration of the person. Its starting point is that body and mind are one: posture, gesture and movement express feeling, often more truthfully than words, and changing how one moves can change how one feels and relates. Its founder in the United States was Marian Chace (1896 to 1970), a dancer trained at the Denishawn school of Ruth St Denis and Ted Shawn, who noticed that some of the pupils in her dance classes came less for technique than for the feelings that dancing released. From the early 1940s she worked with psychiatric patients at St Elizabeths Hospital in Washington, many of them withdrawn, mute or severely disturbed, and found that patients who could not be reached in conversation would join a circle of movement. Four ideas sum up her method: body action (helping the patient to move, loosen tension and become aware of the body), symbolism (movement as a way of expressing and working through feelings and memories), the therapeutic movement relationship (the therapist joins and reflects the patient's movements, establishing contact through movement), and rhythmic group activity (shared rhythm organises behaviour and creates a sense of belonging). In 1966 she helped found the American Dance Therapy Association and became its first president.

A second tradition, authentic movement, grew from the work of Mary Starks Whitehouse (1911 to 1979), who called her approach "movement in depth" and drew on Carl Jung's active imagination; her student Janet Adler later developed it into the mover and witness form. The mover, eyes closed, follows inner impulses to move, while a witness watches without judgement, and the two then speak about what was experienced and seen. Many dance movement therapists also use the system of movement analysis developed by the dance theorist Rudolf Laban to describe qualities of movement such as weight, time, space and flow. The approach suits people for whom words are hard: children with autism, survivors of trauma, people with chronic psychosis, older adults with dementia, and patients with medical illness. Indian practitioners have adapted classical and folk dance forms to the same ends. The evidence remains limited. A Cochrane review of dance movement therapy for depression (Bonnie Meekums, Vicky Karkou and Andrea Nelson, 2015) found only three small trials with 147 participants and concluded that the low-quality evidence did not allow any firm conclusion about its effectiveness.

Exam trap

Dance/movement therapy is Marian Chace's (St Elizabeths Hospital; first president of the American Dance Therapy Association, founded 1966). Authentic movement is Mary Whitehouse's, influenced by Jung. Do not credit dance therapy to Moreno (psychodrama) or to Naumburg (art therapy).

Drama therapy and how it differs from psychodrama

The term drama therapy (in British spelling, dramatherapy) means the intentional use of drama and theatre processes, such as role-play, improvisation, storytelling, puppetry, masks, enactment of myths and scripted scenes, to achieve therapeutic goals. It is easy to confuse with psychodrama, which Section 7.11 described: Jacob Levy Moreno's group method in which a protagonist enacts scenes from his or her own life, with auxiliary egos, a director, role reversal and doubling. The two share a belief that acting can heal, but they come from different roots and work differently. Psychodrama was invented by one man, a psychiatrist, and goes straight to the protagonist's real life. Drama therapy grew in Britain and North America from theatre, drama in education and remedial drama, and it often works through fiction and metaphor: a client may play a character in a story, speak through a puppet or a mask, or re-enact a myth, and discover his own life in it at a safer distance. The British drama educator Peter Slade is usually credited with first using the word "dramatherapy", in the 1930s, and the British Association of Dramatherapists was founded in 1977, with Sue Jennings among its founder members.

Three ideas from drama therapy are worth knowing. Jennings described a developmental sequence of dramatic play, embodiment, projection and role: infants first explore the world through the body, then project their feelings onto objects and toys, and finally take on roles, and drama therapy can work at whichever stage a client needs. The American drama therapist Robert Landy built a role method on the idea that personality is a system of roles (the victim, the hero, the helper, the critic) and that therapy helps a person discover, play and balance them; he also stressed aesthetic distance, the point at which a person can feel strongly while still being able to reflect, which drama therapy tries to find by moving the client closer to or further from personal material. Renée Emunah proposed an integrative model in five phases, moving from dramatic play through scene work and role play to a culminating enactment of personal material and finally a closing dramatic ritual. Drama therapy is used in schools, in work with children and adolescents, in psychiatric and forensic hospitals, and with people with learning disabilities; its evidence comes mostly from small studies.

Exam trap

Psychodrama (Moreno, Section 7.11) enacts the protagonist's own life directly, with a director, auxiliaries, role reversal and doubling. Drama therapy is a separate profession that often works through fiction, story, role and metaphor at a safe distance (Jennings, Landy, Emunah). Some revision lists write "Drama therapy: Moreno"; if the options include psychodrama, Moreno belongs with psychodrama, and if a question pairs "drama therapy" with Moreno and offers no drama therapist (Slade, Jennings, Landy, Emunah), the setter means psychodrama. Sociodrama (also Moreno's) enacts the shared problems of a group or community.

Bibliotherapy and expressive writing

The term bibliotherapy means the use of reading material, chosen for its relevance to the reader's situation, to help with emotional or behavioural problems. The idea that books heal is very old. The Greek historian Diodorus Siculus recorded that the royal library of the Egyptian pharaoh Ramses II bore over its entrance the words "healing place of the soul", and Benjamin Rush recommended reading for patients in American hospitals in the early nineteenth century. The word itself was coined by the American essayist and minister Samuel McChord Crothers (1857 to 1927) in an article titled "A Literary Clinic", published in the Atlantic Monthly in September 1916.

There are two kinds. Affective (or creative) bibliotherapy uses fiction, poetry and biography. The reader is thought to move through three stages: identification with a character whose situation resembles his own, catharsis as he lives through the character's feelings, and insight as he sees his own situation in a new light. A teenager reading a novel about a boy whose parents divorce, or an Indian reader who finds Arjuna's despair on the battlefield in the Bhagavad Gita uncannily like his own (Section 8.11), is using literature this way. Cognitive (self-help) bibliotherapy uses structured self-help books that teach the methods of a therapy, usually CBT, step by step, such as David Burns's Feeling Good (1980) for depression or the behavioural programme Control Your Depression by Peter Lewinsohn and colleagues. Bibliotherapy can also be clinical, used by therapists as part of treatment, or developmental, used by teachers, librarians and parents to help ordinary children through ordinary difficulties such as starting school, a new sibling or a death in the family.

Self-help bibliotherapy has a respectable evidence base. In a series of studies at the University of Alabama, Forrest Scogin and his colleagues showed that mildly and moderately depressed older adults who read Feeling Good or Control Your Depression improved more than those waiting for treatment (1989), and that most kept their gains two years later (1990); with depressed adults recruited from the community, minimal-contact cognitive bibliotherapy was clearly better than a waiting list, with gains maintained at three months and reductions in dysfunctional attitudes and negative automatic thoughts (Jamison and Scogin, 1995). Self-help works best for mild to moderate problems, for motivated readers, and when someone checks in on progress; this is guided self-help, the printed ancestor of the guided internet CBT described in Section 5.11.8. In Britain, Books on Prescription schemes allow a general practitioner to "prescribe" an evidence-based self-help book, which the patient borrows from the public library.

Writing can heal as well as reading. In 1986 James W. Pennebaker and Sandra Beall asked college students to write for fifteen minutes on four consecutive days, either about the most traumatic or upsetting experiences of their lives, with their deepest thoughts and feelings, or about trivial topics. Those who wrote about both the facts of their traumas and their feelings were more upset immediately after writing, but made fewer visits to the university health centre in the following months. This expressive writing paradigm has since been tested in hundreds of studies, with small but reliable benefits; journaling, letter-writing (including unsent letters to someone who has died) and poetry therapy are its clinical relatives.

Exam trap

Samuel Crothers coined "bibliotherapy" (1916, "A Literary Clinic"). The three stages of affective bibliotherapy are identification, catharsis and insight. Self-help (cognitive) bibliotherapy, for example with Feeling Good, is effective for mild to moderate depression, especially when guided. Expressive writing about trauma is Pennebaker's (with Beall, 1986).

Animal-assisted and horticultural therapy

In the 1950s the American child psychologist Boris M. Levinson (1907 to 1984) had an unexpected session. A withdrawn child came to his office while Levinson's dog, Jingles, happened to be there; the child, who had barely spoken in therapy, began to talk to the dog, and then, through the dog, to Levinson. Levinson began to use Jingles deliberately as what he called a "co-therapist", and presented the idea at an American Psychological Association meeting and in a paper, "The dog as a 'co-therapist'", published in Mental Hygiene in 1962. Many in his audience laughed, but Levinson persisted, coined the term "pet therapy" in 1964, and set out his methods in Pet-Oriented Child Psychotherapy (1969). He was not entirely the first: the York Retreat, founded in 1796, is said to have kept small animals for its patients, and Sigmund Freud often had his chow Jofi in the room during sessions. But Levinson made the animal a deliberate part of therapy and gave the field its research agenda.

Today a distinction is drawn between animal-assisted activities, informal visits by animals and their handlers to hospitals, old-age homes or schools for comfort and recreation, and animal-assisted therapy, a goal-directed intervention in which a specially trained animal is part of a treatment plan delivered by a professional, with goals set and progress recorded. Dogs are most common, but horses are used in equine-assisted therapy (and in hippotherapy, a physiotherapy in which the horse's movement is used to improve posture and balance). Animals seem to help in several ways: they are non-judgemental, they make contact easier (a "social lubricant" between client and therapist), touching and stroking them can lower arousal, and caring for an animal gives a sense of competence and responsibility. The evidence, however, consists mostly of small studies, often without adequate control groups, and the benefits may owe much to novelty and enthusiasm. There are practical risks too: allergies, bites, infections passed from animals, the welfare of the animal itself, and clients who are afraid of animals or, as many people in India are, wary of dogs for reasons of culture, religion or fear of street dogs and rabies.

In much the same spirit, horticultural therapy uses gardening and plant-based activities, guided by a trained practitioner, to improve physical and mental well-being. Benjamin Rush is often quoted as having observed that the patients in his hospital who worked in its gardens recovered better than those who did not, and many asylums of the nineteenth century had farms and gardens worked by patients, an idea that survives in the occupational and vocational rehabilitation of Section 9.8. Gardening combines physical activity, structure, a sense of achievement and contact with nature, and is widely used in psychiatric rehabilitation, dementia care and work with people with intellectual disability. Table 10.27 brings the creative and adjunct therapies together.

Table 10.27 The creative and adjunct therapies at a glance.

Exam trap

Animal-assisted therapy is credited to Boris Levinson and his dog Jingles (paper 1962; "pet therapy" 1964; book 1969). Animal-assisted activities are informal visits; animal-assisted therapy is goal-directed, delivered by a professional and documented. Hippotherapy is a physiotherapy using the horse's movement; equine-assisted psychotherapy is the psychological use of horses.

Yoga and meditation in Indian clinical settings

For many Indian clients, yoga is not an exotic import but something their grandparents did every morning, and that familiarity is part of its clinical value. Classical yoga is set out in the Yoga Sutras attributed to Patanjali, compiled perhaps two thousand years ago (the dating is uncertain). Its second sutra defines yoga as chitta vritti nirodha, the stilling of the fluctuations of the mind, and its eight limbs (ashtanga) lay out a path from ethics to absorption: yama (restraints, such as non-violence and truthfulness), niyama (observances, such as cleanliness and contentment), asana (posture), pranayama (regulation of the breath), pratyahara (withdrawal of the senses from their objects), dharana (concentration), dhyana (meditation) and samadhi (absorption). What is used in clinics is mostly the middle of this list: postures, breathing practices, relaxation in the corpse posture (shavasana) and simple meditation, taught in a structured module and then practised at home.

India's leading centre for testing yoga as a treatment is NIMHANS in Bengaluru, where the NIMHANS Integrated Centre for Yoga offers yoga therapy as an add-on to psychiatric treatment and a Department of Integrative Medicine studies it, often with researchers from yoga institutions such as Swami Vivekananda Yoga Anusandhana Samsthana (S-VYASA). You have already met the first landmark trial in Section 4.8.3: in 2000, N. Janakiramaiah, B. N. Gangadhar and colleagues found Sudarshan Kriya Yoga, a rhythmic breathing practice, about as effective as imipramine (though less effective than ECT) for melancholic depression, in a trial that could not be blinded. Research on schizophrenia followed.

Key study: yoga as an add-on treatment for schizophrenia (NIMHANS, 2007 and 2012)

G. Duraiswamy, Jagadisha Thirthalli, H. R. Nagendra and B. N. Gangadhar randomly assigned 61 moderately ill patients with schizophrenia, all continuing their antipsychotic medication, to four months of yoga therapy or of physical exercise, the exercise acting as a control for activity, attention and contact. A rater who did not know the groups assessed them before and after. Of the 41 who completed the trial, those in the yoga group had significantly less psychopathology and better social and occupational functioning and quality of life than those in the exercise group (2007). A larger trial by Shivarama Varambally and colleagues (2012) randomised 119 outpatients to yoga, exercise or a waiting list, with a month of supervised training followed by three months of home practice. Only the yoga group showed significant reductions in negative symptoms, and about 35 percent of the yoga group improved, against fewer than 10 percent in the other groups. The authors themselves noted the limits: the effects were modest, many patients dropped out, and home practice could not be checked. The conclusion is that yoga is a promising complement to medication, especially for negative symptoms, not a replacement for it.

For depression, a meta-analysis by Holger Cramer and colleagues (2013) of twelve randomised trials with 619 participants found moderate evidence that yoga reduces the severity of depression in the short term compared with usual care, and limited evidence of benefit compared with relaxation and aerobic exercise. But only three of the trials were at low risk of bias, too few trials followed patients up for any long-term effect to be pooled, and none reported safety data. Read all this evidence carefully, because enthusiasm often runs ahead of it. What the trials show is that yoga, added to standard treatment, produces short-term improvements in symptoms and functioning in several disorders. What they do not show is that yoga can replace medication or psychotherapy, that its effects last, or that they are due to anything specific to yoga rather than to activity, relaxation, expectation and the group. The trials cannot be blinded, many are small, and many are run by researchers committed to yoga, an allegiance effect of the kind described in Section 8.6.

Meditation has followed a similar path. The mindfulness-based therapies of Section 5.9 grew from Buddhist meditation, much of it originating in India; Herbert Benson's relaxation response (Sections 4.8.3 and 9.4.1) was first studied in practitioners of Transcendental Meditation, a mantra technique popularised by Maharishi Mahesh Yogi; and Vipassana, an Indian Buddhist insight meditation taught in ten-day silent courses, has been offered in prisons (Section 10.9.8). Meditation is generally safe, but case reports describe anxiety, depersonalisation and, rarely, psychotic episodes during intensive retreats in vulnerable people, so a history of psychosis or recent trauma calls for caution and supervision. The older Indian attempts to build psychotherapy on yoga, such as N. S. Vahia's psychophysiologic therapy in Bombay, are described in Section 8.11.

Indian context: how Indian psychology differs from Western psychology

"Indian psychology" does not mean psychology as practised in India; it is an approach that draws its ideas and methods from Indian philosophical, spiritual and religious traditions (the Upanishads, Samkhya, yoga, Buddhism) and restates them in terms that can be studied and applied. In 2002 more than 150 psychologists meeting in Pondicherry issued a Manifesto for Indian Psychology, which criticised mainstream psychology in India as largely a Western import that ignored India's own psychological traditions, and The Handbook of Indian Psychology (2008), edited by K. Ramakrishna Rao, Anand C. Paranjpe and Ajit K. Dalal, set out the field. Compared with mainstream Western psychology, which grew from Wundt's laboratory and tends to be empirical, analytic, materialist and centred on the separate individual, Indian psychology is holistic (body, mind and spirit as one system), consciousness-centred and spiritual (its aim is self-realisation and the transformation of the person, not only the relief of symptoms), it accepts first-person, experiential and contemplative methods alongside experiment, and it sees the self as embedded in family and community relationships (Sections 3.11 and 8.11). Its proponents present it as complementary to Western psychology, not a replacement. So when asked how Indian psychology differs from Western psychology, the answer is that it emphasises holistic and spiritual approaches, including community dynamics. It does not rely solely on ancient texts without modern relevance (it seeks to test and apply them); Western psychology does not focus entirely on spirituality and community (that reverses the contrast); and the two do not emphasise individualism equally.

Exam trap

Patanjali's eight limbs, in order: yama, niyama, asana, pranayama, pratyahara, dharana, dhyana, samadhi. Pranayama = breath control; pratyahara = withdrawal of the senses; dharana = concentration; dhyana = meditation. The NIMHANS yoga trials show yoga as an add-on (with medication) that helps symptoms and functioning in the short term, especially negative symptoms in schizophrenia; they do not show that yoga replaces medication. Sudarshan Kriya Yoga for depression: Janakiramaiah, Gangadhar and colleagues, 2000.

Telepsychology and digital mental health

The newest adjunct is not an art but a technology. The term telepsychology covers the provision of psychological services by telecommunication: telephone, video, text and email. Its history is longer than the smartphone's. In 1959 the Nebraska Psychiatric Institute began to use two-way closed-circuit television for teaching, and by 1964 a television link connected it to Norfolk State Hospital, elsewhere in the state, for consultations and group therapy. For half a century telepsychology remained a niche, used for remote areas and the housebound. Then the COVID-19 pandemic of 2020 moved much of the world's psychotherapy online in a matter of weeks. In India, the Telemedicine Practice Guidelines of 25 March 2020, issued for registered medical practitioners, gave remote consultation a legal footing, and Telepsychiatry Operational Guidelines prepared by the Indian Psychiatric Society and the Telemedicine Society of India with NIMHANS followed in May 2020. Reviews of psychotherapy by videoconference have generally found outcomes and therapeutic alliance broadly comparable with face-to-face therapy for depression, anxiety and post-traumatic stress, though most clients and therapists still report that something of the room is lost. India's national tele-mental health service, Tele-MANAS (14416), launched in October 2022, extends this to the whole country by telephone (Sections 5.11.8 and 9.7; the national programmes are described in Chapter 1).

The phrase digital mental health covers the broader family of interventions delivered through digital technology, with or without a clinician. Section 5.11.8 described its best-tested member, internet-delivered CBT, and the finding that guided internet CBT can match face-to-face CBT while unguided programmes lose many of their users (Eysenbach's law of attrition). Beyond these structured programmes lie thousands of smartphone apps for mood tracking, psychoeducation, CBT exercises, mindfulness and peer support, most of which have never been tested. Phones can also collect data: brief questionnaires several times a day in the person's ordinary life (ecological momentary assessment), and passive data from the phone's own sensors, such as movement, sleep, typing and calls, which some researchers hope can detect relapse early, an idea called digital phenotyping. Virtual reality exposure, the immersive form of digital therapy, was described in Section 4.3.

The most dramatic development is the chatbot. The first was ELIZA, written at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology in the mid-1960s by the computer scientist Joseph Weizenbaum (1923 to 2008). Its best-known script, DOCTOR, imitated a Rogerian psychotherapist by turning the user's statements back into open questions ("I am unhappy." "Why do you say you are unhappy?"), with no understanding at all. Weizenbaum was disturbed by how readily people confided in it (his own secretary is said to have asked him to leave the room so that she could talk to ELIZA in private), and in Computer Power and Human Reason (1976) he argued that machines should never replace human judgement in matters of care. The tendency to attribute understanding and feeling to a program that has none is now called the ELIZA effect.

Key study: Woebot, a CBT chatbot (Fitzpatrick, Darcy and Vierhile, 2017)

Kathleen Kara Fitzpatrick and colleagues at Stanford tested whether a fully automated conversational agent could deliver CBT. Seventy students aged 18 to 28 with self-reported symptoms of depression and anxiety were randomly assigned either to two weeks of daily text conversations with Woebot, which delivered CBT-based self-help (mood checks, identifying thinking errors, short lessons), or to an information-only control: an e-book on depression in college students from the American National Institute of Mental Health. After two to three weeks, the Woebot group showed a significant fall in depressive symptoms on the PHQ-9 and the control group did not; anxiety fell in both groups. Users said they valued the bot's "empathy" and check-ins. Notice the limits: a tiny sample of students, two weeks, self-report measures, no blinding, a weak control condition, and a declared conflict of interest, since the second author had founded the company that built Woebot. The study shows that a chatbot can be acceptable and helpful in the short term, not that it can treat clinical depression.

Since the early 2020s, chatbots built on large language models, which generate fluent, apparently understanding replies to anything a user types, have been used by very large numbers of people as informal confidants and "therapists", mostly outside any health service. They are available at three in the morning, never tired and never judgemental, and some users find them genuinely helpful. They also raise the problems Weizenbaum foresaw, in sharper form: they can produce confident but wrong or harmful advice, they cannot reliably recognise or respond to a suicidal crisis, they may encourage dependence on a relationship with a machine, and they are owned by companies whose interests are not the user's. Figure 10.15 places these tools on a single dimension: how much human contact each involves.

Return to Leela. After discharge she returned to a village two hours from the nearest clinic. Her psychologist arranged monthly video sessions, and between them Leela used a free CBT-based app to record her mood and practise behavioural activation, which the psychologist reviewed at each session: a guided, blended arrangement in the middle of Figure 10.15. The psychologist also agreed with her, at the first video session, where she would be during sessions, who else was in the house, which phone number to call if the connection failed, and what to do, and whom to call, if she ever felt unsafe. That last conversation is the subject of the final subsection.

Exam trap

ELIZA (Joseph Weizenbaum, MIT, mid-1960s) imitated a Rogerian therapist; the ELIZA effect is attributing understanding to a program. Woebot (2017) reduced depressive symptoms in students over two weeks compared with an e-book, but the trial was small, short and run by the company's founder among others. Guided digital help beats unguided (Section 5.11.8). Tele-MANAS = 14416, launched October 2022.

The ethics of digital mental health

The general ethics of teletherapy (consent, confidentiality, competence and jurisdiction) are set out with the rest of professional ethics in Chapter 1. Digital mental health adds problems of its own, which Table 10.28 summarises. The first is privacy and data. A therapy session in a room leaves no record except the therapist's notes; a session through an app or a platform may leave recordings, transcripts, location data and usage logs on servers owned by a company, sometimes in another country, and some mental health apps have been found sharing users' data with advertisers. In India the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 sets rules for consent to, and the handling of, personal data, but a clinician must still know what a platform does with the data before recommending it. The second is confidentiality at the client's end: in a crowded home with one shared phone, a family member may be listening, so the clinician must agree where the client will be and how to signal if someone enters.

The third is emergencies. A therapist in a room can call for help; a therapist on a video call to a suicidal client in another town may not even know where the client is. Good practice is to record the client's location at every session, keep a local emergency contact and the nearest services on file, and agree a crisis plan at the start. Automated tools are weakest exactly here, which is why every responsible app and chatbot directs users in crisis to human help. The fourth is competence and jurisdiction. In the United States a psychologist is licensed by a state and may generally treat only clients located in states where he is licensed, a problem that interstate agreements such as PSYPACT were created to ease; in India a clinical psychologist's practice rests on registration with the Rehabilitation Council of India, and a client abroad may be subject to that country's law. Clinicians who work online also need the technical competence to choose secure platforms and to manage a session when the connection fails.

The fifth is evidence. Most mental health apps have never been tested, marketing claims run far ahead of data, and published trials are often run by the products' developers, as in the Woebot study. The sixth is equity: the digital divide (Section 5.11.8) means that the people with least access to clinics, older, poorer, rural and female users, are often also the least likely to have their own smartphone, data and privacy, so digital services can widen the very gaps they are meant to close. The seventh is automation and accountability: when a chatbot gives harmful advice, nobody is professionally responsible in the way a therapist is, and users may not even realise that no human being is involved. The principle that ties these together is the one with which this chapter began, with children who cannot refer themselves: the more vulnerable the client, the more the clinician must think about who is protecting them.

Table 10.28 Ethical issues in telepsychology and digital mental health.
IssueThe riskSafeguard
Privacy and dataRecordings and usage data stored or shared by companiesSecure platforms; know the data policy; informed consent about data
Confidentiality at the client's endFamily members overhear in a shared home or phoneAgree a private place and a signal if someone enters
EmergenciesThe clinician does not know where the client isRecord location; local emergency contact; crisis plan at the start
Competence and jurisdictionPractising where one is not licensed; technical failurePractise within one's registration; training in online work
EvidenceUntested apps marketed as treatmentRecommend only tools with trial evidence; watch for developers' conflicts of interest
EquityThe digital divide excludes the poor, rural, older and femaleTelephone options (Tele-MANAS); local language; offer in-person care
AutomationChatbots give wrong advice, miss crises, foster dependenceHuman oversight; clear disclosure that the user is talking to a machine
Exam trap

The core ethical issues of digital mental health are privacy and data security, confidentiality, informed consent (including the limits of the technology), emergency management, competence and jurisdiction (licensure), evidence and equity. An option such as "digital therapy needs no informed consent because the client chose the app" is wrong. Telepsychology does not remove the duty to protect a client at risk; it makes planning for it harder and more important.

  8. Margaret Naumburg saw spontaneous art as symbolic speech; Edith Kramer emphasised healing through the creative process and .

Answers: (1) Dusky, (2) cognitive, (3) Institute, (4) 22, (5) 15, (6) need, (7) works, (8) sublimation, (9) 1916, (10) Rogerian (person-centred).

The case of Dibs, revisited

Go back to the playroom where Virginia Axline let a five-year-old lead. You can now see how typical of child work almost everything in Dibs's story was. He did not refer himself: the adults around him decided that something was wrong and could not agree on what it was. His teachers thought him retarded on some days and unusually bright on others, his parents had decided that he was defective, and a psychiatrist had told them that the problem was theirs. That disagreement is the informant problem that Achenbach, McConaughy and Howell measured (Section 10.1): each adult saw a different slice of a child whose behaviour changed with the setting, and the school psychologist and the paediatrician could not test a child who would not cooperate with strangers. Tested a week after his sessions ended, he scored 168 on the Stanford-Binet, a reminder that no single test, informant or setting is ever enough. The method that reached him was non-directive play therapy (Section 10.2), Carl Rogers's client-centred approach applied to a child through Axline's eight principles of warmth, acceptance, permissiveness, reflection of feeling, respect for the child's own capacity, following his lead, not hurrying and keeping only the limits that anchor therapy to reality. When Dibs locked the doll family in the house and staged a fire, Axline named what he showed instead of interpreting it as Melanie Klein would have done, and he went on to stage his own rescue.

The chapter has also shown the limits of the story. Dibs is a case study, with one child, one therapist and no comparison group, and Bratton's meta-analysis and the trials of Sections 10.3 to 10.5 suggest that play therapy suits emotional and relationship difficulties, while specific problems such as anxiety, defiance, ADHD and autism respond better to structured treatments delivered through parents and schools. His parents, who drilled and tested him without ever quite getting close to him, were part of the problem as well as part of the solution, which is the insight behind filial therapy, parent training and family work. And the three things you were asked to notice at the start hold for every client in this chapter: the woman with dementia whose family brings her, the student whose career his father has chosen, the accused whom a court sends, the grieving mother who could paint her loss before she could speak of it. None of them is the standard adult who walks in alone and asks for help, and with each the clinician has to ask who made the referral, what language the client can use, and which family, school, court or community the problem lives in. As Section 10.10 ended by saying, the more vulnerable the client, the more the clinician must think about who is protecting them.

Chapter summary

10.1 Children as Clients. Children are referred by adults, mostly for externalising problems, while internalising problems are under-referred; abnormality is judged against age norms, and a child talks through play and lives inside systems he cannot change. Developmental psychopathology (Sroufe and Rutter, 1984) studies normal and abnormal development together: homotypic and heterotypic continuity, equifinality and multifinality, Rutter's cumulative risk from the Isle of Wight studies, Werner and Smith's resilience on Kauai and Sameroff's transactional model. Thomas and Chess's New York Longitudinal Study (1956) described easy, difficult and slow-to-warm-up children and goodness of fit. Informants agree poorly (about 0.60, 0.28 and 0.22 in Achenbach, McConaughy and Howell, 1987), so assessment is multi-informant because symptoms vary across contexts. Parents consent and children assent, and confidentiality yields to risk and to POCSO. India's first child guidance clinic opened in Bombay in 1937, and RBSK (2013) screens for the four Ds.

10.2 Play Therapy. Play is the child's natural medium: Piaget's symbolic play, Vygotsky's zone of proximal development, and play's powers of communication, release, mastery (Freud's fort-da game), safe distance and rehearsal. Hug-Hellmuth (1920) first used play in child analysis. Anna Freud (1927) began with a preparatory phase, worked with parents and doubted that children form a full transference neurosis, while Klein treated play as free association and interpreted deeply from the first session. Levy's release therapy (1938), Hambidge's structured play therapy and the relationship therapy of Taft, Allen and Moustakas led to Axline's non-directive play therapy (1947) and its eight principles. Landreth's child-centred play therapy added the ACT model of limit setting, the Guerneys' filial therapy (1964) trains parents, Lowenfeld's World Technique (1929) became Kalff's Jungian sandplay, and Jernberg's Theraplay is adult-led. Bratton's 2005 meta-analysis found an average effect of 0.80, largest when parents delivered the sessions.

10.3 Cognitive Behavioural, Parent and School Interventions. CBT for children is rebuilt, not merely simplified: it is more behavioural the younger the child, concrete and visual, uses coping modelling, and brings in parents as consultants, collaborators or co-clients, because family accommodation keeps anxiety going. Kendall's Coping Cat (16 sessions, the FEAR plan, graded exposure in the second half) treats childhood anxiety, and in CAMS (2008) CBT plus sertraline beat either alone. Trauma-focused CBT (Cohen, Mannarino and Deblinger) builds a trauma narrative with a non-offending caregiver. Dodge's hostile attribution bias underlies Kazdin's problem-solving skills training and Lochman's Coping Power. Barkley's Defiant Children and Sanders's five-level Triple P extend parent training, which removes negative reinforcement rather than using it; helicopter parenting has many causes. Schools offer three tiers of support (Horner and Sugai), social and emotional learning and the Olweus programme, and India adds RKSK, Manodarpan and the Happiness Curriculum.

10.4 Treating Common Childhood and Adolescent Problems. Nocturnal enuresis is treated first with the urine alarm and overlearning, with retention control, dry-bed (Azrin) and full-spectrum home (Houts) training as packages; retentive encopresis needs the bowel cleared and scheduled toileting with rewards. Safety signals and accommodation keep anxiety alive. Berg separated school refusal (anxious, at home with the parents' knowledge) from truancy, and Kearney and Silverman's four functions of school refusal behaviour, two negatively and two positively reinforced, guide a quick, graded return; selective mutism yields to stimulus fading. In the MTA study (1999) medication management beat behavioural treatment for core ADHD symptoms, the combination helped other outcomes, and the differences had gone by three years. Conduct problems call for parent training, then child skills training and, in adolescence, multisystemic therapy, avoiding deviancy training. Adolescent depression responds to CBT and IPT-A, and repeated self-harm to safety planning, DBT-A and MBT-A.

10.5 Autism, Intellectual Disability and Learning Disability. Kanner first described early infantile autism in 1943 and Asperger the verbally fluent form in 1944; Bettelheim's refrigerator mother gave way to Rimland's biological view. Behavioural work is the foundation: early intensive behavioural intervention and functional communication training, after problem behaviour has been analysed for attention, escape, tangible or sensory functions. Schopler's TEACCH (1971) uses structured teaching, Bondy and Frost's PECS (1985) runs in six phases from requesting to commenting, Gray's social stories describe more than they direct, and pivotal response treatment, the Early Start Denver Model and DIR/Floortime teach through play; PACT and PASS coach parents. Facilitated communication was discredited in 1993. Intellectual disability, from Itard and Seguin onwards, is met with normalisation, levels of support, adaptive skills training and the National Trust's schemes; specific learning disability with remediation (phonological awareness, Orton-Gillingham, Fernald's VAKT method, response to intervention) together with accommodation.

10.6 Child Abuse, Child Protection and Adolescents. Kempe's battered-child syndrome (1962) made abuse visible. Maltreatment is physical, sexual or emotional abuse or neglect, and neglect is a failure of care, not poverty. A disclosure calls for calm listening, no promise of secrecy, a few open questions, exact notes and a report; Summit's accommodation syndrome explains delayed and retracted disclosure but proves nothing. Ceci and Bruck's research, including the Sam Stone study, showed how stereotypes and repeated leading questions create false reports, so the NICHD protocol relies on open invitations. The POCSO Act 2012 covers everyone under 18, is gender-neutral, makes reporting mandatory under section 19 and punishes failure to report under section 21. TF-CBT with a non-offending parent is first-line. Adolescents need engagement, the HEADSS interview and confidentiality with stated exceptions; continued interdependence with parents is healthy; and affirmative therapy addresses Meyer's minority stress, while conversion therapy is unethical.

10.7 Working with Older Adults. Freud thought old people no longer educable, but they benefit from therapy about as much as younger adults. Gerontology (Metchnikoff, 1903), geriatrics (Nascher, 1909) and geropsychology differ, and Butler coined ageism (1969). Caregiver burden, objective and subjective, is measured with the Zarit Burden Interview; counselling the spouse delayed nursing-home placement (Mittelman, 2006), and Kitwood's person-centred care replaced malignant social psychology. Folsom's reality orientation restates time, place and person; Butler's life review (1963) gave rise to reminiscence and life review therapy; Feil's validation therapy accepts the person's reality; Spector's cognitive stimulation therapy (2003) has the best evidence. Behaviour that challenges is analysed for physical causes and unmet needs. Late-life depression is not normal ageing and responds to IPT, CBT and collaborative care (IMPACT, PROSPECT). Capacity is presumed and decision-specific, elder abuse is mostly psychological and committed by family, and the 2007 Act protects senior citizens.

10.8 Counselling. Guidance helps people understand themselves and their opportunities; it is educational, vocational or personal-social. Its services are orientation, appraisal, information, counselling, placement and follow-up, and the counselling service is the heart of the guidance programme; aptitude tests predict potential, records document students, and the counselling interview is the vehicle of counselling. Williamson's directive counselling runs from analysis to follow-up, Rogers's non-directive counselling lets the client lead, and Thorne's eclectic counselling chooses between them. Hackney and Cormier's five stages run from rapport to termination and follow-up; a paraphrase restates content in the counsellor's own words, a reflection names the feeling and a summary draws threads together. Counselling spans schools, colleges, marriage, rehabilitation, HIV testing (the five Cs), de-addiction, grief and the workplace (EAPs), which is not industrial-organisational psychology. Career theories include Parsons's matching, Holland's RIASEC hexagon, Super's life-span, life-space theory, Ginzberg's three periods and Krumboltz's planned happenstance.

10.9 Forensic Clinical Psychology. Forensic psychology applies psychology to legal questions. A clinician may be an evaluator, expert witness, consultant, treating clinician or researcher, but never both therapist and evaluator for one person. Fitness to stand trial concerns the present (Dusky, 1960; BNSS sections 367 to 369 in India). Insanity concerns the moment of the act: M'Naghten (1843) is cognitive, the irresistible impulse test volitional, Durham (1954) a product test and ALI (1962) a substantial-capacity test, and the Insanity Defense Reform Act (1984) followed Hinckley; India's section 22 of the BNS, formerly section 84 IPC, follows M'Naghten. Actuarial prediction equals or beats clinical prediction (Meehl), and malingering needs an external incentive. Custody follows the child's best interests. The Juvenile Justice Act 2015 allows a preliminary assessment of 16 to 18 year olds accused of heinous offences, and Martinson's "nothing works" gave way to Andrews and Bonta's risk-need-responsivity model.

10.10 Creative, Adjunct and Digital Therapies. Adjunct therapies add to the main treatment. Adrian Hill coined art therapy (1942); Naumburg saw art as symbolic speech within therapy and Kramer saw art as therapy through sublimation, and the MATISSE trial found no benefit in schizophrenia. Music therapy is active or receptive (Nordoff-Robbins; Bonny's Guided Imagery and Music), Marian Chace founded dance movement therapy, and drama therapy works through fiction and role at a safe distance, unlike Moreno's psychodrama. Crothers coined bibliotherapy (1916); guided self-help reading helps mild to moderate depression, and Pennebaker's expressive writing brings small benefits. Levinson's dog Jingles began animal-assisted therapy. Patanjali's eight limbs underlie yoga, which the NIMHANS trials show to be a promising add-on, especially for negative symptoms, and Indian psychology is holistic and spiritual. Telepsychology, internet CBT, apps and chatbots, from Weizenbaum's ELIZA to Woebot, raise questions of privacy, emergencies, jurisdiction, evidence and equity.
