From the past papers

  1. When diagnosing developmental disorders, multi-information assessment is recommended because (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) parents tend to over report problems (b) teachers provide no relevant information (c) symptoms may vary across contexts (d) only clinical observation is not sufficient

    Answer: (c) symptoms may vary across contexts. A child's behaviour depends on the setting and on who is with him (situational specificity), which is why Achenbach, McConaughy and Howell (1987) found agreement of only about 0.28 between informants who see the child in different settings, and concluded that each informant adds valid information. Parents under-report some problems as well as over-reporting others, teachers are often the best judges of attention, learning and peer behaviour, and "clinical observation is not sufficient" is a consequence of the real reason, since the clinic is just one more context.

  2. Helicopter Parenting is the result of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Parental anxiety (b) Parental unfulfilled wishes (c) Parents' personality traits (d) All of above

    Answer: (d) All of above. Helicopter parenting, named from the teenager in Haim Ginott's Between Parent and Teenager (1969) whose mother "hovers over me like a helicopter", is an over-involved, over-protective style with no single cause. Parental anxiety, wishes the parents live out through the child, traits such as perfectionism and a need for control, and a competitive education system all feed it, so no single option is complete.

  3. Which of the following is not a part of Parent Management Training? (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Attending positive behaviour (b) Rewarding positive behaviour (c) Ignoring undesirable behaviour (d) Negative reinforcement

    Answer: (d) Negative reinforcement. Attending to and rewarding positive behaviour, and planned ignoring of minor misbehaviour (extinction), are core skills of every parent training programme from Patterson's onwards. Negative reinforcement is the trap the training exists to remove: in the coercive cycle the parent withdraws a demand when the child screams, which reinforces the screaming, and the parent's giving in is itself reinforced when the screaming stops.

  4. A parent deliberately ignores a child's temper tantrums in an attempt to discourage them. The parent's strategy is in accordance with: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Negative reinforcement (b) Extinction (c) Operant escape (d) Operant avoidance

    Answer: (b) Extinction. The paper's key, (c) operant escape, is wrong. Ignoring withholds the attention that has been reinforcing the tantrums, which is extinction; parent training teaches parents to sit out the extinction burst that comes first. Escape describes behaviour that ends something unpleasant that is already present, which is what a parent who gives in to stop the noise is doing; avoidance prevents an aversive event from starting; and negative reinforcement strengthens a behaviour rather than discouraging it.

  5. Which parenting style is characterized by the combination of giving emotional support, setting clear boundaries, and maintaining accountability for children's behavior? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Authoritarian parenting (b) Authoritative parenting (c) Permissive parenting (d) Neglectful parenting

    Answer: (b) Authoritative parenting. Diana Baumrind's authoritative parent is warm and responsive and at the same time firm, consistent and willing to explain the rules, which is the parent that behavioural parent training sets out to create. Authoritarian parenting is strict and cold, permissive parenting warm with few rules, and neglectful parenting is the uninvolved style (the styles themselves are taught in the Developmental book).

  6. A stimulus that is constantly present, or paired with, the absence of a noxious stimulus may become a: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Safety signal (b) Avoidance cue (c) Relief stimulus (d) Readiness prompt

    Answer: (a) Safety signal. A stimulus consistently present with the absence of something feared comes to reduce fear, as a mother does for a child with separation anxiety, which is why safety signals are gradually faded in exposure therapy. An avoidance cue (warning signal) predicts that the aversive event is coming, relief is the pleasant drop in fear when an aversive event ends, and "readiness prompt" is not a learning-theory term at all.

  7. "Escape learning" differs from "avoidance learning" in that, in escape learning, the behavior: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Prevents the onset of an aversive stimulus (b) Terminates an aversive stimulus that is already present (c) Is maintained by positive reinforcement (d) Is under the control of a discriminative stimulus

    Answer: (b). In escape learning the behaviour ends an aversive situation that is already present; in avoidance learning, option (a), it prevents the aversive situation from beginning. Both are maintained by negative reinforcement, not positive, which is why the two school refusal functions of avoiding distressing school stimuli and escaping evaluative situations sit on the negative-reinforcement side of Kearney's model; option (d) does not distinguish the two at all.

  8. According to DSM-5, which of the following is required for a diagnosis of Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD)? (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) Delayed speech alone (b) Persistent deficits in social communication and interaction across multiple contexts (c) Intellectual disability only (d) Hyperactivity and impulsivity

    Answer: (b). DSM-5 requires persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction across multiple contexts together with restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour, interests or activities, present from the early developmental period. Language and intellectual impairment are recorded as specifiers, not requirements, and hyperactivity-impulsivity belongs to ADHD, which DSM-5 allows to be diagnosed alongside autism (the full criteria are in the Psychiatric Disorders book).

  9. Infantile Autism was first described by: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Leo Kanner (b) Cameron (c) Goldstein (d) Jasper

    Answer: (a) Leo Kanner. Kanner's 1943 paper "Autistic disturbances of affective contact" described eleven children at Johns Hopkins with extreme autistic aloneness and an anxiously obsessive desire for sameness, and from 1944 he called the syndrome early infantile autism. Norman Cameron is known for over-inclusive thinking in schizophrenia, Kurt Goldstein for the loss of the abstract attitude after brain injury, and "Jasper" most likely means Karl Jaspers, the founder of phenomenological psychopathology.

  10. Infantile autism was first described by : (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Hans Asperger (b) Leo Kanner (c) Eugen Bleuler (d) Kurt Schneider

    Answer: (b) Leo Kanner, in 1943. Hans Asperger described the verbally fluent form a year later (1944), in a German paper that Lorna Wing brought to English readers in 1981. Eugen Bleuler coined the word autism in 1911, but for the withdrawal into an inner world that he saw in schizophrenia, and Kurt Schneider is known for the first-rank symptoms of schizophrenia.

  11. The "Lovaas programme" utilises behaviour modification techniques for the treatment of : (RML 2022)

    (a) Autism (b) Aphasia (c) Acute depression (d) Alcoholism

    Answer: (a) Autism. Ole Ivar Lovaas's programme at UCLA gave young children with autism about 40 hours a week of one-to-one discrete trial teaching, and his 1987 report claimed normal functioning in 9 of 19 children against 1 of 40 controls. Later work cut that claim down, but early intensive behavioural intervention helps many children; Lovaas treated autism, he did not describe it, and his programme has nothing to do with aphasia, depression or alcoholism.

  12. A child with autism is taught to request a "break" using a picture card instead of engaging in a tantrum. This intervention is a key component of: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Cognitive-Behavioral Therapy (CBT) (b) Applied Behavior Analysis (ABA) (c) Play Therapy (d) Psychoanalytic Psychotherapy

    Answer: (b) Applied Behavior Analysis. The technique is functional communication training (Carr and Durand, 1985), a form of differential reinforcement of alternative behaviour: the child learns a communicative response that is functionally equivalent to the tantrum (it gets him out of the task) and more efficient. CBT needs verbal reasoning that many such children lack, and neither play therapy nor psychoanalytic psychotherapy teaches a replacement behaviour.

  13. Over-correction is a behavioural technique to manage: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Bed wetting in children (b) Repetitive aggressive behaviour in autistic children (c) Temper tantrum in children (d) Alienation in adolescent

    Answer: (b). Overcorrection (Section 4.6) is a punishment-based procedure keyed for repetitive aggressive or disruptive behaviour in autism and intellectual disability, and it is now reserved for dangerous behaviour that has not responded to positive methods. Dry-bed training for bedwetting contains positive practice, a form of overcorrection, which makes (a) tempting, but bedwetting is treated with the urine alarm; tantrums are handled with planned ignoring and time-out; and alienation is not a behaviour overcorrection targets.

  14. Name of the Indian tool to assess the severity and disability of children with autism : (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) CGAS (b) Childhood Autism Rating Scale (c) CBCL (d) ISAA

    Answer: (d) ISAA. The paper's key, (b) the Childhood Autism Rating Scale, is wrong. The Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism, developed at the National Institute for the Mentally Handicapped in Secunderabad (now NIEPID), is the Indian instrument used to certify the severity and disability of autism; CARS is the American rating scale of Schopler's group. Section 9.9 explains how to handle this key in an IHBAS paper. CGAS is a single 1 to 100 rating of a child's overall functioning and the CBCL is Achenbach's parent-report problem checklist; neither is an autism tool.

  15. Which of the following disabilities is explicitly covered under the scope of The National Trust Act (1999) in India for the welfare of persons with the disability? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Autism Spectrum Disorder (b) Specific Learning Disability (c) Intellectual Disability (Mental Retardation) (d) Both (a) and (c)

    Answer: (d) Both (a) and (c). The Act's full name gives the answer: the National Trust for the Welfare of Persons with Autism, Cerebral Palsy, Mental Retardation and Multiple Disabilities Act. Specific learning disability and mental illness are among the 21 specified disabilities of the RPwD Act 2016 but are not covered by the National Trust.

  16. Which of the following is NOT listed as a specified disability under the RPwD Act, 2016? (Recall set)

    (a) Specific learning disabilities (b) Intellectual disability (c) Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) (d) Autism spectrum disorder

    Answer: (c) ADHD. Intellectual disability, specific learning disabilities and autism spectrum disorder are all among the Act's 21 specified disabilities; ADHD is not, however common it is in child clinics. Do not let the neurodevelopmental grouping of DSM-5, which includes all four, mislead you about the Act's list.

  17. Psychologists and psychiatrists have often viewed adolescence as a time when the healthy trajectory of development involves detachment from the parents. Today, however, there is a greater awareness that: (RML 2022)

    (a) it can be healthy for interdependence to continue (b) detachment typically occurs earlier, in middle childhood (c) the peer group is more important than parents (d) all parents have the same goal for their adolescent child

    Answer: (a). The view of G. Stanley Hall's "storm and stress", and of Anna Freud and Peter Blos, that healthy adolescents must detach from their parents has given way to the finding that autonomy grows best within a continuing, warm relationship. Detachment has not simply moved into middle childhood; peers gain influence in everyday matters but add to the parents' influence rather than replacing it; and families differ in what they hope for from their adolescents.

  18. Which of the following technique/process involved in guidance is called "the heart of the guidance programme"? (RML 2022)

    (a) Case Study (b) Interview (c) Cumulative record (d) Aptitude test

    Answer: (b) Interview. The paper's key, (d) aptitude test, is wrong. The phrase belongs to counselling: the counselling service is the heart of the guidance programme, because it is where the student brings knowledge of herself and of the world together and makes the choice. Counselling is not among these options, and the interview, as the counselling interview, is the vehicle through which counselling is done, so it is the most defensible answer. An aptitude test is only one tool of the appraisal service, the cumulative record is that service's main record, and the case study is the most comprehensive technique; if "counselling" is offered, choose it.

  19. When a counsellor repeats what the client says, but uses words that are different than what the client used, the counsellor is using what skill? (RML 2021)

    (a) Paraphrasing (b) Questioning (c) Empathy (d) Nonverbal communication

    Answer: (a) Paraphrasing. A paraphrase restates the content of what the client said in the counsellor's own, different words, usually more briefly, without adding an interpretation, a question or a feeling; repeating the client's exact words would be parroting. Questioning asks for information, empathy is the broader capacity whose main vehicle is reflection of feeling, and nonverbal communication is posture, eye contact and tone (SOLER). Paraphrase is content, reflection is feeling, summary is many statements drawn together.

  20. Which branch of psychology is concerned with optimising human behaviour in workplaces? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Counselling psychology (b) Industrial-organizational psychology (c) Forensic psychology (d) Developmental psychology

    Answer: (b) Industrial-organisational psychology. It applies psychology to the workplace, from job analysis, selection, training and ergonomics on its industrial side to motivation, leadership and teams on its organisational side, and Hugo Münsterberg is usually taken as its founder. Counselling psychology helps individuals with problems of living, at work through employee assistance programmes, but does not study the organisation; forensic psychology applies psychology to the law; developmental psychology studies change across the life span.

  21. The qualities of successful rehabilitation programs to treat offenders include (RML 2020)

    (a) Cognitive interviews (b) Offender profiling (c) Being community-based (d) Focus on relationship with parents

    Answer: (c) Being community-based. Programmes that reduce reoffending are community-based where possible, cognitive-behavioural and skills-based, target criminogenic needs and concentrate on higher-risk offenders, as Andrews and Bonta's risk-need-responsivity model requires. The cognitive interview (Fisher and Geiselman) and offender profiling are investigative tools for solving crimes, not treatments, and family problems are only one of the central eight risk factors, so a focus on the relationship with parents is not a defining quality.

  22. How does Indian psychology differ from Western psychology? (RML 2025)

    (a) It solely relies on ancient texts without modern relevance (b) Western psychology focuses entirely on spirituality and community (c) Indian psychology emphasizes holistic and spiritual approaches including community dynamics (d) Both emphasize individualism equally

    Answer: (c). Indian psychology draws on Indian philosophical and spiritual traditions (the Upanishads, Samkhya, yoga, Buddhism) and is holistic, consciousness-centred and spiritual, with a self embedded in family and community, as the Pondicherry Manifesto of 2002 argued. It seeks to test and apply its ancient sources rather than rely on them alone, option (b) reverses the contrast, and mainstream Western psychology is the more individual-centred of the two.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. Leo Kanner first described early infantile autism in 1943 ("Autistic disturbances of affective contact": eleven children at Johns Hopkins with extreme autistic aloneness and an obsessive desire for sameness). Not Hans Asperger (1944, the verbally fluent form), Eugen Bleuler (coined "autism" in 1911 for a symptom of schizophrenia), Kurt Schneider, Norman Cameron, Kurt Goldstein or Karl Jaspers. asked 2 times
  2. Multi-informant assessment is recommended because symptoms vary across contexts. Agreement is about 0.60 between similar informants, 0.28 between different informants and 0.22 between the child and adults (Achenbach, McConaughy and Howell, 1987). asked once
  3. Parent management training teaches attending to and rewarding positive behaviour, clear commands, planned ignoring and brief time-out. Negative reinforcement is not part of it: it is the trap of Patterson's coercive cycle that the training removes. asked once
  4. A parent who deliberately ignores tantrums to discourage them is using extinction. Amity 2023 keyed "operant escape", which is wrong: escape is behaviour that ends an aversive situation already present. asked once
  5. Helicopter parenting (Ginott's teenager whose mother "hovers over me like a helicopter", 1969) has many causes: parental anxiety, unfulfilled wishes and personality traits, so the answer is "all of the above". asked once
  6. A stimulus present with, or paired with, the absence of a noxious stimulus becomes a safety signal. An avoidance cue (warning signal) predicts the aversive event; relief is the drop in fear when it ends. asked once
  7. The Lovaas programme (Ole Ivar Lovaas, UCLA; 1987 report) is intensive behaviour modification for autism: about 40 hours a week of one-to-one discrete trial teaching. asked once
  8. Teaching a child with autism to ask for a "break" with a picture card instead of having a tantrum is functional communication training (Carr and Durand, 1985), a key component of applied behaviour analysis, not CBT, play therapy or psychoanalysis. asked once
  9. Overcorrection is keyed for repetitive aggressive behaviour in autistic children, not bedwetting, tantrums or adolescent alienation. asked once
  10. The Indian tool for the severity and disability of autism is ISAA, the Indian Scale for Assessment of Autism (developed at NIMH Secunderabad, now NIEPID). CARS is the American Childhood Autism Rating Scale of Schopler's group; IHBAS 2024 keyed CARS. asked once
  11. The National Trust Act 1999 covers autism, cerebral palsy, intellectual disability (mental retardation) and multiple disabilities; not specific learning disability or mental illness. asked once
  12. ADHD is not one of the 21 specified disabilities of the RPwD Act 2016; intellectual disability, specific learning disabilities and autism spectrum disorder are. asked once
  13. Adolescence: the old view that healthy development requires detachment from parents (Hall's "storm and stress", Anna Freud, Blos) has given way to the finding that continued interdependence with parents is healthy. asked once
  14. The counselling service is the heart (hub) of the guidance programme. Offered only case study, interview, cumulative record and aptitude test, choose the interview (the counselling interview); RML 2022 keyed aptitude test, which is wrong. asked once
  15. Paraphrasing restates the client's content in the counsellor's own, different words. Reflection of feeling names the emotion; a summary draws many statements together. asked once
  16. Industrial-organisational psychology optimises human behaviour in workplaces (Hugo Münsterberg is usually taken as its founder); counselling psychology helps individuals, forensic psychology serves the law. asked once
  17. Successful offender rehabilitation programmes are community-based, cognitive-behavioural and skills-based, target criminogenic needs and concentrate on higher-risk offenders (Andrews and Bonta's risk-need-responsivity model). Cognitive interviews and offender profiling are investigative tools. asked once
  18. Indian psychology differs from Western psychology in emphasising holistic and spiritual approaches, including community dynamics (Manifesto for Indian Psychology, Pondicherry, 2002). asked once
  19. Goodness of fit (Alexander Thomas and Stella Chess) is the match between a child's temperament and the demands of the environment. The New York Longitudinal Study (from 1956): easy about 40 percent, difficult about 10 percent, slow-to-warm-up about 15 percent. asked once
  20. Anaclitic depression (1946), the withdrawal and decline of infants separated from their caregivers, was described by René Spitz, who also described hospitalism (1945); not Piaget, Skinner or Eysenck. asked once
  21. Vygotsky's zone of proximal development is the range of tasks too complex to master alone but achievable with guidance from a more skilful partner; "scaffolding" is Wood, Bruner and Ross's term (1976). asked once
  22. The main goal of trauma-focused CBT (Cohen, Mannarino and Deblinger) is the trauma narrative and the desensitisation of traumatic memories; coping skills are its first phase, and the caregiver who takes part must be non-offending. asked once
  23. Affirmative therapy is designed to support LGBTQ+ clients and addresses minority stress (Ilan Meyer, 2003); conversion therapy is unethical, and in India professional misconduct (National Medical Commission, 2022). asked once
